Executive Summary
Advanced Reactor Designs Security Analysis, Risk, and Recommendations
Risks, Consequences, and Possible by-Design Mitigation Approaches Associated with Select
Advanced Reactors
Next-generation advanced reactors (ARs) incorporate enhanced safety systems, have smaller
source terms, and feature compact modular designs , which should lessen their collective risk
profiles. However, to fully evaluate risk, security needs to be a part of the equation. Without taking
security into consideration, safety systems and components in the new ARs may be vulnerable to
sabotage. These base attributes, coupled with enhanced security features specific to AR design
through sound engineering and security-by-design (SeBD), should provide developers and operators
with lower inherent security risk profiles. Building security early into the AR design may remove or
passively secure potential critical targets from an adversary’s reach , thereby increasing overall
safety and security. An integrated approach and diverse design team that includes engineering,
operations, and security experts are fundamental to building security into the design without
sacrificing fundamental operational efficiencies and principles.
The objective of this project was to evaluate the security and safety interfaces for five classes of
reactors, identify potential security vulnerabilities of structures, systems, and components (SSC),
and underscore the need to consider security alongside safety in the design o f these concepts. The
five reactor classes evaluated in this project and presented in this report are molten-salt reactors
(MSR), high temperature gas reactors (HTGR), sodium-fast reactors (SFR), advanced light-water
reactors (ALWR), and microreactors. These designs were selected because they reflect the
concepts that are closest to market deployment and have received significant resource
investments from the public and private sector. This project assesses the inherent security risks
posed by common classes of ARs, provides a methodology and framework to assess security along
with safety, and offers an analysis of potential mitigation strategies that could be incorporated. For
each AR technology, the SSCs that relate to radionuclide source safety functions are discussed to
understand the SSC contribution to safety and relative importance in the protective strategy for the
design. The assumptions that went into evaluating each reactor concept originated from generic
publicly available nonproprietary information and should not directly be used to qualify an absolute
risk profile nor to rank specific AR designs. Instead, the purpose of the analysis is to understand
and compare the generic inherent security risks of different AR technologies.
The methodology developed in this project is intended to be used to identify opportunities to build-
in resilience to the AR concepts, specifically security-by-design (SeBD) and safety-and-security-by-
design (SSeBD) (as defined below) opportunities . This is based on a safety system’s relative
importance of protection and their vulnerability to an adversary. By utilizing the proposed
methodology in a qualitative way, using appropriate safety and security analysis methods, AR
developers, end users, and regulators can incorporate risk -informed SeBD and SSeBD concepts
into their reactor designs and oversight programs early in the design process. As a design matures ,
AR developers can apply more specific analysis methods to provide higher fidelity. A summary of
the methodology can be found in Appendix B: Security Risk Assessment Summary Use and
Application Guide.

xvi

As an established concept, SeBD can be defined as an approach to integrating protection-related
elements into the design of nuclear facilities while considering security, safeguards, safety, and
operations.
This report defines the concept for integrating safety and security for the design of nuclear
facilities, referred to as SSeBD. SSeBD is the incorporation of security goals into a new nuclear
power plant or facility design, resulting in the optimization of safety systems and operational
programs to increase the resistance of the facility to radiological sabotage and reduce potential
dose consequences to the public. SSeBD seeks to identify and incorporate safety and security
design and operational changes for safety systems, increase the facility’s resistance and resiliency
to an attack, reduce safety system vulnerabilities, and optimize safety capabilities to prevent or
mitigate consequences from adversary actions . SSeBD is intended to be implemented early in the
design process.
This study demonstrated that new AR technologies can provide improved levels of safety with
inherent design features that could pose unique risks from a security perspective . However, they
could also result in a lower security risk profile as compared to large light -water reactors (LLWRs)
when security is considered alongside safety. The study provides insights on the importance of
technology-specific (not design-specific) systems of interest and provokes thought on why and how
the system should or could be designed to minimize or lessen risk. The study was limited to
assessing intrinsic reactor risk and did not focus on site-specific considerations or physical or
cybersecurity measures that are not a direct function of the design. The outputs of the study are a
qualitative ranking of security risk profiles of various SSCs by AR classes.
Some key insights from the process and cross-cutting results of the study are provided below :
 • The methodology utilized in this study could be extended to quantitatively evaluate any
 given nuclear technology using safety analysis methods such as probabilistic risk
 assessment or more discrete security analysis methods such as design evaluation process
 outline (DEPO), Risk Informed Management of Enterprise Security ( RIMES), systems-
 theoretic process analysis (STPA), or cyber-informed engineering. Applying these methods in
 combination would further inform a developer when making decisions related to tra de-offs
 between additional systems redundancy, system reinforcement, or protection as part of the
 design and site protective strategy.
 • Many technologies studied have non-core source terms that rise to levels of potential
 consequence near that of reactor core source terms and , in some cases, rely on active
 support systems rather than utilizing passive systems. In many designs, sequestration
 (hardening) of source terms in process, reducing source term dispersibility through physical
 change or strengthening of ex-core source term barriers, could provide significantly
 increased resistance to a direct adversary attack and dispersion of those so urce terms.
 • The simplicity of passive decay heat removal systems boast near walk-away safety of
 design. However, passive safety systems do not imply substantial resilience to adversary
 attack of those systems even if these systems rely primarily on first principle physics and
 simplistic system designs. Currently, there are very few published studies on sabotage
 methods for these systems and due to proprietary designs , little exists in the public domain
 on relative accessibility or vulnerability of these systems. Additional research needs to be

xvii

 conducted to evaluate credible and realistic threats to passive decay heat removal
 systems.
 • Some AR fuels such as tristructural isotropic (TRISO) are perceived as being nearly
 impervious, as related to dispersibility from an adversary attack due to its resilience to high
 temperatures. There is a great deal of data to support a finite analysis with LLWR fuels ;
 however, information for advanced fuels is relatively limited. Additional research needs to
 be conducted on sabotage consequences associated with AR fuels, to include those using
 high-assay low-enriched uranium (HALEU).
 • ARs exhibit, in general, smaller and more compact designs that reduce the footprint of the
 critical systems for a given technology. The colocation and small footprint provide an added
 risk and vulnerability to critical systems. This reenforces the importance of final protective
 barrier resilience, consideration of locating critical systems in hardened or subterranean
 locations, and putting in place a robust insider threat mitigation and access control
 program.
Most AR technologies studied, especially microreactors, plan to rely heavily on advanced
operations and digital monitoring systems (to include the possibility of remote operation) , which is
a significant departure from the current primarily analog deployment models . Though many
advanced nuclear plant designs incorporate advanced passive safety features, the digital
connectiveness of the designs may introduce multitudes of pathways for compromise of digital
systems. ARs will need to employ SSeBD methods to create robust safety responses that minimize
reliance on digital assets and use SeBD methods to protect plant infrastructure to negate or
minimize impacts from compromised digital systems.

xviii

1. INTRODUCTION
Next-generation advanced reactors (ARs) 1 may be able to use enhanced safety technolog ies, have
 0F

smaller source terms, and feature compact modular designs, which should lessen their collective
risk profiles. However, these attributes may not contribute to a facility more resistant to
radiological sabotage. These AR concepts possess unique technologies, materials, and concepts of
operations, some of which may present greater risk associated with a security eve nt. These base
attributes, coupled with security-by-design (SeBD) methods, should provide developers and
operators with inherently lower security risk profiles. Buildi ng security early into the AR design will
increase the overall safety and security of the facility.
The objective of this project was to evaluate five classes of AR designs for the inherent security of
the reactor technologies and an analysis of their mitigation strategies. For each AR technology,
structures, systems, and components (SSCs) are discussed that relate to radionuclide source
safety functions within different areas. By doing this, a holistic 10,000 -foot view of each AR
technology is created. Each analysis originat es from generic publicly available non proprietary
information and should not be used to qualify an absolute risk profile nor to rank AR technologies.
Instead, the purpose of the analysis is to understand and compare the generic inherent security
risks of different AR technologies.
To conduct the analysis, a methodology was developed. For this report, this methodology was
applied in a generic and qualitative manner. The methodology can be used to identify SeBD and
SSeBD (as defined below) opportunities based on a safety system’s relative importance of
protection and their vulnerability to an adversary. By utilizing the proposed methodology in a
qualitative way, using appropriate safety and security analysis methods, AR developers, end users,
and regulators can incorporate risk-informed SeBD and SSeBD concepts into their reactor designs
and oversight programs early in the AR design process. As a design matures, AR developers can
apply more specific analysis methods to provide higher fidelity. A summary of the methodology can
be found in Appendix B: Security Risk Assessment Summary Use and Application Guide.
As an established concept, SeBD can be defined as an approach to integrating physical protection
into the design of nuclear facilities while holistically including security, safeguards, safety, and
operations. (Evans 2021).
This report defines a concept for integrati ng safety and security into the design of nuclear facilities,
referred to as SSeBD. SSeBD is the incorporation of security goals into a new nuclear power plant
or facility design, resulting in the optimization of engineered safety and operational programs to
increase the resistance of the facility to radiological sabotage and reduce potential dose
consequences to the public. SSeBD seeks to identify and incorporate safety system design and
operational changes to increase the facility resistance to an attack, reduce safety system
vulnerabilities, and provide additional safety capabilities to prevent or mitigate consequences from

1 Throughout the course of this study, advanced reactor (AR) and advanced small modular reactors (A/SMR)

are used interchangeably, delineating advanced reactor designs that incorporate enhanced safety features
beyond the current large light-water reactors regardless of thermal or electric power rating.

2

adversary actions. SSeBD is intended to be implemented early in the design process, as seen in
Figure 1.1.

Figure 1.1. Security design and facility design.

The first steps in developing basic security risk profiles for ARs require analyzing and evaluating
new technologies systematically to identify design attributes that represent potential radiological
sabotage risks—despite the anticipated possible increased safety margins and lower source terms
associated with ARs. Developing risk profiles that are technology-specific, but not developer
specific, and providing potential design considerations to reduce that risk are the study’s primary
focus. The approach analyzes ranges of worst -case source terms inherent in fuel designs and
operational attributes and provides minimum design system analysis for security direct and indirect
radiological sabotage vulnerability. This work also demonstrates that, in many cases, the nexus
between a technology’s engineering design to contain fission product (FP) and fuel source terms
during operational abnormal conditions can be beneficial to also protect against direct and i ndirect
adversary attacks.
Designers and end users can utilize the insights gained in this study or as directly applied to their
technology through specific analysis, in conjunction with an integrated project team that brings
security, safety, engineering, and operations experts together to leverage security early into the
design process. The developer or end user must ensure that country -specific regulatory
considerations are included in the decision -making process for SeBD design changes to ensure
regulatory goals or requirements a re met. This study focuses on the inherent technology design
aspects of SeBD and SSeBD, and not the plant physical protection system (PPS). It should be
recognized by the reader that although this study focuses on the design aspects of AR
technologies, the integration of the security design and posture of the plant physical security
attributes are essential. The method in this report can provide an early determination for sabotage
targets to inform PPS design until greater fidelity on targets has been devel oped. The work
performed in this study, as qualitative and performed on potential worst -case source terms, should
not be interpreted to qualify an absolute risk profile for a given technology. However,
understanding these bounding, worst-case source terms, the technology critical systems affecting
those source terms, and their potential for adversary attack, qualitatively, provides for insight on
developing design attributes to mitigate the security risk involved.

2. REGULATORY CONSIDERATIONS AND BENEFITS OF SECURITY-BY-
 DESIGN INPUT
Although today’s AR technologies inherently have lower risk profiles through the use of smaller core
source terms and compact designs, the overall goals of safe, secure, and efficient economic power
production can be further enhanced through thoughtful design. The nexus of safe and secure
technologies while minimizing up-front design costs to developers can be incentivized through
longer-term cost savings to operators of those technologies. These up -front engineering methods
(e.g., hardening direct attack vulnerabilities through measures such as design strength or physical
system location or lower target source terms buildup through system segmentation, separation,
and relocation) can also be incentivized by regulation.
Incentivizing enhanced security methods in the design process to prevent costly retrofits and long -
term operational security cost reductions is evolving domestically at this time but shows promise
for AR developers and operators. Currently, the U.S. Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) is in the
process of a limited-scope physical security rulemaking for 10 CFR Part 73 as well as the new
proposed 10 CFR Part 53 rulemaking. The NRC has issued a draft rulemaking , NRC-2017-0277,
providing alternatives to the prescriptive physical security rules if a licensee can “perform a site -
specific analysis to evaluate the potential off site radiological consequences and demonstrate how
the performance requirements set forth in § 73.55(b)(3) are met when selected alternatives are
used.” This effort, when implemented, would support enhancing plant design features that would,
by design, provide for analysis and possible operating cost reductions when licensing under 10 CFR
Part 50 or 10 CFR Part 52. Another opportunity where SeBD and SSeBD could be incentivized to
provide a more robust security and nuclear safety posture while potentially providing long -term
operator cost savings has been proposed as part of the 10 CFR Part 53 regulatory framework. The
proposed 10 CFR Part 53 or “Part 53” licensing process is a new risk -informed and technology-
agnostic framework and was sponsored by the Nuclear Energy Innovation and Modernization Act of
2019 (Public Law No: 115-439). Part 53 is graded in many capacities and is intended to provide
performance-based nuclear safety and security approaches that are very similar to the limited -
scope rulemaking mentioned previously. Advanced LWR and non -LWR reactor technologies are
increasingly relying on passive and inherent safety mechanisms. The proposed framework from the
NRC provides a pathway for considering these capabilities in the physical security posture. Finally,
10 CFR Part 53 as proposed takes SSeBD a step further into the requirements for plant design as
drafted in 53.440 (f): “(f) Safety and security must be considered together in the design process
such that, where possible, security issues are effectively resolved through design and engineered
security features.”
Each aforementioned potential rulemaking action affords designers the ability to demonstrate,
through analysis, where safety design features can contribute to prevent ing or mitigating
radiological sabotage. These rulemakings and other similar regulatory requirements that could be
enacted internationally provide the groundwork that supports the philosophy of SeBD and SSeBD
during the design and licensing phase and guidelines for the design and implementation of modern
physical security strategies and technol ogies. Lastly, international users must consider
international regulatory guidance and requirements as part of this methodologies application to
ensure goals and objectives are met.

5

3. TECHNOLOGY SELECTION CONSIDERATIONS AND LIMITATIONS
AR developers are expeditiously designing and researching a broad spectrum of advanced , small
modular, and microreactor platforms (A/SMRs). These technologies vary considerably in coolant
types and neutronic spectrums (i.e., fast or thermal). In an effort to logically analyze current and
proposed future A/SMRs, the team primarily grouped the technologies based on coolant type : high-
temperature gas reactors (HTGRs), molten-salt reactors (MSRs), liquid-metal (i.e., sodium)-cooled
fast reactors (SFRs), and advanced light-water reactors (ALWRs).Although this effort grouped and
studied specific reactor types, as previously mentioned, the methodology could be used on other
applications such as differing neutron spectra and coolant types. The project team specifically
used only publicly available and nonproprietary information, and the research report remained, to
the extent practical, not controlled security information to ensure the widest possible distribution
of the methodology and high-level insights into the proposed technologies . No specific vendors
were consulted, and this research contains no proprietary or sensitive information.
The technologies analyzed and generically portrayed in these studies were derived from open -
source information such as the International Atomic Energy Agency’s (IAEA’s) Advanced Reactors
Information System (ARIS) (IAEA n.d.), NRC listings of ARs in process, and the Nuclear Energy
Agency Small Modular Reactor Dashboard (OECD 2023). As the landscape of AR technologies and
developers is everchanging, the technologies and the state of these technologies are not all
comprehensive. The technologies were selected based on publicly available information,
technology readiness level, and commercial viability. To ensure the analyzed technologies will likely
be deployed domestically or internationally, to the extent practical, U.S. Department of Energy
(DOE) or university test reactors were not included in the study.

4. SECURITY RISK ASSESSMENT APPROACH AND METHODOLOGY
The security risk assessment methodology in this report is centered on identifying fundamental
safety functions and qualifying the anticipated offsite dose consequences to qualify the general
security risks associated with postulated reactor designs and technologies. The approach to
developing a security risk qualification methodology is intended to evaluate a reactor or
radionuclide system design in a technology-neutral way that credits the facility design safety
features against radiological sabotage. The approach is focused on sour ces of radioactivity at a
commercial nuclear reactor site to include the reactor core, new fuel, used fuel, and waste -
processing streams. Security risk is evaluated by analyzing: the fulfillment of the IAEA-recognized
fundamental safety functions (IAEA 2007); control of heat removal, control of reactivity, and
retention of radionuclides; defense-in-depth (DID); possibility of safety systems compromise;
vulnerability of safety systems to different adversary sabotage methods; and the qualitative rating
of the anticipated offsite dose consequences from adversary action , and does not consider
probability or likelihood in the determination .
The methodology presented here is designed to be scalable to the design stage of a reactor. This
report demonstrates the security risk methodology at a high level for generic AR technologies
similar to how the methodology could be applied within the early stages of a reactor design.
Additional processes like probabilistic risk assessment (PRA) or systems-theoretic process analysis
(STPA) can be employed to increase the fidelity of the analysis by identifying specific pieces of
equipment and their combinations that lead to offsite release . By employing the methodology early
in the design phase, changes can be identified for incomplete designs and implemented as safety
system design and operational details are finalized. Further insights can be identified as system
designs and safety logic are determined and the total design matures.
This methodology can be adapted to meet different security system goals, regulatory targets, and
adversary characteristics. For example, the U.S. NRC proposed limited-scope physical security
rulemaking, and the Part 53 rulemaking proposes a regulatory framework for a security program to
prevent offsite dose consequences in excess of 25 rem (250 mSv) total effective dose equivalent
(TEDE) at the site boundary for the 2 hours following the radioactive material release. The
methodology can use different dose v alues or security event end states (i.e. , core damage). The
adversary characteristics in this report are consistent with those described in 10 CFR 73.1 but can
be adapted to match the characteristics of an adversary based on regulatory requirements or
specific threat analyses. The use of adversary characteristics informs the ability of an adversary to
access, disable, and compromise equipment in the security design risk and vulnerabilities in the
assessment.
The methodology demonstrated in this report is similar to the vital area identification methodology
found in IAEA Nuclear Security Series Technical Guidance 16 but adapted to align with the modern
top-down safety analysis methods found in IAEA TEC DOC 1570 and offsite dose consequence
prevention goals associated with new physical security licensing frameworks. The U.S. NRC
endorses a fundamental safety function and offsite dose consequence -based SSC classification
methodology in Regulatory Guide 1.233. The U.S. NRC Part 53 Framework A proposed rule
language also defines a regulatory framework around fundamental safety functions and offsite
dose prevention criteria for design ing and licensing commercial nuclear power plants (NPPs).

7

The results of the security risk methodology will provide insights into those radionuclide sources
and their associated safety systems that have the greatest vulnerabilities and associated
consequences to their sabotage. These results can be used to inform reactor engineered safety
feature designs and implement a site protective strategy. In addition, insights into generic safety
feature changes or additions to enhance the resilience and redundancy of safety system
performance will be discussed to increase SS eBD for ARs.

4.1. SECURITY RISK ASSESSMENT OVERVIEW
The security risk assessment is designed to identify those SSCs that support the fundamental
safety functions for all pertinent radionuclide sources at a nuclear power reactor. Similar to the
vital equipment and target set identification processes, those SSCs that support the safety of the
radionuclide source would be required to be accounted for in the protective strategy. This SSC
identification is a top-down, qualitative (i.e., using a low, medium, or high scale) approach to
identify systems by their functional contribution to safety, or their potential for compromise
(i.e., malevolent use), and can be conducted early in the design process, before detailed system
performance or failure combinations are determined (e.g., PRA). Importantly though, these
qualitative scales are defined for each security characteristic to ensure a repeatable and
transparent evaluation process. This would support a holistic view of the facility’s safety system
response to a security event.
The methodology can support further detailed analyses to include STPA methods and determine
adversary actions that can defeat or compromise safety systems. Ultimately, this method could
support vital area/equipment identification analyses to support meeting regulatory requirements
and the development of target sets.
The result of the security risk qualification for a n NPP is the identification of radionuclide sources,
the qualification from none to very high of the importance of protection for the safety systems
associated with each radionuclide source, the qualification from none to very high of the safety
systems vulnerability to attack, and qualification from none to very high of the potential
consequences from the release or dispersal of the radionuclide source. This methodology would
highlight the radionuclide sources with the greatest vulnerabilities and consequence potentials
that could most benefit from improvements in SS eBD, SeBD, or increased protective strategy
attention.
This security risk methodology is depicted in Figure 4.1 and described below in Section 4.2.

4.2. DESIGN SECURITY RISK AND VULNERABILITIES
Advanced nuclear power reactors will have the opportunity to implement SeBD and SSeBD
methodologies in the design of the engineered safety features, facility, and security features. The
methodology outlined here is intended to:
 • Address only the engineered safety features or normal operations found in a reactor design
 • Credit the expected plant layout, structures, and barriers associated with the safety
 features of the reactor design (e.g., credit is given to the biological shield as a barrier to
 access but not the vital area barriers or delay fences)
 • Be relatively agnostic to the applied design basis threat (DBT) and thus would be applicable
 to a variety of regulatory environments (i.e., the specific equipment or capabilities are not
 considered but could be applied by each user)
 • Be agnostic to the protective strategy with no credit given to security barriers, sensors,
 controls, personnel, or equipment.

The approach of not crediting potential security features provides a technology baseline that can
be further evaluated, as needed, for specific design s incorporating security enhancements. Note
that the use of this baseline approach is not intended to imply that security features are not
needed or are not desirable. Instead, this approach provides a method to identify where an AR
technology may benefit from additional security (and safety) features to mitigate inherent risks
associated with that technology.
Qualifying safety system importance and vulnerability to adversary sabotage is captured within a
security risk matrix included in Table 4.1 below. The following section describes the identified
steps in Figure 4-1, and how they correlate to the row and column headers in Table 4.1.

4.2.1. Design Information
The analysis begins with the collection of design information. This should include preliminary
reactor design information, safety SSCs and their performance criteria, reactor safety system
response, and other plant information.
Preliminary reactor design information should include building layouts, the planned location and
magnitudes of radionuclide sources (e.g., core, used fuel, fresh fuel, waste processing ) and safety
systems. Information on the planned safety barriers ( e.g., reactor cells, confinement barriers,
radionuclide retention barriers, building envelopes) will inform the consequence analysis and
accessibility for the attack vectors.
The safety SSCs that support the safe use or storage of radioactive materials will directly inform
the initial target identification for the security risk methodology. Identification of SSCs that meet
the fundamental safety functions of control of heat removal, control of reactivity, and retention of
radionuclides, as well as present or potential chemical hazards or impacts to safety systems will
inform SSeBD and SeBD efforts to prevent radiological sabotage. The performance criteria of these
systems will inform to what degree the SSCs can maintain the fundamental safety functions in
response to adversary action. Though a full vital equipment or target se t equipment identification
is not conducted as part of this methodology, preliminary information on the capabilities of the
safety SSCs will inform their importance of protection.
An important source of information in determining the importance of protection of a n SSC is the
reactor safety system logic, to include fault trees, failure modes and effects analyses, accident
analyses, hazard analyses, and probabilistic risk assessments. The intent is to understand the
behavior of safety systems during normal operations and accidents and identify those SSCs that
the plant relies on to greater degrees, to risk-inform the importance of protection qualification.
Additional information on radionuclide sources, waste -processing systems, fresh and spent fuel
storage and handling, and anticipated startup and operational actions relating to the safety SSCs
can inform this methodology and provide opportunities to identify SSeBD and SeBD opportunities .
The results of the methodology are intended to be updated as a design matures . Changes to
reactor safety, layout, access to SSCs, or improvements from SSeBD insights should result in an
update to the results of the methodology. Eventually, developers should integrate the insights and
identified safety systems from this methodology into vital equipment and/or target sets, as
appropriate for the applicable regulatory requirements.

4.2.2. Identify Radionuclide Sources
The identification and analysis of radionuclide sources defines the scope of the analysis. For
nuclear power reactors, this may include the power reactor core, used nuclear fuel, fresh fuel,
waste processing, and specific locations, structures, and system holdups that could be considered
a sabotage target. Systems or areas can be considered as a single source (e.g., a liquid -fueled MSR
core and fuel/coolant loop). This report does not include considerations for onsite fuel processing
(e.g., preparing fresh fuel either from fresh stock or spent nuclear fuel or reprocessing/recycling of
fuel after removal from the active region of the reactor) or long -term onsite fuel storage. Some MSR
and fast reactor designs include fuel processing in their conceptual design s but that is not being
considered in this study.

12

Discretion should be exercised to define boundaries between systems and clearly delineate the
function of safety systems to support the fundamental safety functions (e.g., off -gas system piping
may be included in the coolant system or the waste -processing system depending on the locations
and functions of valves, or necessary radioactive waste coolant systems). Radionuclide sources
may also be more easily defined by location ( e.g., piping within the reactor building vs. the
radioactive waste building) and accessibility (e.g., within a room vs. within subterranean vaults).

4.2.3. Screening Radioactive Sources
The consequence analysis described below may be beneficial in identifying radionuclide source
terms. If the subject radionuclide source (e.g., the off -gas piping between two check valves) does
not contain a quantity of radionuclides possible of exceeding the dose reference values used for
this analysis (not crediting any barriers or mitigation), then that source could be screened out from
the analysis (and properly documented as such).
Collective radionuclide sources whose postulated , unmitigated complete release due to adversary
action cannot exceed the dose reference values may be screened out from analysis, as they would
be either inherently safe/secure or beyond the capabilities of the planned adversary characteristics
as described in the applicable DBT. Radionuclide sources that are screened out from analysis
should be documented, and the reasons for screening should be captured. Care should be
exercised to not screen sources that ha ve interfaces with other sources, are co-located with other
sources, or are dependent on the same safety systems that could be grouped into a single
radionuclide source exceeding the dose reference values.
Changes to design or operational plans could change radionuclide source terms . Screened sources
should be revisited to ensure sources do not screen-in to the analysis.

4.2.4. Identify SSCs that Meet Fundamental Safety Functions
Once the radionuclide sources have been identified and their boundaries defined, the safety
systems that support the fundamental safety functions must be determined. These safety systems
would be similar to those SSCs determined in traditional security target identification, such as vital
equipment identification or target set element identification. However, these SSCs will have a
broader scope to represent all applicable engineered safety SSCs, not restrict the selection to SSCs
that support safe shutdown or core and spent fuel pool integrity. Each radionuclide source is
analyzed individually to determine those SSCs that require protection and their associated
vulnerability to adversary actions and captured on a security risk matrix.
The intent is to identify those SSCs that support the fulfillment of the fundamental safety functions
(FSFs): control of heat removal, control of reactivity, and retention of radionuclides. By maintaining
these functions, radionuclides can be held in a safe and stable state. The loss of any one safety
function can consequently impact the fulfillment of another ( e.g., excess reactivity can lead to heat
in the excess of the SSC capabilities to remove, excess heat can lead to loss of radionuclide
barriers). Thus, we can expect that the loss of any one function could lead to an off site release, the
extent of which would be determined in the consequence analysis. In addition, the identification of
those SSCs that can be affected by chemical impacts is important, and the loss of control of
chemical impacts may also affect other FSFs (e.g., sodium fires, oxidation of structural graphite,
plating of liquid fissile material).

13

Identifying safety systems that meet the FSFs should be conducted without strict adherence to the
safety-related status of the system. Systems should be reliable and maintained under a plant
maintenance or observation program. Systems that normally respond to transie nts, fulfill active, or
passive operational functions (e.g., heat removal , radionuclide barriers) and those that respond to
design-basis and beyond-design-basis events are the types of systems that should be identified.
Analysis should follow the safety defense line (DL) concept associated with DID, as described in
IAEA SRS-46 (IAEA 2005). This methodology has been adapted as shown in Table 4.2 below for this
security risk methodology.
In the identification of SSCs, the entire path for success should be considered. For example, the
primary heat transport system can transfer heat to the ultimate heat sink, through the use of the
primary coolant loop, steam generator, main steam lines , condenser, and other water sourc es like
auxiliary feedwater. The importance of protection and the vulnerability of this SSC to meet the
fundamental safety function should consider this entire primary path, as well as support systems
(e.g., plant cooling, power, instrumentation and control [I&C]). Similarly, the insertion of safety rods
may fail-safe through passive insertion, but control rods may require the control rod drive
mechanisms, plant control systems, control room or remote shutdown stations, as well as power
and I&C to actuate and meet the safety function .

4.2.4.1. Control of Heat Removal
The fundamental safety function of control of heat removal serves to address the excess heat
contained within an active, energy-producing nuclear power system, and the decay heat from the
decay of radionuclides. SSCs that meet this safety function would include those that are used to
shed excess heat from a full-power reactor system upon shutdown commands to enable eventual
long-term core cooling through decay heat removal systems, as well as those systems that remove
decay heat from the reactor, used fuel, and heat -producing radioactive waste systems, and include
the primary heat transport system (i.e., the path that converts fission energy into commercial
commodities like heat and power). Passive heat removal systems should consider the systems
necessary to maintain the conditions needed for the passive system to operate (e.g., if heat is
rejected to the reactor building atmosphere, are reactor building heating, ventilation, and air
conditioning [HVAC] systems necessary to maintain that functionality?).

4.2.4.2. Control of Reactivity
The fundamental safety function of control of reactivity seeks to control the nuclear chain reactions
associated with fissile and fissionable material. Losing control of reactivity can lead to excursions
that challenge heat removal and radionuclide retenti on systems. This fundamental safety function
would apply only to those sources that include special nuclear material or fissionable material in
the presence of neutron flux. Systems that exhibit inherent or passive behaviors (e.g., negative
temperature reactivity feedback) should consider the systems necessary to maintain the conditions
needed for the inherent or passive system to operate (e.g., MSRs may have favorable -geometry
drain tanks for the fuel salt meaning the shape of the drain tank and the drain plug are needed to
attain and maintain this reactivity control).

4.2.4.3. Retention of Radionuclides
The fundamental safety function of the retention of radionuclides is essential to the safe use of
radioactive materials. SSCs that are identified as fulfilling this function should provide a preventive

14

or mitigating function to the release of radioactive material. The performance of these SSCs is
defined in the consequence analysis. Identif ying the SSCs that perform this function helps to
determine barriers and systems that are potential adversary targets, to qualify the importance of
these systems based on the degree to which the safety basis of the radionuclide source relies on
the performance of the SSC, and the degree of vulnerability the SSC h as to adversary attack. The
consequence analysis will credit the performance of the barriers in the retention and mitigation of
radionuclides.

4.2.4.4. Chemical Interactions
In addition to the fulfillment of the FSFs, unique chemical hazards may be possible for some
A/SMR designs. The nature of the chemical hazards should be analyzed as interactions between
chemicals onsite, or that could be transported onsite by an adversary force and the safety systems
that support the radionuclide source. The goal is to characterize the unique hazards associated
with chemicals on the safety systems of the facility. Chemical hazards may include the ingress of
air or water to systems that are normally isolated, introduction of offsite chemicals that can create
safety challenges to systems, or manipulation of onsite chemistry to challenge the safety limits of
SSCs. Chemical interaction hazards may be to specific SSCs and safety functions as an a dversary
defeat or prevention of that SSC, a compromise of that SSC, or may be captured as a separate line
on the security risk matrix.

4.2.5. Determine SSCs Using Defense Line Concept
Defense in depth is a safety philosophy that applies graded requirements to address challenges to
the safety of the facility. IAEA has captured a DID approach in the formation of 5 defense lines that
categorize plant equipment and programs into DID capabilities in responding to transients. This
same method can be amended for the performance of plant equipment and programs in response
to security events.
The SSeBD DL concept shown in Table 4.2 has been adapted from the five line concept from IAEA
SRS-46 into a three line methodology to categorize facility SSC and program response to security
events. The SRS-46 DL1 and 2 were combined into SSeBD DL1, SRS-46 DL3 and DL4 were adapted
to SSeBD DL4 and DL5 respectively, and the SRS-46 DL5 of offsite emergency response is not
considered in this analysis nor typically credited in plant security response.

15

Table 4.2. IAEA SRS-46 vs. SSeBD DL concepts.

 SRS-46 DL 4: Control of severe plant SSeBD DL3: Mitigation of loss of fundamental

By categorizing SSC by FSF and then DL, a progression of plant behavior and system importance
may be understood. Individual plant response to safety or security initiating events may result in
differences to the representative designs presented in this rep ort. The DL1 through DL3 examples
in Figure 4.2 are representative of FSFs of a typical LWR.

16

Figure 4.2. SSeBD defense line concept.

DL1 should capture those SSCs that meet the FSFs during normal and transient operations. For a
LWR, DL1 could consist of heat removal via the primary coolant loop and steam generators,
reactivity control by the control rods, and the reactor coolant pressure boundary for the function of
radionuclide retention. Other SSCs would likely need to be included for a full analysis ( e.g.,
turbine/condensate water system, negative reactivity feedback, fuel cladding). Figure 4.2 presents
representative DL1 SSCs as SSC A and SSC B, two non-related SSCs that support FSFs for normal
and transient operations.
DL2 should consist of those SSCs that respond to accidents (both within the design basis and
beyond the design basis). For a LWR, DL2 may capture the emergency core cooling systems, as well
as long-term cooling via the residual heat removal system, assurance of criticality control by the
shutdown rods, and the reactor containment for the retention of radionuclides from an accident.
Figure 4.2 represents redundant DL2 safety trains as SSC C1 and C2, and that these should be
considered in terms of a reduction in the individual system importance of protection in the security
risk methodology.
DL3 should consist of those SSCs that provide mitigative actions in response to accidents. For a
LWR, this may include diverse and flexible coping strategies (FLEX) (NEI 2016) equipment to
provide core cooling and makeup, injectable moderators to control reactivity, and containment
spray to mitigate radioactive material release. Figure 4.2 presents representative DL3 SSCs as
SSC D and SSC E, two non-related SSCs that mitigate impacts from the loss of DL1 and DL2 SSCs.
The analysis of SSCs that meet the FSFs should focus primarily on the importance and vulnerability
of the DL1 and DL2 SSCs. DL3 SSCs should also be considered for the DID they provide or the

17

mitigation or delay of consequences. DL3 SSCs may not completely fulfil l an FSF but may
contribute meaningfully to or extend the time to fulfill an FSF. Each DL may have one or several
SSCs that meet the safety function. DL2 and DL3 may have no redundant SSCs to DL1 SSCs
depending on reactor design.
Considerations should be made for the capability of a system to meet the safety function ( e.g.,
does it complete the function 100%, or does it rely on additional systems to complete the
function?) and the timeframe associated with the required capability. A timeframe should be
defined to bound the plant safety response to a security event. The U.S. NRC has defined a
reasonable assurance of protection time (RAPT) of 8 hours for current U.S. LWR plants given their
robust security measures and expected offsite response from local, state, and federal agencies.
Similarly, a reasonable time estimate should be defined given planned deployments for the nuclear
power reactor, or a conservative value (e.g., 24 hours) used instead of a site -specific estimate.
Deployments of ARs may determine site-specific security bounding times (SBT) to establish t he
anticipated conservative value for the end of a security event, or the Adversary Interference
Preclusion Time (AIPT) 2 for all security events (U.S. NRC 2024). Plant target sets that result in an
 1F

offsite release beyond the SBT or AIPT that are reversible or preventable in that time between the
scenario end and release may be screened from inclusion in site -specific target sets. Implementing
SSeBD to increase timeframes or prevent capabilities to offsite releas e could result in significant
capital and operational cost reductions for the PPS.
Due to the interconnected nature of NPP components and systems, a safety SSC may support more
than one radionuclide source and appear on more than one security risk matrix. Each source
analysis and resulting security risk matrix should stand-alone, modeling an adversary force
attacking a single radionuclide source target. The contributions to safety for those SSCs that
appear in more than one analysis should be evaluated for each analysis individually, and their
fulfillment of safety functions should not be confused between radionuclide sources. For example,
a helium purification system in a n HTGR can be considered a radionuclide source target due to the
holdup of fission and activation products. The helium purification system can also provide
overpressure protection to the reactor coolant loop that supports the reac tor core radionuclide
source target. The helium purification system should be considered separately in this case, and an
analyzed attack on the helium purification radionuclide source would not impact the core
radionuclide source for clarity in analysis. However, when the loss or compromise of an SSC
negatively affects multiple radionuclide sources, that insight should be brought forward in the
analysis. Examples would be common headers without functional separation, common support
systems, and common retention barriers. These insights reveal opportunities for SSeBD
improvements to the security posture of the design.

4.2.6. Impact on Safety Function
SSCs that fulfill the FSFs are qualified on the degree of which their prevention, disruption, or
destruction of their functionality can impact the safety of the radionuclide source. The impact is
rated from low to high and are qualified as:

2 The SBT and AIPT strategies were presented by the NRC in a draft regulatory guide and have not yet been officially

 approved for use for A/SMRs.

18

 • High: primary safety function system or SSC, ultimately relied upon to fulfill a safety
 function, short timeframe to impact, irreversible, system or SSC interfaces with multiple
 important systems, and system or SSC fulfills multiple safety functions
 • Medium: medium safety function system or SSC (contributes to the fulfillment of a safety
 function for some time period or significance), contributes to safety function fulfillment as
 one of a few systems, medium timeframe to impact, reversible, system or S SC interfaces
 with few important systems, and system or SSC single safety function
 • Low: low safety significance system or SSC ( may fulfill the safety function, but not under
 adverse conditions), contributes to safety function fulfillment as one of many systems, long
 time to impact, reversible, system or SSC interfaces with no important systems, and system
 or SSC fulfills single safety function.

The importance of safety systems should consider the normal plant operations (e.g., at power, low
power, refueling, ascension, descension) and normal plant response to loss of off site power or
other common adversary-initiated events (attacks on other normal -operation SSCs). For example,
DL1 systems for control of heat removal that are relied on for normal operations would likely score
lower than heat removal DL2 systems that are relied on to respond to initiating events (as most
initiating events result in DL2 systems being relied upon or acting as DID to DL1 systems).
Reactivity control SSCs DL1 and DL2 systems may score high or low depending on the reactivity
control and success logic scheme, and radionuclide retention DL1 systems will likely score higher
or similar to DL2 radioactive retention systems due to the passive nature of these barriers,
implementation of functional containment, and the planned plant response to initiating safety
events.

4.2.7. Support Systems
The performance of primary safety systems in the fulfillment of the FSFs or the prevention of
impacts from chemical interactions can rely on plant support systems, to include power, I&C,
cooling, and HVAC. Often these support systems serve multiple safety systems and thus could be
an interfacing system target. These support systems are identified for each SSC that fulfills a
fundamental safety function as part of the security risk qualification. These support systems should
be collated for each radionuclide source, and each included as an SSC for analysis on the security
risk matrix in the lefthand column.
For the qualification of plant support systems importance of protection, the following criteria
should be used:
 • High: supports primary safety function SSC (DL2) with high importance of protection, short
 timeframe to impact, irreversible, and system or SSC supports fulfillment of multiple safety
 function SSCs of medium importance of protection .
 • Medium: supports safety function system for operation (DL1) that could meet the function
 for safety but is not the primary system to be relied upon, supports single safety function
 SSC of medium importance of protection, is a redundant support system, medium
 timeframe to impact, and supports multiple secondary or tertiary safety function SSCs.
 • Low: operational (DL1) SSC, supports tertiary safety function SSC (DL3), long time to
 impact, and reversible.

19

 • Generally, the importance of a support system should mirror the SSC it supports (as the
 support system would be another target for defeating that system). The compounding
 qualification of a safety system should be elevated beyond the importance of a single
 safety system if it supports many primary or high importance safety systems.

4.2.8. Reliance on Support Systems
The qualification for those SSCs that help in meeting the FSFs often rel ies on support systems to
supply power, control, cooling, or makeup coolant. The degree to which an SSC relies on plant
support systems is rated from none to high and qualified as:
 • High: safety function system or SSC relies on three or more support systems
 • Medium: safety function system or SSC relies on two support systems
 • Low: safety function system or SSC relies on one support system
 • None: safety function system or SSC is stand -alone, inherent, or passive.

4.2.9. Defense-in-Depth
The SSCs that help in meeting the FSFs often have DID that provides additional capabilities to
successfully fulfill the safety functions. DID can be redundant or diverse systems, delays, or
mitigation strategies. The degree to which an SSC provides DID is rated from low to high and
qualified as:
 • High: safety function system or SSC has no DID
 • Medium: safety function system or SSC has two or three redundant trains, or has another
 fully redundant diverse system
 • Low: safety function system or SSC has multiple redundant or diverse systems/trains
 (i.e., 4).

4.2.10. Compromise Risk
The SSCs that help in meeting FSFs can also have negative outcomes associated with the
compromise of the SSC. For this methodology, we define this concept of “compromise” as the use
of a facility SSC or material by an adversary against the intended safety function of that SSC or
material for the facility (i.e., malevolent use). Though traditionally captured within the concept of
sabotage, this is captured separately in this methodology. For this methodology, the compromise
risk qualifies the potential for the SSC to be used by an adversary against the safety of the facility
(this is different from the prevention of SSC operation as intended). The intent is to capture the
qualification of compromise of an SSC that would otherwise have a lower qualification associated
with the loss or prevention of that SSC’s intended safety function.
Examples of SSC compromises are opening valves to drain coolant systems (vs. preventing coolant
flow), igniting plant materials to initiate a fire, breaching of fluid barriers to initiate flooding to
destroy electrical SSCs, or reversing pumps to reverse fluid flow. Qualification of compromise risk
can be aided by following an STPA process to capture sabotage methods (that can aid in the
determination of various pathways to destroy, defeat, or compromise SSCs) and system failure and
compromise paths. The compromise risk of an SSC is rated from none to high and qualified as:
 • High: safety function system or SSC has severe negative outcomes
 • Medium: safety function system or SSC has medium negative outcomes

20

 • Low: safety function system or SSC has negligible negative outcomes
 • None: safety function system or SSC has no negative outcomes .
The outcomes from compromise should result in similar qualifications to the fulfil lment of the FSFs
(i.e., SSC importance and compromise qualifications are weighted the same in the determination of
total SSC importance of protection ).

4.2.11. SSC Vulnerability to Attack Vectors
The SSCs that help in meeting FSFs can be susceptible to different types of attacks. The type s of
attacks analyzed are defined here as direct SSC, indirect SSC, and cyber. These attack vectors are
primarily qualified based on accessibility to an adversary, as relative accessibility can be used as a
surrogate for the difficulty of applying SeBD methods. The most accessible areas of a reactor
facility (outside of the reactor island) would generally receive a qualification of high vulnerability,
while the least accessible (inside a reactor cell) would receive a qualification of low vulnerability.
Figure 4.3 illustrates the relative qualification s based on accessibility for attack vectors.

 Reactor Cell Reactor Building(s) Exterior of /to Reactor

 (low) (med) Building(s)

 (High)

Figure 4.3. Attack vector accessibility levels.

4.2.12. Direct SSC Attack Vulnerability
Direct SSC attacks refer to attacks that can happen directly to the SSC. Direct SSC attacks are
primarily evaluated on the accessibility of the SSC, the time required, and the equipment needed to
defeat the SSC. The direct SSC attack vulnerability of an SSC is rated from none to high and are
qualified as:

21

 • High: safety function system or SSC can be defeated quickly by hand -carried equipment or
 hand manipulation and is very accessible (exterior of nuclear island building [s])
 • Medium: safety function system or SSC can be defeated by hand -carried equipment with
 time required for the task and has medium accessibility (interior to nuclear island with
 personnel access, to include containment structures with doors, and would not include
 areas that need heavy equipment for access)
 • Low: safety function system or SSC is resistant to hand -carried equipment, takes extensive
 time to defeat, is not very accessible (contained within a confinement cell that requires
 time and heavy equipment to access)
 • None: safety function system or SSC is beyond adversary capability or time to defeat is
 beyond the timeframe established for a security event .

4.2.13. Indirect SSC Attack Vulnerability
Indirect SSC attacks refer to attacks that can occur remotely to the SSC. Indirect SSC attacks are
evaluated on how and where the number of alternative locations an SSC can be accessed as well
as any connections to other plant systems. The indirect SSC attack vulnerability of an SSC is rated
from none to high and qualified as:
 • High: safety function system or SSC can be defeated or compromised by other plant
 systems or from several plant locations that have high accessibility (exterior of nuclear
 island building[s])
 • Medium: safety function system or SSC can be manipulated or compromised from few plant
 locations with medium accessibility (interior to nuclear island with personnel access, to
 include containment structures with doors, and would not include areas that need heavy
 equipment for access)
 • Low: safety function system or SSC can be manipulated or compromised from the main
 control room or a single location with medium accessibility
 • None: safety function system or SSC cannot be manipulated or compromised remotely .

4.2.14. Cyberattack Vulnerability
Cyberattacks, for the purposes of this study, refer to cyber-physical attacks, attacks on air-gapped
critical digital assets (CDAs), and perpetrated by an internal or external adversary with hands -on
access to the CDA. The cyberattack vulnerability of an SSC is rated from none to high and qualified
as:
 • High: safety function system or SSC is directly controlled by a CDA that is accessible
 • Medium: safety function system or SSC relies on CDA information that is accessible
 • Low: safety function system or SSC has minimal connections or requirements on a CDA or
 the CDA is very inaccessible
 • None: safety function system or SSC has no CDA connections or requirements , or access is
 beyond accessibility
Cyberattack vulnerability within this methodology represent s the reliance of SSCs on CDAs within
the reactor design. Those SSCs that are controlled by CDAs, whose CDAs are within accessible
locations of the facility are of the highest risk to a cyber -physical-type attack. Those CDAs that are
actuated through automatic mechanical means or local CDAs that cannot be interrupted from

22

accessible portions of the facility would be less vulnerable to a cyber -physical attack. This analysis
is security agnostic, in that it does not credit security barriers, controls, or programs, and meant to
risk-inform a protective strategy. For cybersecurity, this methodology can risk -inform the
application of physical and cybersecurity controls for CDAs, reducing their vulnerabi lity, or design
changes to provide physical restrictions on some SSCs (e.g., control rod drive withdrawal rate
limiters) or disconnection from CDA control for actuation (e.g., always connected passive cooling
paths and burst disks).

4.2.15. Other Considerations
The DBT used in this analysis was consistent with that found in 10 CFR 73.1. This methodology can
be adapted to use the adversary characteristics chosen by the user. This has the greatest bearing
on the achievability of targets, both in term s of capabilities of an adversary and the equipment they
can carry. In addition, there should be an established end or bounding time for security events.
This may bound the analysis to limit the scope to a conservative value or be established by an
analysis based on offsite response. This bounding time can screen targets outside of the security
analysis, consistent with the NRC guidance on RAPT found in RG 5.76, as their destruction, defeat,
or compromise would lead to a preventable or mitigatable off site release within that timeframe.
The vehicle bomb attack associated with radiological sabotage was not considered in this
particular analysis but could be included, consistent with the requirement for vital area analysis, to
understand the relative SSC importance and susceptibility to a v ehicle bomb assault.

The logic found in Table 4.3 below summarizes the resulting qualitative determinations for SSC
importance and security vulnerability. The purpose of the logic is to provide a heat map that quickly
summarizes the risk and vulnerability of each radioactive source.

The primary factors that result in the SSC importance of protection are the impact of the defeat,
prevention, or destruction of that system on a primary safety function, and the risk associated with
the compromise of that system. The DID associated with that system and the reliance on safety
functions can contribute to the importance of protection . Table 4-3 demonstrates the relative logic
of the different qualification combinations. SSCs A, C, D, E, H, I show that the resulting SSC
importance of protection correlates to the qualification in the Impact on Safety Function or
Compromise Risk columns (H and I specifically show that whichever has the higher qualification
gives the result for the SSC Importance of Protection).
The qualification values associated with the Reliance on Support Systems and Defense-in-Depth
Risk columns can influence the resulting value from the Impact on Safety Function to the SSC
Importance of Protection. Examples in Table 4.3 are:
 • Reductions for Importance of Protection
 o SSC B shows that a Med value for the Impact on Safety Function can be
 modulated to a Low value if there are Low values for both Reliance on Support
 Systems and Defense-in-Depth Risk. The intent is to place less emphasis on
 SSCs with low reliance on supporting systems and multiple redundant trains.
 o SSC G shows that a High value for the Impact on Safety Function can be
 modulated to a Med value if there are None and Low values for Reliance on
 Support Systems and Defense-in-Depth Risk. The intent is to place less emphasis
 on SSCs that would otherwise be qualified as High, if they have no reliance on
 support systems, and multiple redundant trains.
 • Increases for Importance of Protection
 o SSC F shows that if both Reliance on Support Systems and Defense -in-Depth Risk
 values are High, then the Med value associated with Impact on Safety Function is
 elevated to a High for the SSC Importance of Protection.
 o SSC J, K, and L show that if Impact on Safety Function is qualified as high, and if
 Reliance on Support Systems, Defense-in-Depth Risk, or Compromise Risk values
 are also High, then the SSC Importance of Protection is Very High.
The SSC vulnerability evenly weights Direct SSC Attacks, Indirect SSC Attacks, and Cyber/Physical
Attacks (as show in Table 4.3, SSCs A-D, and H-L). Any two High vulnerabilities result in a Very High
SSC vulnerability rating (as show in Table 4.3, SSCs E, F, and G).

4.2.17. Inform Design
The purpose of the above methodology is to inform the design of a complex nuclear system early in
the design stage, to implement SeBD and SSeBD. The resulting tables and information gathered for
the methodology could be used to inform changes to the safety of the facility to reduce importance
of protection for individual SSCs, or apply SeBD changes to mitigate vulnerabilities that cannot be
addressed by SSeBD. Safety design changes should reflect the intent to make a facility more
robust to adversary actions, which may include flexible redundancies, functional or digital
interlocks, changes to design or implementation that reduce consequences, or changes to
operational aspects that reduce possible source terms. SeBD should be applied to those risks that
cannot be managed with SSeBD changes, or as a means of providing additional DID for security
events.

25

The method is intended to be iterative during the phases of design maturity , to ultimately inform
vital area identification and sabotage logic models , and the full PPS design and implementation for
a completed design. Designers are encouraged to develop initial PPS designs as a result of this
methodology. As SeBD and SSeBD insights are enacted in the design, improvements to the security
stature of the facility and changes to the PPS can be noted and improved upon.

4.2.18. Security Methodology Example
The application of the above methodology will be shown in the technology -specific sections below.
The following is an example demonstration of the security methodology that is not design or
technology-specific. For simplicity, this examines only the fundamental safety function of heat
removal.
A radioactive source at a plant has the activity to screen into the analysis and the need for
protection against radiological sabotage. The source contains FPs and has two systems to remove
the FP decay heat. The loss of the fundamental safety function of decay heat removal is anticipated
to impact at least one other fundamental safety function (retention of radionuclides). System A is a
single active cooling loop that dispels the heat through a he at exchanger to the plant service water
system. System A is not safety-related. It requires electrical power and I&C to power and modulate
the pump. System B is a passive air-cooling loop (similar to direct reactor auxiliary cooling system
[DRACS] or reactor vessel auxiliary cooling system [RVACS] ) that uses natural convective flow to
dispel the heat through chimneys to the atmosphere. System B has two redundant trains and is
anticipated to maintain the source heat such that damage occurs after 24 hours to the container
barriers. System B is a non-safety-related risk significant system and does not rely on any other
system, operator action, or plant control system manipulation ( i.e., the system is always on).
Table 4.4 shows the resulting security risk matrix.

System A results in a low impact on safety function because it is a DL1 system and is not what the
facility relies on in abnormal conditions. This system has a high reliance on safety systems because
it relies on three of them: power, I&C, and cooling. The DID risk is high as there are no similar
redundant systems or trains. There is not an anticipated risk of compromise degrading the safety of
the facility. The direct SSC attack risk is medium because this system is anticipated to be within
the plant structure, but the indirect SSC and cyberattack are rated as high because the pumps and
valves are susceptible to remote attack via plant locations or the plant control system.
System B has a high impact on safety function, as the plant will rely on the system in off -normal
conditions. This system does not rely on support systems. As there are two redundant trains, the
DID risk is medium. A compromise risk with the system is not anticipated. The direct SSC attack is
rated as high because the chimneys are located on the plant’s exterior. The indirect SSC attack and
cyberattack are rated as low because the flow paths are anticipated to have dampers for
maintenance but do not rely on the initiation of any action to fulfill the safety function.
The support systems that assist System A are also included, as they are required for System A to
fulfill the fundamental safety function. The impact on safety functions is determined to be low,
reflecting the impact on the system they support.
The results on the SSC importance and SSC vulnerability columns highlight the most importan t
systems for applying SSeBD and SeBD principles. For this source and the fulfillment of heat
removal, System B is the most important system to protect. SSeBD insights for this source include
increasing the number of safety trains or connecting another passive system, increas ing DID by
having a DL3 system, similar to FLEX equipment, that would be brought to bear beyond System B ,
using SeBD to harden System B against attack , and reducing the impact of I&C misuse for System
B through reductions in I&C usage, physical restrictions, or interlocks; .

4.3. Radiological Consequences Methodology
Evaluating the radiological consequence potential for each radioactive source within the plant is a
key component of the overall risk assessment methodology. The purpose of the assessment is to
provide high-level insight into the potential radiological release from sabotage events associated
with operating ARs of various design and technology types. Such information can also be utilized to
inform vendors, regulators, and applicants/licensees of the most important SSCs to be considered
in the site protective strategy. For the current effort, the goal of the radiological consequence
assessment method was to develop an approach with the following characteristics:
 • Reactor technology neutral
 • Radioactive source neutral (applicable to core and non -core sources)
 • Comprehensive of key attributes for radiological consequence
 • Straightforward to implement
 o Independent of specific sabotage scenarios but flexibility to align with security
 analysis discussed in Section 4.1.
 o Executed without the need for detailed computational calculations
 • Would not provide any usable technology-specific consequence assessment useful to an
 adversary.

28

Given the spectrum of AR designs, it is vital that the developed approach is reactor technology
neutral. This includes the ability to assess core and non -core sources, as the latter could
potentially be more attractive to hostile actors, depending on the source location and
characteristics. In addition, the method should include the key attributes of radiological
consequence. As discussed further below, this includes dose potential, dispersibility, and barriers
to release. Last, the method must be straightforward to implement and not depend on detailed
analysis of specific sabotage scenarios or detailed computational calculations regarding
radionuclide transport and retention. However, the method should have flexibility to align with the
types of scenarios examined in the security analysis, discussed in Section 4.1.
Based on the above goals and reviews of previous approaches (Sarnoski. et al. 2022;
DOE/NRC 2023), a method was developed that assesses each radioactive source within a plant
based on the following four characteristics:
 • Radioactivity
 • Dose impact
 • Dispersibility
 • Barriers to release.

The first metric is the quantity of radioactive material associated with the source, including the
inventory of specific isotopes. Second is the potential dose impact of the source radioactive
material, including external and internal (inhalation) dose pat hways. The amount of radioactive
material and its dose potential are intrinsically linked and combined into a single factor, discussed
below. The dispersibility of a source is a key characteristic, as it describes the ability of the source
to be transported in the environment and spread to the surrounding area and population. Last, the
barriers to release are a characteristic of both the source itself and the plant layout. For example,
radioactive sources may have intrinsic properties or characteristics that act as barriers to release
(e.g., tristructural isotropic [TRISO] fuel pebbles). However, there are often additional designed
barriers to release (e.g., casks, confinement structures ).
As mentioned, the radioactivity characteristics and dose potential factors were combined into a
single metric, resulting in a total of three attributes reviewed for each source. The assessment is
generally qualitative in nature, keeping with the objective of developing a straightforward
methodology to implement but is complemented with simple quantitative assessments. The result
is the ranking structure discussed in detail in the following three subsections. However, before
reviewing the specifics of each factor, it is important to highlight that each factor is assessed
independently. But, when taken together, they form a representation of the consequence potential
and attractiveness of the source to hostile actors, as demonstrated by the example radar plot in
Figure 4.4. In essence, the consequence potential or source protection importance increases as
the ranking of each factor increases (and the area encompassed by the shape grows). The number
of rankings associated with each of the three factors varies. For example, m aximum dose potential
has five ranks while dispersibility and barriers to release each have three. The description of each
rank for each factor is described in subsequent sections, but Figure 4.5 provides a key of how the
ranks for each factor are represented on radar plots. The rankings for each metric can also be
represented in matrix form as provided in Table 4.5. The definitions for the rankings for each

29

metric may be selected or adapted to meet the needs of the assessment being performed. This
allows the methodology to be flexible to cover a wide range of reactor and other nuclear facility
designs.

Figure 4.4. Example consequence assessment radar plot.

Figure 4.5. Radar plot ranking key.
Note that there is no screening performed for Barriers to Release or Dispersibility in the graphical
representation. Thus, we start the innermost triangle at Low for those and then skip the next
triangle. The Screened and Very High categories only apply to Max Dose Potential.
