4.3.1. Maximum Dose Potential Factor
The dose impact ranking examines the maximum dose potential of each source. This is an intrinsic
characteristic of the source and does not relate to the feasibility of such a release, which must be
assessed with consideration for the dispersibility and bar rier factors. The ranking structure
provided in Table 4.6 is utilized for analysis and contains five ranks based on possible dose
impact. As previously stated, the definitions of each ranking can be modified or adapted to meet
the needs of the evaluation. For example, the screening criteria may be different for different
applications, or the distinction between the “high” and “very high” rankings may be adjusted to be
able to differentiate between various radiological sources. The definitions provided in Table 4.6 are
what was selected for the current analysis.

Table 4.6. Maximum dose impact ranking structure.
  Ranking Maximum Potential Dose Impact Approximate TEDE Evaluation Level
 Very large doses (large radionuclide inventories),
 Possible wide-area early health
Very High such as those associated with full core inventories
 effects
 or multiple used fuel assemblies (or equivalent)
High Possible early health effects 200 rem < x < Very High Rank
Medium Possible late health effects 10 < x < 200 rem
Low Possible exceedance of EPA PAG 1 < x < 10 rem
Screen Below screening limit (EPA PAG) x < 1 rem

To select the appropriate rank for a source, a simplified quantitative dose assessment is
performed. First, an estimated radionuclide inventory for the source is established. For the
analyses reviewed here, this is the bounding radionuclide inventory for t he source, such as the fuel
at maximum burnup level or a storage system at its maximum design capacity and associated
source term or strength. The inventory estimate includes the specific isotopes and their
approximate quantity. Using this information, a s implified TEDE calculation is performed using the
dose conversion factors from FGR 11 and 12 (U.S. EPA 1988; 1993). To estimate the offsite dose
potential, a χ/Q 3 dispersion value of 3.5E-3 sec/m 3 is utilized, which is the DOE conservative
 2F

recommended value for people located 100 m from the release location (U.S. DOE 2014; 2016).
This conservative value was selected mainly as a uniform value for compari ng sources but also
aligns with the fact that ARs are likely to have smaller exclusion area boundaries. Such calculations
can be performed in a spreadsheet without the need for detailed source term analysis or offsite
dispersion assessment codes.
Using the results of the TEDE evaluation, the appropriate rank is selected. If the result of the
evaluation is less than 1 rem, which is the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) threshold for
protective actions for the public, from protective action guide (PAG) (U.S. EPA 2013), then the

3 χ/Q is an atmospheric dispersion parameter that depends on the radionuclide release height,

meteorological conditions, distance from the release location, and distance from the plume centerline, where
χ is the cloud concentration, and Q is the released activity.

31

source is screened from further analysis. If the TEDE is above the 1 rem threshold, then it is placed
in the proper rank using the approximate TEDE bins in Table 4.6, which generally aligns with the
thresholds for late and early health effects.

4.3.2. Dispersibility Factor
The dispersibility factor is important for gauging the ability of the source to be transported in the
environment and can vary dramatically between sources. The ranking criteria in Table 4.7 are used
for the assessment and evaluate the amount of energy needed to disperse the radioactive
material. For example, radioactive gases can often be dispersed without the need for energy input
and would likely rank as high. In contrast, radionuclides co ntained within a solid fuel structure or
chemically/mechanically bonded to a component may need significant energy addition for
dispersion. Sources at the medium rank may need energy to initiate a dispersion, such as igniting
the material, or some amount of continuous energy, such as heating a fluid to cause vaporization.
For sources that may include radionuclides of multiple forms, such as spent fuel that contains
fission gases along with radionuclides retained in the fuel matrix, assess ing the quantity of
material in each form along with their contribution to overall dose may be necessary to determine
the appropriate dispersibility rank. Sources cannot be screened from analysis based on
dispersibility, as adversaries may impart energy or facilitate dispersal within the capabilities of the
DBT.

Table 4.7. Dispersibility factor ranking structure.
 Ranking Dispersibility Characteristics
High Very limited or no energy needed for dispersal
Medium Energy needed to initiate dispersal or small amount of continuous energy
Low Significant energy needed for dispersal

4.3.3. Barrier Factor to Radionuclide Release
The final factor assesses the barriers to radionuclide release. These are physical barriers to the
transport of radioactive material, not barriers preventing hostile actors from sabotaging a source,
although the same barrier may perform both roles. The bar riers may be an inherent property of the
source or applied as part of the facility design. In general, additional credit was given in the
ranking framework outlined in Table 4.8 for inherent barriers. All radionuclide sources contain at
least one physical barrier to prevent release ; therefore, that is considered the minimum (the “high”
rank). As the number of barriers increases, the factor rank is reduced, indicating additional
difficulty to radionuclide release for hostile actors. Sources may not be screened from analysis
based on barriers to radionuclide release, as adversaries will defeat barriers to result in offsite
dose consequences.

32

Table 4.8. Barrier factor to radionuclide release ranking structure.
 Ranking Barrier Factor Characteristics
High No inherent barriers or single applied barrier
Medium Single inherent barrier or multiple applied barriers
Low Multiple inherent barriers or mix of inherent/applied barriers

It is important to note that the barrier assessment can be aligned with the security analysis
described in Section 4.1. if desired. For example, if a specific sabotage pathway results in the
defeat of a barrier, such as breaching a confinement compartment, the number of barriers
assessed in the consequence assessment can be adjusted accordingly. This provides users with
additional flexibility if they would like to reevaluate the consequence potential for such scenarios.

5. TECHNOLOGY-SPECIFIC ANALYSIS
In this section, we provide a high-level analysis of specific AR technology types. This analysis uses
the methodology described in Section 4. The reactor types under consideration are:
 • Molten salt
 • High-temperature gas
 • Sodium fast
 • Advanced LWR
 • Microreactors.

5.1. MOLTEN-SALT REACTORS
MSRs use liquid salts as their fuel medium, coolant, or both. MSRs generally use molten salts to
transfer heat away from the reactor core although liquid metals have also been proposed to
transfer the heat from liquid salt fuel. The electronegative portion of most MSR salts is comprised
of halide atoms though some MSRs employ hydroxide salts. The reactor’s heat can be used to
produce electricity or to support an industrial process. As shown in Figure 5.1, MSRs can be
subdivided into two classes: salt-cooled reactors, which employ a solid fuel and liquid salt coolant ,
and salt-fueled reactors, in which the fissile and fertile isotopes form part of the liquid salt mixture.
Salt-fueled MSR facilities are significantly different from previously licensed nuclear fuel and
reactor facilities. Salt-fueled reactors can be further subdivided into therm al spectrum and fast
spectrum designs (Holcomb et al. 2022). The types of radiological systems and where fissile and
fertile materials are present vary significantly by MSR concept. This section focuses on salt-fueled
MSRs because salt-cooled MSRs (i.e., those with solid fuel) are similar to other AR technologies
such as sodium-cooled reactors in terms of systemic operation and potential sabotage.

Figure 5.1. Categorization of common MSR designs.

The use of liquid salt fuel in MSRs provides substantial design and operational flexibility. MSR fuel
salts consist of mixtures of fissile and generally fertile materials, halides (or hydroxides), and
carrier salts. For example, the molten-salt breeder reactor (MSBR) fresh fuel salt composition
(71.7-16-12-0.3 mole% 7LiF-BeF2-ThF4-UF4 [IAEA 2022]) was planned to consist of uranium,
fluorine, thorium, lithium-7, and beryllium. The liquid fuel is typically combined into a single salt

35

(i.e., single fluid), but in some thermal spectrum designs the fuel can be split into separate salt
streams containing either fissile, fertile, or a mixture of both components (i.e., two fluid) (Riley et
al. 2018). Salt-fueled MSRs generally use molten fluoride, chloride , or a mixture as their carrier
salt, although some use hydroxide carrier salts. The primary purpose of the carrier salt is to lower
the melting point of the fuel salt mixture. Fast spectrum MSRs require a substantially higher
concentration of fissile materials than thermal spectrum MSRs to maintain criticality due to the
much lower fission cross sections at higher neutron energies. Optimized fuel salts will have a high
boiling point, high radiolytic stability, large volumetric heat capacity, acceptable thermal
conductivity, acceptable compatibility with structural materials, low viscosity, low par asitic neutron
capture, and strong retention of FPs. Different designs are currently under development to achieve
different performance objectives (McFarlane et al. 2019). For example, some designs are focused
on consuming the actinides in spent LWR fuel to reduce long -term radiotoxicity, while others seek
to implement a modernized version of the Molten -Salt Reactor Experiment (MSRE) technology to
minimize their development risks (Holcomb et al. 2022).
The fuel salt is highly radioactive after operation due to the presence of fission and activation
products. Therefore, all systems containing fuel salt will be in some type of containment with very
restricted access even when the reactor is shut down. Maintenance within containment is likely to
require remote handling (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018; U.S. Atomic Energy Commission
Division of Reactor Development and Technology 1972 ). Designs that employ substantial shielding
immediately outside of the fuel salt, whether by employing an unfueled coolant or a heavy metal
reflector, can substantially reduce the dose rate in the next containment layer.
Active U.S.-based salt-fueled MSR concepts are listed in Table 5.1 (IAEA 2022). The reader is
cautioned that the set of MSR developers is rapidly evolving. Many variations of MSR technologies
are anticipated to be developed in the coming years and to vary in deployment strategy. Note that
Martingale Inc. has indicated they do not anticipate pursuing U .S. licensing under the NRC’s
licensing process. Instead, they have pursued selling their ThorCon floating MSR concept abroad.
Also, some largely international vendors have a nominal U .S. presence with the potential to expand
in the future (e.g., Moltex Energy Inc.). Moltex Energy Inc.’s initial design is intended to first convert
the actinides from used LWR fuel to fuel salt and then consume them in a fast spectrum burner
MSR. Moltex’s fuel salt is contained within tubes that incorporate chambered fission gas venting
intended to delay the release of fission gases and which resemble fuel rods. The fuel salt tubes are
intended to be externally cooled with pumped coolant salt. Not included in the table is an
accelerator-driven subcritical reactor (ADSR) under development by Muons Inc. The concept is
known as the Mu*STAR. This concept uses an accelerator-driven neutron flux to initiate fission and
generate heat in a fuel-salt-filled reactor vessel. Without the neutron flux from the accelerator, the
ADSR shuts down. Other companies such as CORE POWER and CURIO solutions have shown
interest in MSRs but have not disclosed specific plans. Also, listed companies such as Flibe Energy
Inc. have multiple MSR concepts under development with different fuel cycles and objectives. In
the latter case, the most publicized technology i s listed.

36

Table 5.1. U.S. salt-fueled molten-salt technologies.
 Technology Vendor Spectrum MWe
 Molten Chloride Fast Reactor (MCFR) TerraPower Fast 1200
 Molten Chloride Salt Fast Reactor (MCSFR) Exodys Energy* Fast 1200
 The Do-able Molten-Salt Reactor (ThorCon) Martingale, Inc. Thermal 250
 Liquid Fluoride Thorium Reactor (LFTR) Flibe Energy Thermal 250
 Integral Molten-Salt Reactor (IMSR) Terrestrial Energy Thermal 195

An alternative fuel salt design from a Canadian vendor includes molten fuel salt inside tubular fuel
assemblies connected to inlet and outlet manifolds resembling a tube -and-shell heat exchanger
with the fuel salt on the tube side. Heat is removed by a she ll-side cooling liquid metal. This design
is not currently planned for the U.S. market. As with vendors, reactor fuel system configurations are
rapidly evolving. Reactor configurations different from those currently being commercially pursued
are likely to emerge over time. For example, loop-type configurations, like those employed at the
MSRE, are being displaced by integral and closed channel pool -type designs as pool designs have
largely replaced loop designs for SFRs.
Thermal spectrum breeder technologies, such as the MSBR planned by Oak Ridge National
Laboratory (ORNL), require frequent removal of FPs from the molten fuel salt as well as isolation of
233 Pa from high neutron fluence to achieve breeding gain. Non breeding thermal spectrum reactors

depend on adding fissile material to maintain criticality and do not require chemical FP separation.
Similarly, fast spectrum designs are much less sensitive to FPs due to the lower FP fast spectrum
capture cross sections. Note, however, that some level of FP removal is inherent in any liquid fuel
as gaseous materials bubble out and solid FPs are deposited onto surfaces. Fuel salt does not
have a radiation-damage-determined lifetime, and even thermal spectrum MSRs with sufficient FP
removal can consume limited quantities of minor actinides. Fast spectrum MSRs and thermal
spectrum MSRs that incorporate fast flux traps can more efficiently consume minor actinides.
Consequently, fast spectrum MSRs are often viewed as waste burners.
Both thermal and fast spectrum MSR technologies must account for gaseous FPs by incorporating
an off-gas system in the design. Otherwise, accumulating gases would pressurize the fuel salt
boundary. Fast spectrum MSRs will generate larger quantities of fission gases per unit fuel volume
because of their higher power densities. A typical salt -fueled MSR functional block diagram is
shown in Figure 5.2.

37

Figure 5.2. Generic salt-fueled MSR functional block diagram.

Most MSR technologies involve fuel salt flowing in and out of the core region. This means that as
the delayed neutron precursors are mobile, reactivity will be impacted by the core fuel salt flow
rate. In addition, most MSRs have a large negative temperatu re coefficient of reactivity. If an
accident or transient causes a rise in core temperature, the fuel expands out of the core region and
the neutron spectrum shifts, adding negative reactivity. The moderator temperature coefficient can
be positive in larger thermal spectrum designs. This would generally be accommodated by including
a spectrally sensitive neutron absorber such as 167 Er in the core but may alternatively be
addressed by other negative reactivity insertion means such as active defueling (of all or part of
the core) or shutdown rod insertion.
Most of the core in a thermal spectrum MSR will be filled with neutron moderator material and flow
channels for the fuel salt. The fuel salt may directly contact the moderator or may be contained
within tubes cooled by a low-radioactivity coolant (similar to an LWR). This will necessitate
moderator support structures for the thermal spectrum core. Thermal spectrum cores will be
surrounded by additional moderator/reflector material to improve the neutron economy and a
subsequent outer layer of neutron absorber material to minimize the neutron flux on the reactor
vessel and containment dose levels. The core of most fast spectrum MSRs, on the other hand, will
contain no materials other than the fuel salt and possibly sparging gas to aid in removing FP gases
and to serve as a reactivity control mechanism. Alternat ively, the fast spectrum fuel salt may be
confined to fuel channels cooled by an external high atomic mass coolant similar to an SFR. As with
a thermal spectrum MSR, fast spectrum MSR cores will be surrounded by neutron
reflector/shielding materials to improve neutron economy, reduce radiation damage to the reactor
vessel, and decrease the containment dose levels (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018).
MSRs can be grouped according to where the heat transfer from the fuel occurs: (1) within the core
(similar to solid-fueled reactors), (2) within the vessel outside the core (integral design), or (3)
outside the vessel (loop design) (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018). Basic diagrams of an
integral-type MSR and a loop-type MSR are shown in Figure 5.3. The different reactor
configurations optimize different aspects of MSR performance and are a multifaceted design

38

decision. From a high-level perspective, closed channel designs in which fuel-salt-containing tubes
(either resembling fuel rods or a shell-and-tube heat exchanger) are externally cooled by an
unfueled coolant minimize the quantity of fuel salt and decrease the radiation dose outside of the
reactor vessel. Integral designs minimize the p otential for fuel salt leaks from the reactor vessel
and overall piping complexity, whereas loop designs simplify the hydraulic component requirements
(Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018).

Figure 5.3. Schematic examples of an integral MSR and a loop MSR. Source: Holcomb and
Flanagan (2017).

5.1.1. MSR Design Systems and Processes
MSR configurations are evolving much more rapidly than those of other reactor classes. The basic
layout of both liquid-metal-cooled reactors and HTGRs were established decades ago and have not
substantially changed over the intervening years. Their comparative design stability as well a s the
multiple previous technology demonstrations allows referencing a relevant, substantial body of
prior art. In contrast, nearly every new MSR design entrant has proposed significantly different fuel

39

system configurations, with multiple different options proposed over the past decade. Moreover,
the historic plant configurations were profoundly influenced by the hydraulic component s and
materials that were state of the art more than half a century ago and have comparatively less
relevance to current designs than their solid-fueled counterparts. Consequently, MSR risk and
vulnerability evaluations will be more speculative than those for solid -fueled ARs and need to be
based primarily upon fundamental phys ics and chemistry rather than published design information.
To the extent possible, public-facing documents and the historical record have been employed as
the foundation for risk and vulnerability evaluation. However, a significant amount of engineering
judgment has also been extrapolated from historic technology to understand the change in
objectives from decades ago. For example, a primary historic MSBR program objective was to
produce as much separated fissile material as possible to minimize the need for (what was then
thought to be scarce) uranium resources. In contrast, a basic design requirement today for any
nuclear fuel cycle is to avoid creating accessible, separated fissile materials.
The following assumptions about MSR designs will be helpful in directing and limiting the scope of
MSR accident and sabotage discussions (Holcomb et al. 2020):
 • Multiple layers of essentially leak-tight containment will be employed (functional
 containment).
 • Segmented containment (system cells) will be employed to limit accident propagation
 between systems.
 • In the event of a breach of the salt boundary, the dispersion of radioactive materials is
 minimized due to the low-pressure operating profile of MSRs.
 • A core catch pan (stainless steel and a thermal barrier) is included to prevent direct fuel
 salt interaction with concrete. The catch pan will be sloped to allow spilled salt to flow into
 a cooled tank.
 • The first non-salt-wetted containment layer (e.g., guard vessels, guard pipes, and individual
 reactor cells) will be a material with characteristics similar to stainless steel.
 • Rupture disks or other methods will be employed to isolate fuel salt systems for pressure
 spikes that originate in the power cycle or other connected systems.
 • Containment areas will be controlled to minimize the likelihood and impact of fire (e.g.,
 limited combustible materials, inerted atmosphere).
 • Reactor containment will be primarily below grade. The reactor building surrounding the
 reactor containment may be protected by a berm or it may be below grade.
 • Connected fluids are at similar but higher pressure than fuel salt during normal operation
 to ensure that fuel salt does not leak into connected systems.
 • Large quantities of water (or other phase change or pressure-generating materials) will not
 be used in or near the containment.
 • Elevation and/or watertight compartments will be used to minimize flooding vulnerability.
MSRs with these design features would have the following intrinsic favorable safety characteristics
(Holcomb et al. 2020):
 • The potential for the release of stored nuclear energy is minimized by negative reactivity
 feedback and by maintaining the core in a peak reactivity configuration during normal
 operation

40

 • Low system pressures
 • Fuel salt with a large margin to boiling, which minimizes its potential for direct
 pressurization
 • Fuel salt in a low chemical energy state that do es not react exothermically with
 environmental materials
 • Limited accident escalation from smaller anticipated operational occurrences by avoiding
 stored energy sources (chemical, physical, and mechanical) within the containment that
 could be released and cause the cascading failures needed to breach multiple layers of
 containment.
The above considerations for MSR designs provide unique challenges and considerations for
security analyses. The following sections discuss in greater detail the fuel and safety system
considerations that are of greatest interest for this study.

5.1.1.1. Fuel Preparation
Fuel can be prepared at an offsite facility and transported to the site for use by the MSR , or it can
be prepared at the MSR site or at a centralized site among several MSRs if the owner/operator
controls the land. A fresh fuel storage facility at the MSR site will be necessary. Fuel preparation
may include mixing a concentrated fuel salt mixture with additional salt and additives to prepare
the operational fuel-salt concentration mixture.

5.1.1.2. Fuel Storage
One advantage of MSRs is that they are not limited by the life of a heterogeneous fuel element for
which excess reactivity is designed into the fuel element and the associated control system to
account for fuel burnup. MSRs can be operated with minimal excess reactivity by adding fuel
concentrate continuously or in batch mode during operation. This requires a fuel storage system to
support reactor operations.
The fuel storage system supports storage, fuel addition, and fuel removal to maintain the reactivity
within operational bounds by adding or removing fuel salt. The frequency of fuel salt
addition/removal is determined by the amount of reactivity adjustment available via the control
system. Daily additions/removals are anticipated to be typical (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner
2018). The amount of fuel storage required is determined by a combination of the reactor power
rating, the recent MSR power generation h istory, and the observed reactivity. Fuel will be added
(MSR in burner configuration) or removed (MSR in breeder configuration) to maintain the desired
power level at the specified operating temperature (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018). MSR
cores that incorporate flow channels may be segmented to facilitate load -following. For example,
one region of fuel salt may be drained (along with adjusting the fuel composition in other regions to
maintain criticality) when demand is low and reintroduced into the co re when demand rises.
Limited term used fuel storage will be in cooled drain tanks within containment rather than longer -
term fuel storage.
The MSR fuel storage may include fresh fuel and clean salt, or it may include recycled fuel salt. The
attractiveness and severity of sabotage directed at the storage system will depend on the size,
location, and content of the storage system. Aside from the potential radiological risk associated
with the successful sabotage of the storage system, MSR operation would also have to be

41

suspended since MSRs depend on frequent fuel manipulations. Fresh fuel salt areas are likely to
be more accessible than used fuel salt areas due to the radiation shielding requirements for used
fuel salt.

5.1.1.3. MSR Heat Removal
A fast or thermal MSR primary system consists of the core, heat exchangers that transfer heat from
the fuel salt to the coolant salt, and pumps that circulate the fuel salt mixture. Heat transfer from
the fuel salt can occur in-core (pool-channel configuration), in-vessel (integral configuration), or ex-
vessel (loop configuration). Fuel salt requires a gas expansion region to avoid the potential for
pressure surges and to accommodate the release of fission gases. The functional block diagram in
Figure 5.2 reflects that this primary system interfaces with the off -gas system and the possible
interface with a cleanup system. Some MSR technologies may also employ a fuel salt drain tank
system. Fuel salt or FPs are present in all these interfacing systems and each system will require a
cooling system. Almost 80% of the gaseous heat load is generated in the first hour following
release from the fuel salt and well less than 1% of decay heat remains in the gas stream after 2
days (Smith and Bell 1969). Consequently, some MSRs are designed to keep the off -gas stream
within the reactor vessel for the first few hours, reduci ng the significance of the ex-vessel heat
rejection system.
5.1.1.3.1 Reactor Decay Heat Removal
The inherent characteristics of MSRs (e.g., quantity of heat being produced, thermal storage
potential within containment, practical temperature limits of engineering materials) dictate how
decay heat can be passively transferred from the FPs to the enviro nment (Holcomb et al. 2020).
Decay heat can be removed indirectly from an MSR by passively transferring heat by radiation and
convection from the exterior surface of the reactor vessel to a natural -draft-driven cooling system.
This is typically referred to as an RVACS. Alternatively, decay heat can be removed directly from an
MSR by inserting a natural-circulation-driven decay heat removal loop into the fuel salt. This is
typically referred to as a DRACS (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018). The MSR fuel salt circuit can
also be immersed in a large pool of coolant salt to remove decay heat. This type of cooling
configuration is referred to as a pool reactor vessel auxiliary cooling system (PRACS).
As with any fission reactor, immediately following shutdown after extended operation, the FPs
produce ~7% of full power. For the MSRs that rapidly and proactively remove and isolate fission
gases, roughly 40% of the decay heat could be generated in the cov er gas handling and storage
systems. This reduces the cooling needed in the reactor core at shutdown but adds locations that
require cooling to ensure safe plant operations (Holcomb et al. 2020).
Practical heat rejection to air in an RVACS or DRACS generally employs a structural chimney to
drive natural air circulation past an air draft heat exchanger. A PRACS eventually needs to reject
decay heat to the environment, although the pool for a PRACS would provide additional coping time
due to the large thermal mass. An MSR off -gas system or cover gas system will couple to the
headspace above the fuel salt. The headspace will contain volatile FPs and FP compounds,
activation products, particulates, and mists generated from the movement of the salt through the
primary heat transport system. The fuel salt may also be sparged with an inert gas, such as helium
or recirculated 85 Kr, to liberate entrained neutron poisons (e.g., 135 Xe) and other gaseous FPs from

42

the fuel salt. The gases will be highly radioactive and will constitute a substantial heat load (a few
tens of megawatts for a large reactor) (McFarlane et al. 2019).
Roughly 40% of FPs have a gaseous phase in their decay chains. Noble gases are not highly soluble
in fuel salts, so much of the FP burden will accumulate in an MSR’s cover gas. This cover gas can
either be kept mostly within the reactor vessel, allowing on ly the long-lived fission gases to escape,
or the cover gas can be removed into an off -gas system to prevent daughter FPs from accumulating
in the fuel salt. Historically, activated carbon beds or a hydroxide-based scrubber were proposed to
hold the fission gases within the off-gas system (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018). Today,
zeolite beds would perform much the same function as activated carbon beds without the potential
fire vulnerability. A scraping system would be employed to return solid deposits to the fuel salt that
plate out in the first stage of the off -gas system.
The off-gas total decay heat load for the MSBR design (a large MSR) was estimated to be 21.3
MWt, including entrained aerosols and mists. As a result, the upstream portions of the cover gas
system could have a significant thermal load during normal operations. Therefore, an active cooling
system will need to provide cooling during normal operations and a passive decay heat removal
system will be needed in the event of a loss of forced cooling (Holcomb et al. 2020). For this
reason, some designers have elected to employ an in-vessel fission gas delay line to keep the
thermal energy where it is beneficial during normal operations at the expense of not employing a
sparging-based gas disengagement system, which would yield more gas than practical to keep
within the reactor vessel for hours.
The major components of the off-gas system will likely be in a containment cell separate from the
reactor to prevent damage in one system from impacting the other. Sabotage to an active cooling
system for the off-gas system could cause damage to the system infrastructure , leading to a leak of
FP gases. Mitigation could include a passive system that can handle the short -term high-heat load
and the longer-term lower heat load. Designs that keep the fission gases within the reactor vessel
for the first few hours may avoid the need for a separate system to prevent the release of
radioactive materials during off-normal events.
5.1.1.3.2 Cleanup System Decay Heat Removal
A fuel salt solids removal system or polishing system consists largely of a high -surface-area
mechanical filter. The purpose of the side stream filter would be to limit the quantity of FP solids
that are deposited onto the fuel salt primary system internals and their potential agglomeration
into erosive particles. The filter is likely to be made from porous metal with a composition similar
to the primary loop alloy (to limit dissimilar material corrosion) and will likely be placed in a
recirculating side stream from the reactor core (McFarlane et al. 2019). The accumulated FPs and
other materials will generate decay heat and will require a passive heat removal system. Flotation -
based solid separation systems have also been proposed. In this case, the solids would accumulate
in a scum layer that would be decanted from the bulk of the fuel salt in a side stream.
5.1.1.3.3 Drain Tank Decay Heat Removal
As was done for the MSRE and planned for the MSBR, some designs include a fuel salt drain tank
as a safety, maintenance, or load-following system. The fuel salt drain tank could be connected to
the bottom of the reactor vessel through a drain line with pre ssure-based control of the fluid
location. For example, MSRs could employ a lower gooseneck -type connection and differential

43

pressure to keep the fuel salt within the reactor or drain tank as desired. A gas accumulator could
be used to remove the fuel salt more rapidly from the core. A gooseneck- and accumulator-type fuel
volume control system has been used for aqueous fuel reactors. Alternatively, a freeze valve could
be used as a passive safety measure to enable draining of the fuel salt as a response to
overtemperature events. While a freeze valve was implemented at MSRE, freeze valves tend to be
slow to operate and can cause stress on piping. Pressure-based fluid location manipulation
appears to be more likely in future plants. Passive cooling is required to remove decay heat from
the fuel salt. Passive decay heat removal would have vulnerabilities similar to those for passive
decay heat removal for fuel salt in the reactor vessel.

5.1.1.4. Reactivity Control
Neutron-absorbing control rods are the typical means for controlling reactivity in an LWR, but there
are many more ways to control reactivity in an MSR. MSR designs can control reactivity through
fuel displacement, neutron absorption, neutron reflection, n eutron spectral adjustment, or a
combination of these methods. As a result, MSRs typically use the term control elements in place
of the term control rods. Control elements can be solids, liquids, or gases, and they can be
passively or actively positioned. References to fully inserted or fully withdrawn elements must be
carefully considered regarding what condition relates to the most or the least reactivity state
(Belles et al. 2020).
As noted above, some designs incorporate a fuel salt drain tank as a safety, maintenance, or load -
following system. When included in the design, the fuel salt storage tank is hydraulically connected
to the reactor core and serves as a safe storage volume for the fuel salt when it is removed from
the circulation loop. A critical mass cannot exist in the storage tank due to both geometry
(increased leakage) and insufficient neutron moderation. The drain system can also be designed to
provide leak protection for the fuel salt circulating loop by providing a storage location for fuel salt
leakage into any guard system (Robertson et al. 1971).
Even though fuel salt removal to a drain tank is a positive MSR shutdown mechanism, it is not
normally used as an emergency procedure since numerous other reactor control and safety
mechanisms can quickly take the reactor subcritical while fuel salt continues to circulate to
facilitate FP decay heat removal via the primary heat exchangers or other dedicated decay heat
removal heat exchangers (Robertson et al. 1971), such as the RVACS or DRACS. Still, a fuel salt
removal system provides shutdown DID.
Because of the large negative fuel salt temperature coefficient of reactivity, MSRs have large
safety margins. Even if some reactivity control measures (active control elements) are affected by a
loss of power, in most designs negative thermal feedback wil l effectively drive the reactor
subcritical. As noted, if fuel salt removal is included in the design, it will provide additional defense
because the fuel salt removal trigger mechanism will activate and direct the fuel salt into a non -
critical configuration.

5.1.1.5. Radionuclide Retention
The functional block diagram in Figure 5.2 shows the numerous potential primary system
interfaces. Due to the radioactive content of all these interfacing systems, they will all be enclosed
in a functional (layered) containment structure and behind bio -shields.

44

5.1.1.6. Fuel Salt
In MSRs, FPs are generated and entrained in the fuel salt as the reactor is operated. Gaseous,
soluble, and insoluble FPs build up in the fuel salt during normal MSR operation. Gaseous FPs,
such as xenon and krypton, bubble off continuously and are typically removed from the cover gas
space through a gas management system. Xe-135 is a significant neutron poison, so its removal
improves neutronic efficiency. Soluble and non -soluble FPs remain in the fuel salt. Therefore, the
fuel salt in the MSR becomes highly radioactive. Insoluble FPs tend to plate out on salt -wetted
surfaces. Soluble FPs can be removed from the fuel salt by chemical or physical processing. This
can be accomplished using batch operations or continuously in a side stream. In some MSR
designs, the filtering system or chemical processing loop can also be used to add additional fuel to
the fuel salt. Because soluble FPs remain in the fuel salt, the fuel salt acts as a radionuclide
retention mechanism.

5.1.1.7. Containment
The distributed nature of the fuel salt supports segmenting the containment into various functional
cells with the equipment needed for operations in each functional area. This is like the
segmentation found in hot cell facilities. This lowers the risk of releasing radionuclides from a
failure in one containment cell to another containment cell (Holcomb et al. 2020).
Although the fuel salt provides a limited physical barrier, the fuel system boundary (e.g., vessel,
cover gas boundary, and associated piping) forms the first nominal leak -tight containment layer
(essentially equivalent to the fuel clad in an LWR). The second containment layer, more typically
referred to as reactor containment, is the first containment layer not directly contacting or wetted
by the fuel salt or cover gas (outside the fuel system boundary) (Holcomb et al. 2020). This may
include guard vessels, guard pipes, and individual system cells.
The second containment layer is assumed to be contained within a third barrier layer, typically
thought of as a reactor building. As noted, there may be multiple system cells within the reactor
building that limit the risk of widespread radionuclide release. The requirement to withstand large
civilian aircraft impact (10 CFR 150) may provide an economic incentive to locate low -pressure
containments below grade. Below-grade system cells will be protected beneath significant bio -
shields to protect workers, sensitive equipment, and the environment. The shielding mass can also
serve to absorb and/or deflect impacts from above (Holcomb et al. 2020). In addition, underground
structures exhibit excellent earthquake performance because they are constrained and supported
by the surrounding medium, generally do not move independently of the soil or rock medium nor
are they subjected to vibration amplification (U.S. NRC 2006). The interface between the reactor
containment layer and the reactor building layer determines the functional requirements for
containment during accident scenarios (Holcomb et al. 2020). The reactor building layer is more
likely to contain typical building services , which provide the most complex system interactions.
Molten salts are readily contaminated by air exposure, but they do not have any energetic chemical
reactions with air or water. Hot fuel salt would energetically and physically react with water,
resulting in a steam explosion. Consequently, the reactor building environment, or the environment
in the individual functional cells, may be inerted to minimize the potential for contamination during
any maintenance activities in which the salt boundary is broken , and only limited quantities of
water will be within the reactor building. The containment floor will almost certainly be made of

45

stainless steel with a thermal barrier to prevent hot salt from directly interacting with concrete in
the event of a severe accident (McFarlane et al. 2019).

5.1.1.8. Off-Gas System
Following fission of the different fuel types, a variety of radioactive gaseous FPs or nonradioactive
gases/vapors mixed in with radionuclides could be released, including particulates, aerosols,
reactive gases, hydrogen (e.g., tritium), water, nitrogen, o xygen, and noble gases (e.g., Xe, Kr).
These radionuclides will be generated continuously during reactor operation, and releases must be
managed to be below regulatory limits (Riley et al. 2018).
The mass load of fuel salt constituents in the off -gas system will be determined by the
thermochemical and thermophysical properties of the salt and the transport of fluids through the
reactor. The long-lived noble gas FPs must be removed from the fuel sal t to avoid pressurization of
the fuel salt boundary. Shorter-lived gaseous FPs may also be removed from the fuel salt as a
matter of convenience (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018). Operational experience with MSRs is
limited to the MSRE, which provided substantial information to support the conceptual design for
the larger MSBR. In the MSBR design, the off -gas system was to rely on charcoal beds, in which
radionuclides can decay for 90 days, leaving 85 Kr as the most important contributor to the source
term (McFarlane et al. 2019). Note the role of charcoal beds would largely be replaced by zeolite in
a modern design to avoid the potential for combustion.

5.1.1.9. Online Cleanup System
The need for an online cleanup system or polishing system will vary with MSR system design. Most
thermal MSR designs will require removal of FP poisons and periodic fuel additions to maintain
criticality. A single-fluid, thermal spectrum MSR implementing a Th-U breed-and-burn fuel cycle will
require substantial fuel salt processing to achieve breeding gain. On the other hand, fast spectrum
breed-and-burn MSR designs operating on a U-Pu fuel cycle may require little (if any) fuel salt
processing beyond timely redox adjustment to compensate for fission being an oxidative process .
Note that FP decay back toward stability is likely to be a reductive process, so redox control will be
time dependent (Holcomb et al. 2020). Polishing systems can be run in either continuous or batch -
style processing routes in which FPs can be removed during operation or at end of life (or end of
batch) of the reactor (Riley et al. 2018).
As with other major systems, any required fuel salt processing will likely be performed in
containment cells separated from the reactor core to minimize the potential for disruptions in one
system to affect another (Holcomb et al. 2020). If needed, the fuel salt polishing system may
consist of a high-surface-area mechanical filter, likely a nickel mesh, to promote the deposition of
suspended, undissolved fission and/or corrosion products. Alternatively, a flotation -based
suspended particle separation system 4 may be implemented to remove insoluble materials from
 3F

the fuel salt without requiring a mechanical filter (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018). The filter
will require periodic changing, which involve s pulling the filter out of the salt, allowing any salt
inside the filter to drain, installing a new filter, and then packaging the old filter for disposal. The
filter’s purpose would conceptually resemble that of resin beds in PWRs. Like the filters that may

4 See https://www.thermopedia.com/content/769 for more information.

46

be used in the off-gas system, routine maintenance and exchange of particle filters with the reactor
in operation may be particularly problematic. The dose rate on the old filter will be very high due to
the insoluble FPs and any residual fuel salt on the filter (McFarlane et al. 2019). If necessary, the
fuel addition system will likely consist of a fuel salt melting pot connected to the polishing system
piping with a valve (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018).
Online cleanup systems or polishing systems will only be required for some MSR designs depending
on spectrum and breeding goal. The systems will have smaller flow side functions and may not
operate continuously.

5.1.1.10. Maintenance
After reactor operation, radiation levels near any system containing fuel salt will be much too high
for staff access, even following shut down and fuel salt draining. Access inside primary
containment will likely not be possible (with the potential exception of channel configuration MSRs
that employ non-fueled coolants), and access to certain areas within the reactor building may,
likewise, be extremely limited depending on the amount of shielding available and any shutdown
operations in progress. Therefore, many maintenance activities will require remote handling using
cameras and long-handled tools, or electronics that are highly tolerant of radiation with local
shielding (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018). Some designs call for significant core component
reconfigurations/replacements to establish an economically viable plant life. These designs will
also require consideration for remote component replacements and storage of activated
components.

5.1.2. MSR C onsiderations for the Back End of the Fuel Cycle
The back end of the fuel cycle for an LWR includes:
 • Spent fuel storage
 • Streams for operating wastes
 • Streams from decommissioning and decontamination .
By comparison, the typical MSR back-end waste materials will be more complex. Typical generic
MSR waste streams include the following (Riley et al. 2018):
 • Used fuel
 • Unseparated salt-based waste streams
 • Separated salt-based waste streams
 • Carbon-based waste streams
 • Metal-based waste streams
 • Streams for operating wastes
 • Streams from decommissioning and decontamination.
5.1.2.1.1 Used Fuel
The concepts of used fuel and spent fuel are different for a salt -fueled MSR. By design, solid fuel
accumulates FPs and radiation damage and ages with time, eventually requiring replacement.
Therefore, solid fuel is considered spent after a period of operation. On the other hand, liquid fuel
does not accumulate radiation damage.

47

With appropriate maintenance of the fuel salt chemical composition, the liquid fuel will not age or
need to be replaced. 5 MSR fuel salts inherently contain the FPs that do not escape during
 4F

operations, as well as accumulated amounts of corrosion and environmental contamination
products. For MSR designs that do not implement FP removal, the fuel salt will eventually reach an
equilibrium level of FPs as FPs burn out, transmuting into a gaseous or non -soluble form, and build
up within the fuel salt. For designs that provide for reconditioning of the fuel salt and those with
equilibrium FP contents that result in acceptable thermophysical properties, the fuel salt would be
usable for future generations of MSRs (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018). In this case, MSR fuel
can be referred to as used, but not spent. The liquid fuel would not become waste until MSRs
cease to be an operating reactor class.
Therefore, MSR fuel salt can be reused indefinitely provided it has adequate fissile content.
Consequently, MSRs do not necessarily have an equivalent to semipermanent spent fuel storage
(Holcomb et al. 2020). Nevertheless, MSR fuel salt must be stored between uses. Processing and
storage of the used fuel may be performed near the reactor site at a facility designed for this
purpose. Likewise, processing may be performed offsite . The fuel salt will need to be stored
temporarily to allow for radionuclide decay before it is transported to an offsite processing site.
Additionally, the radionuclides that separate from the liquid fuel salt (gases, vapors, and insoluble
materials) will require containment, and used fuel salt will require near-term cooling while in
storage.
5.1.2.1.2 Salt-Based Waste Streams
If the used fuel is no longer required for re use in another MSR, then constituents in the fuel salt
may be separated and recycled. An important reason to remove the halide s from the salt is to
impart radiolytic and chemical stability to the waste form (McFarlane et al. 2019). Because of its
solubility in water, the MSR salt is not a good medium for long -term immobilization. Therefore,
conversion of unseparated salt streams to another form is necessary. A variety of options for
immobilizing unseparated salt streams are available depending on the salt type. Options include
glass, ceramic, glass-ceramic, glass-bonded ceramic, and ceramic-metallic forms (Grabaskas, Fei,
and Jerden 2022; Riley et al. 2018).
5.1.2.1.3 Carbon-Based Waste Streams
Graphite will be a waste form in the case of thermal spectrum MSRs, and it may be the limiting
factor of the reactor lifetime (McFarlane et al. 2019). Graphite damage is introduced by neutron
irradiation in the structure of graphite core components. Dimensional changes tend to occur over
time with irradiation. The rate of change depends on the carbon grade and the manufacturing
process (Riley et al. 2018).
The graphite will be contaminated with FPs captured by recoil , with fluoride from the salt itself, and
the graphite may also have a significant loading of tritium from activation of lithium in the core.

5.1.2.2. MSR—Other Systems
In addition to the primary systems and the waste streams found in MSRs, other systems that
support the operation and safety response of the plant will need analysis. Though many of these

5 Additional fuel may be added over time as the MSR is operated to account for fuel burnup.

48

systems are similar to those in use at current reactors, there are some MSR specific considerations
that should be analyzed.
5.1.2.2.1 Support Systems
The current U.S. vendors have closely held significant design features for their respective designs ;
therefore, detailed information on support systems is not available. However, considerable
information for the proposed MSBR is available, as well as general information on proposed
support systems for advanced passive reactors. The following discussion is based on this
information, but may vary for other designs.
5.1.2.2.2 Rupture Disks
Each of the four MSBR salt coolant loops (intermediate loops) were to be provided with rupture
disks to prevent system overpressurization should a steam generator tube leak occur (Robertson et
al. 1971). The rupture disks would help protect the primary heat exchangers from damage due to
overpressure.
5.1.2.2.3 Coolant Salt Drain System
A series of four tanks was proposed for the MSBR design to allow maintenance on the salt coolant
loop (intermediate loop). A series arrangement was adopted to facilitate heat removal if the salt
coolant became contaminated with fuel salt. The tanks are in a cell directly b eneath the steam
generator cells. This cell is heated to about 800°F by electric resistance heaters to maintain the
salt above its liquidus temperature (Robertson et al. 1971).
5.1.2.2.4 Power Conversion (MSBR—Super-Heated Steam)
The thermal energy generated by the MSBR was to be converted to electric power in a steam cycle
employing once-through steam-generator superheaters, a turbine generator, and a regenerative
feedwater heating system (Robertson et al. 1971; Rosenthal 1969). Terrestrial Energy, Exodys
Energy, and ThorCon International also plan to use steam generators to provide super-heated
steam to a turbine generator (IAEA 2022). Flibe Energy has proposed using a supercritical carbon
dioxide gas turbine (IAEA 2022) to provide electricity. Other power conversion systems are possible
such as storing the reactor heat in molten -salt storage tanks to provide steam to on -demand
turbine generators (Moltex Energy in Canada has proposed this option [ IAEA 2022]). Designers may
also opt to provide process heat directly to nearby industry.
5.1.2.2.5 Ultimate Heat Sink
After reactor shutdown, under normal operating conditions, decay heat is removed from the fuel
salt via the steam system. The steam can be dumped to a condenser and removed as normal until
steam can no longer be generated. At that point, the diminishing decay heat is dumped to the
atmosphere or to a controlled volume surface pond to control overcooling the fuel salt. This is done
through the RVACS, DRACS, or PRACS, as discussed in Section 5.4.1.3.1. During a loss of forced
circulation, decay heat can immediately be removed through the RVACS, DRACS, or PRACS. In this
case, there will be a higher heat load on these heat removal systems and the
surrounding/supporting SSC.
5.1.2.2.6 Diesel Generator (Non-Safety-Related)
Most passive reactor designs continue to include a support diesel generator (DG). The support DG
is not required to start and load in a short period of time to support safety -related SSC. Because

49

the support DG does not power safety-related SSC, it does not require weekly testing that often
leads to maintenance and related safety-related SSC outages. However, the support DG provides
DID by supplying backup power to lighting, instrumentation, batteries, etc.
5.1.2.2.7 Battery System
In the event of a loss of offsite power and a failure of the standby DGs, battery systems will be
used to supply backup power to lighting, instrumentation, batteries, etc. In the MSBR, batteries
were also planned to supply power to pony motors on the salt -cooling pumps (intermediate loop) to
provide forced circulation for decay heat removal to continue for an unspecified interval of time
(Rosenthal 1969). This was intended to ease the heat load on the MSBR RVACS.
5.1.2.2.8 Pipe Heat
If the fuel salt or coolant salt is overcooled, a phase change will occur in the salt. The salt will
become solid. Electric pipe and vessel heating systems may be necessary to maintain each salt
system above its liquidus temperature to avoid system damage.

5.1.2.3. Typical Plant Footprint
The typical layout for most proposed MSR designs is a single high -bay reactor building over below-
grade cells containing the reactor, intermediate loops, off -gas system, fuel salt conditioning, etc.
This is typified in Figure 5.4 for the proposed MSBR (Rosenthal 1969). An additional structure for
the power conversion system would be necessary. Other separate or combined structures may
include support DGs, breaker rooms, waste handling, used fuel handling and processing, gas stack,
a control room, administrative, training, security, and a warehouse. The overall footprint would be
directly correlated with plant thermal energy.

50

Figure 5.4. MSBR reactor building. Source: Rosenthal (1969).

5.1.3. MSR Consequence Assessment
The major risk from any nuclear power plant is its potential to release radioactive materials into
the environment. A secondary risk is loss of production (power or process heat) for extended
periods of time that negatively impacts consumers and their confidence. (i.e., energy security).
Both the safety and security features of a plant are intended to prevent the release of radioactive
materials as human error is frequently indistinguishable from malicious acts. For example, to
support both safety and security objectives, sampling lines into radioactive material areas could be
made as small as possible and incorporate check valves to prevent introducing damaging
materials.
One way of evaluating the risk consequences of a nuclear plant begins with identifying the
magnitude of radioactive material sources. Following this, an assessment is made of their
dispersibility if released from their intended locations. Next, the barriers to the material release are
identified, and an assessment is made of their vulnerability to being bypassed. The three elements
(magnitude of potential sources, vulnerability to dispersal, and barriers to release) are then
combined into an overall evaluation of the risk consequences of the plant.

51

MSR risk consequences depend on the plant status. For example, power-range operation results in
the largest sources of radioactive materials , and continuing operation builds up progressively more
long-lived radionuclides. However, barriers to release are fully in place during operation. Similarly,
release and dispersal of reactor off-gases during power-range operation or within the first couple of
hours after shutdown would be catastrophic. Consequently, multiple substantial barriers are
employed to prevent large, early off-gas release. The consequences from off-gas release would be
much less a few hours later as the short -lived decay products will have decayed and the vapors and
aerosols will have plated out into much less dispersible forms. Table 5.2 summarizes risk
consequence rankings for both thermal and fast spectrum MSRs with typical features. Explanations
for each table row follow.

Table 5.2. Summary of MSR risk consequence rankings for various sources .
 Reactor Source Maximum Dose Dispersibility Barrier
 Type Potential Ranking Ranking
 Ranking
 Thermal fuel salt Very High Low Low
 Fast fuel salt Very High Medium Low
 Off-gas initial holdup Very High Medium Low
 Off-gas secondary holdup Medium High Low
 Fast reactor vessel High Low Low
 Thermal vessel including Medium Low Low
 MSR graphite
 Used fuel: ½ core drain Very High Low Low
 tanks
 Bred fuel storage Very High Low Low
 Fuel waste packaging High Low Medium
 Used fuel air cooled Very High Low Medium
 Used fuel liquid cooled Very High Low Low
NOTE: Multiple barriers, inherent or applied, results in a lower ranking. For example, the “Low” ranking is characterized
by multiple inherent or mix of inherent/applied barriers while the “High” ranking is characterized by no inherent barrier
or a single applied barrier. Section 4.3. provides a more detailed description of these rankings.

5.1.3.1. Thermal Fuel Salt
Fuel salt is nuclear fuel so it contains substantial amounts of radionuclides once used. When
evaluating the maximum dose potential, all radioactive materials are considered independent of
their ability to be dispersed or the number of barriers in place to limit their release and transport.
The fuel salt is located within containment in the fuel salt circuit, which is within the guard circuit,
so it is inaccessible. The fuel salt is at low pressure and is well below its boiling point, so
significant additional energy would be required for it to disperse. Note, the volatile species from
the fuel salt are released into the off -gas as part of normal operations.

52

Figure 5.5. Thermal fuel salt.

5.1.3.2. Fast Fuel Salt
Fast fuel salt is similar to thermal fuel salt in that it contains substantial quantities of
radionuclides and is located behind multiple barriers. Fuel salt is nuclear fuel, so it will contain
substantial amounts of radionuclides once used. The fuel salt is located within containment in the
fuel salt circuit, which is within the guard circuit, so it is inaccessible. The fuel salt is at low
pressure and is well below its boiling point, so significant additional energy would be required for it
to disperse. The dispersibility of fast fuel salt is slightly higher than that of thermal fuel salt as the
boiling point of UCl4 is only 791°C. While uranium is not highly radioactive, the fuel salt does
release slightly more material than thermal spectrum salts. Note that the volatile species from the
fuel salt are released into the off-gas as part of normal operations.

Figure 5.6. Fast fuel salt.

5.1.3.3. Off-Gas Initial Holdup
Fissions produce radioactive noble gases and noble gas precursors. Some of the noble gases will
leave the fuel salt. Initially, the off-gases will contain a few percent of the reactor power. The most
likely holdup location for the gases is within the reactor vessel for designs that do not increase the
volume of the gases to disengage them from the fuel salt , and in the fuel salt drain tanks for those
that do increase the off-gas volume. In both cases, the gases would be dispersible, but the
suspended aerosols and vapors would be only somewhat dispersible since they will plate out

53

locally. All the material would be behind multiple barriers. The potential dose, barriers to release,
and dispersibility are shown graphically in the following Figure 5.7.

Figure 5.7. Off-gas initial holdup.

5.1.3.4. Off-Gas Secondary Holdup
Once the off-gas has had at least a couple of hours to decay and for its daughters to plate out, it
becomes significantly less radioactive as its primary radioactive constituents become noble gases
and then decay to stable isotopes. The off-gas will most likely be held up within a zeolite bed for
further decay and to allow any carried-over daughter products or volatile species to plate out. The
gases retain high dispersibility but have only medium potential dose. The potential dose, barriers to
release, and dispersibility are shown graphically in Figure 5.8.

Figure 5.8. Off-gas secondary holdup.

5.1.3.5. Fast Spectrum Reactor Vessel
Fuel-salt-wetted components will need to be replaced over the life of the plant. A salt-wetted
reactor vessel will be activated, have insoluble FPs deposited onto its interior surface, and have
recoil FPs embedded in its interior surface. The potential dose from the vessel will largely be
determined by the time between use and replacement as many of the fission and activation
products have short half-lives. However, the plant has a significant economic imperative to perform
the replacement and return to service as rapidly as possible. The potential dose, barriers to
release, and dispersibility are shown graphically in the Figure 5.9.

54

Figure 5.9. Fast spectrum reactor vessel.

5.1.3.6. Thermal Spectrum Reactor Vessel with Interior Graphite
Fuel-salt-wetted components will need to be replaced over the life of the plant. A salt-wetted
reactor vessel will be activated, have insoluble FPs deposited onto its interior surface, and have
recoil FPs embedded in its interior surface. Thermal spectrum reactor vessels will also contain
moderator material (likely graphite), which will also contain deposited FPs. The interior graphite
shielding may somewhat reduce the vessel activation. The potential dose from the vessel will
largely be determined by the time betwee n use and replacement as many of the fission and
activation products have short half-lives. However, the plant has a significant economic imperative
to perform the replacement and return to service as rapidly as possible. The potential dose,
barriers to release, and dispersibility are shown graphically in Figure 5.10.

Figure 5.10. Thermal spectrum reactor vessel with interior graphite .

5.1.3.7. Used Fuel Half Core Drain Tanks
Used fuel salt will contain substantial quantities of radionuclides —actinides, FPs, activated
corrosion products, etc. However, the drain tanks will be low down in the plant and behind multiple
barriers. Moreover, volatile materials will have been released during operation , decreasing the

55

material dispersibility. The potential dose, barriers to release, and dispersibility are shown
graphically in Figure 5.11.

Figure 5.11. Used fuel half core drain tanks.

5.1.3.8. Bred Fuel Storage
Bred fuel salt will contain substantial quantities of radionuclides —actinides, FPs, activated
corrosion products, etc. Bred fuel will accumulate over time. The bred fuel tanks will be located
behind multiple barriers. Moreover, volatile materials will have been released during operation ,
decreasing the material dispersibility. The potential dose, barriers to release, and dispersibility are
shown graphically in Figure 5.12.

Figure 5.12. Bred fuel storage.

5.1.3.9. Fuel Waste Packaging
Fuel salt may be prepared for longer-term storage or disposal at the site. As the fuel will need to
have decayed substantially, and only limited quantities of fuel salt would be process ed at a time,
the dose potential will be reduced. Further, the dispersibility of solidified salt will be low and the
processing will take place in a robust waste-handling facility. The potential dose, barriers to
release, and dispersibility are shown graphically in Figure 5.13.

56

Figure 5.13. Fuel waste packaging.

5.1.3.10. Used Fuel Air Cooled
Used fuel may be stored onsite following use, prior to re use or packaging as waste. Once the fuel
salt heat production is sufficiently low, the salt may be air cooled. The salt would be solid, so its
dispersibility would be low and the storage would be behind substantial shielding, but potentially it
would no longer be within containment. The potential dose, barriers to release, and dispersibility
are shown graphically in Figure 5.14.

Figure 5.14. Used fuel air cooled.

5.1.3.11. Used Fuel Liquid Cooled
Used fuel may be stored onsite following use, prior to re use or packaging as waste. Initially the fuel
salt may require liquid cooling if sufficient amounts of radionuclides have built up within it. The salt
would be solid, so its dispersibility would be low, and the storage would be behind substantial
shielding, within a robust structure. The potential dose, barriers to release, and dispersibility are
shown graphically in Figure 5.15.

57

Figure 5.15. Used fuel liquid cooled.

5.1.3.12. Consequence Assessment Summary
Different design features make radioactive materials more or less accessible. For example, the
primary heat transfer from the fuel salt can take place in -core (fuel salt in tubes—similar to solid-
fueled reactors), in-vessel (integral design), or ex-vessel (loop design). Loop designs were more
common historically due to the technological limits of available pumps and heat exchanger
materials. Integral and tube-type designs that keep the fuel salt in-vessel have become more
common (and are anticipated in future designs), much as pool-type designs have overtaken loop
designs for sodium-fast reactors. Keeping the fuel salt and off-gas in-vessel reduces the number of
locations for large quantities of radioactive materials during operation and therefore the number of
potential targets for an adversary. Most MSR designs include the ability to remove salt fuel from
the active circuit and put it in a drain tank. A liquid salt drain tank serves a purpose that is similar
to a spent fuel pool at an LWR. The drain tank locations provide shielding from the fuel salt
radioactivity, provide cooling, and ensure that the fuel remains well subcritical. Drain tanks are
generally located well below grade and are well shielded. Their major potential security
vulnerability is their interface with the environment for decay heat rejection. Security would be
improved by employing redundant, mechanically robust heat rejection systems. For example, decay
heat could be rejected to an engineered pond that employs natural ci rculation—that is buoyancy-
driven heat transfer via multiple, redundant natural circulation heat transfer loops. Off-gas after
the first few hours will primarily contain longer -lived radioactive noble gases (e.g., 85 Kr and 133 Xe)
that decay to stable isotopes and stable noble gas isotopes. The off-gas will also contain some
tritium, with the quantity dependent both on the fuel salt type (much less for fuel salts that do not
contain lithium or beryllium) and the off -gas separation technology. Designs that incorporate quasi-
online fuel salt processing will have additional areas that include substantial quantities of
radioactive materials, and breeder designs will include progressively larger quantities of fissile
materials.
The risk for release of radioactive materials begins with the receipt of radioactive materials onsite.
MSRs that begin operation with fresh fuel will have lower risks for releasing radioactivity to the
environment prior to startup. Fresh fuel for thermal spectrum MSRs would not need to contain
uranium enriched above 5 wt% (i.e., generally less than LWR fuel). Fresh fuel for fast spectrum
MSRs would need to incorporate additional fissile material. However, MSRs can also be started up
with previously used fuel. Multiple vendors have announced plans to derive their fissile material

58

from previously used LWR fuel. Also, fuel salt for designs that incorporate significant processing
would have a lifetime that exceeds that of the plant. Introducing used fuel salt into a new plant
introduces additional sabotage vulnerability as the fuel salt would be highly radioactive and initially
located in the fuel receipt and storage area. However, due to the radioactive nature of the fuel salt,
its storage area would need to incorporate significant shielding. Newly introduced fuel salt derived
from used LWR fuel may also be more attractive for diversion from peaceful uses as its plutonium
is unlikely to contain greater than 80% 238 Pu and may not produce a dose sufficient to decrease its
attractiveness category. Although not studied in this body of work, these considerations must be
accounted for by plant owners and should be studied in greater detail.
MSR waste streams will incorporate substantial quantities of radioactive materials. For example, if
a hydroxide scrubber is employed in the off -gas system, the hydroxide will progressively become
loaded with FPs and activated materials. Once saturated, the hydroxide would likely be processed
into saltstone (National Research Council et al. 2000). Local waste-processing facilities could be
vulnerable to sabotage. However, stabilized waste forms would be massive and both chemically
and mechanically robust. Similarly, fuel-salt-wetted components may be replaced multiple times
over the course of plant life. The components are anticipated to have substantial quantities of
radioactive material deposited on their surfaces and potentially to be significantly activated. The
used components are anticipated to be initially stored locally behind robust shielding. Once their
dose rates have decreased sufficiently, they would be prepared for either long -term onsite storage
or transportation to an offsite disposal facility. The most likely preparation/stabilization route
would be to first reduce the component volume by cutting or crushing it and then encapsulating it
in grout. The waste-handling facility could be vulnerable to sabotage, but the stabilized waste
forms would be both massive and robust. In general, MSRs will incorporate additional, non -
traditional fuel cycle SSCs and processes for fuel receipt and storage, processing while in use, and
waste form preparation and storage. These SSCs and processes will be important to plant security
given the increased accessibility of their materials compared to in-vessel materials. However, they
are beyond the scope of the current evaluation.
While MSRs during operation will have massive shielding in place and multiple layers of
containment, fuel salt sampling and refueling/defueling lines provide potential pathways to bypass
the barriers. Safety rules require valves on lines that penetrate con tainment, and sampling lines
will be designed to limit the quantity of material that can be removed or added. However, fuel salt
can be made much more corrosive with the addition of small quantities of material , potentially
resulting in substantial plant damage and extended shutdown. Freshly removed, used fuel salt will
be highly radioactive and could be mechanically dispersed using external energy sources.
Moreover, extended material diversion could lead to the accumulation of progressively larger
quantities of material that could be diverted from peaceful uses. Overall, barrier bypass lines will
be comparatively more vulnerable to sabotage than other areas of the plant.

5.1.4. MSR Security Risk Assessment
This section describes and summarizes the security risks of representative fast and thermal
spectrum MSRs. All nuclear reactors have the same fundamental risk : their radionuclides can be
moved from their intended locations to cause harm to the public or the environment. A second-tier
risk consideration is the potential for damage to the plant sufficient to prevent it from operating.
The quantity of radionuclides at any reactor is largely determined by the total quantity of applicable

59

material and the reactor’s operating power history. However, reactors that do not depend on
external services to achieve their safety functions are much less vulnerable to external risks.
The set of security tables and their structure has two origins : (1) major system plant layout and (2)
vulnerabilities of the current fleet. Lines and systems that bypass the normally strong lines of
defense at an MSR are a potential vulnerability not directly captured by the current security table
structure. For example, a refueling line could connect the fresh fuel storage area and the fuel in
use. Ordinarily, introducing a significant quantity of water through the refueling line would be
nearly impossible due to steam blockage developing from the first water introduced, but a
malefactor could employ a high-pressure pump to drive large quantities of water into the system.
Standard design measures like employing a small -diameter fuel salt addition line to prevent
inadvertent overfueling would partially mitigate such an attack. However, including a rupture disk
where the fuel salt line first encounters high temperature would further mitigate the attack’s
impact. Similarly, strong lines of defense would not nec essarily be in place when the plant is not
operating. The following sections contain summary security assessment rankings. The complete
security risk tables are found in Appendix A of this report. Figure 5.16 shows the relative location
of the major safety SSCs analyzed for MSRs in this report.

Fresh thermal MSR fuel is anticipated to be several hundred kilograms of 5%-enriched-uranium fuel
salt, posing a low reactivity risk. The largest security risk is associated with retention of the fuel
salt mixture within the storage canisters, which would be stored within a storage vault at the plant
site.

5.1.4.1. MSR Fast Fuel Salt Security Assessment
Fresh fast MSR fuel is anticipated to be several hundred kilograms of high-assay low-enriched
uranium (HALEU) fuel salt. As such, criticality would be of greater concern than it is for 5%-enriched
LWR fuel. An overpack would be used for storage as an engineered criticality control and to retain
any leaks.

61

5.1.4.2. MSR Fast Reactor Core Security Assessment
An MSR fast reactor core would contain significant quantities of actinides and FPs. Excess fuel salt
heat removal that does not occur during operation would be accomplished by a passive and
redundant core cooling system such as DRACS, RVACS, or PRACs, or if a drain tank was employed,
by passive cooling of the drain tank. These passive cooling systems would always be active and
require no actuation for operation, and thus their protection importance is reduced to medium. The
passive cooling paths would dispel heat to the atmosphere though chimneys on the exterior of the
reactor building, and these paths should provide robust protection against an adversary attack.
Reactivity is largely controlled in the core region through inherent reactivity feedback. Maintaining
the conditions (temperature, pressure, and fuel salt characteristics) would allow this negative
reactivity feedback to be reliable in a security event. Reactivity is also controlled in response to a
safety event using a displacement device (tungsten rod) that changes the core geometry and fissile
material content, or by using drain tanks for the fuel salt to drain into favorable -geometry storage
vessels. Either of these methods would be considered safety -related and expected to perform in
response to design basis events and would thus also require a high level of protection , as their
location is assumed to be outside the reactor cell in a separate but accessible cell. Passive
actuation of shutdown without power or I&C and redundant systems would provide robust
protection for this fundamental safety function. The passive performance of radionuclide retention
by the coolant boundary lowers the importance of protection from high to medium. The radionuclide
retention provided by the containment is the creation of a low -pressure reactor cell and thus would
require a high level of protection. The introduction of chemicals into the fuel sal t loop can cause
rapid corrosion of SSCs within the reactor, and thus the protection of penetrations to the fuel salt
loop has high importance for protection. I&C controls the operation of the plant and has the
highest qualification of importance for protection and vulnerability to adversary action. Physical or
digital interlocks to prevent negative contributions to safety would increase the robustness of the
facility to the adversarial compromise of the I&C.

5.1.4.3. MSR Thermal Core Security Assessment
An MSR thermal core largely has similar safety systems compared to a fast MSR core, with a similar
importance of protection and vulnerabilities for safety systems. Thermal MSRs differ from fast
MSRs as thermal MSRs are anticipated to add fuel to the reactor frequently to maintain the low
excess reactivity in the core. The thermal MSR fueling sys tem would be a highly important source to
protect, as it provides access to the fuel salt loop. Interlocks and limiting characteristics would
reduce or prevent the introduction of large quantities of excess fuel or compromising chemicals
into the fuel salt loop.

5.1.4.4. MSR Off-gas Initial Holdup Security Assessment
The MSR off-gas initial holdup is an in-vessel temporary storage location for off-gases collected
from the primary loop and associated SSCs. Gas is stored here for the decay heat to decrease so
that it may pass on to the secondary holdup and filter systems. The initial holdup relies on the
reactor core passive cooling systems for decay heat removal, which are anticipated to have cooling
chimneys on the exterior of the reactor building. Radionuclides are contained within the holdup
vessel, which would be within the fuel salt coolant boundary. A negative -pressure containment is
anticipated to encapsulate the reactor shell. I&C is anticipated to support the isolation of the
reactor cell, and thus it also has a high designation for importance of protection.

64

5.1.4.5. MSR Off-gas Secondary Holdup Security Assessment
The secondary off-gas holdup contains FP gases after an anticipated second holdup in the primary
off-gas holdup, which is contained within the reactor vessel for the decay of 133 Xe and 85 Kr. The
secondary holdup is anticipated to be within a cell near the reactor cell, within the containment of
the reactor. The secondary holdup is anticipated to require active cooling, even though the off-gas
will have cooled some since capture from various head spaces in the reactor systems. This active
cooling is assumed to rely on plant cooling systems (like a service water system), power, and I&C.
For this reason, the active cooling system and the support systems are given very high importance
for protection designations. A passive cooling system is anticipated to provide DID to the active off-
gas cooling system, but the timeframe associated with the efficacy of the passive system before
vessel damage may be within the window for security events. Increased passive decay heat
removal capabilities would provide greater resilience for the heat removal safety function. Similar
to the reactor coolant boundaries, the vessel, off -gas lines, and filter bank housings reliably
provide passive containment of FP gases, so the importance of protection is reduced from high to
medium. The reactor containment that provides a low -pressure reactor cell would be the primary
retention SSC should the vessel fail, and thus it has a high level of protection applied.
