5.1.4.6. MSR Used Fuel ½ Core Drain Tanks Security Assessment
Upon completion of a thermal MSR operation cycle, the fuel salt core contents are anticipated to
be drained into two drain tanks for storage until they can be removed from the site. These drain
tanks are anticipated to be deep within the reactor cell. The drain tanks would require active
cooling to maintain vessel integrity. This active cooling is assumed to rely on plant cooling systems
(like a service water system), power, and I&C. For this reason, the active cooling system and the
support systems are given very high importance for protection designations. A passive cooling
system is anticipated to provide DID to the active off-gas cooling system, but the timeframe
associated with the efficacy of the passive system before vessel damage may be within the window
for security events. Increased passive decay heat removal capabilities would provide greater
resilience for the heat removal safety function. Similar to the reactor coolant boundaries, the
vessel, off-gas lines, and filter bank housings reliably provide passive containment of FP gases;
thus, the importance of protection is reduced from high to medium. The reactor containment that
provides a low-pressure reactor cell would be the primary retention SSC should the vessel fail, and
thus it has a high level of protection applied.

5.1.4.7. MSR Vessel Waste Security Assessment
Periodically, MSR reactor vessels will defuel and the core vessel will be changed out when
degradation of the structures necessitates the change. This vessel and the associated core
internals (to include graphite) would be held onsite, likely within the reactor cell, until disposal or
site decommissioning. This vessel would be fuel -salt-wetted, activated, and possibly imbued with
FPs. The vessel material itself would be robust to the dispersion of radionuclides. Protecting the
containment barrier that creates the low-pressure reactor cell would be of high importance.
Containing the vessel within another sealed cell within the containment would further protect and
prevent the release of radionuclides.

 Reactor building 3 Low High

 Support systems None N/A N/A N/A

5.1.4.8. MSR Fuel Waste Packaging Security Assessment
Throughout the life of an MSR plant, used fuel salt will be packaged for storage or disposal. This
fuel salt will have decayed since removal from the core but will still require cooling. The fuel salt
container would require active cooling to maintain vessel integrity. This active cooling is assumed
to rely on plant cooling systems (like a service water system), power, and I&C. For this reason, the
active cooling system and the support systems are given very high importance for protection
designations. A passive cooling system is anticipated to provide DID to the active off-gas cooling
system, but the timeframe associated with the efficacy of the passive system before vessel damage
may be within the window for security events. Increased passive decay heat removal capabilities
would provide greater resilience for the heat remo val safety function. The used fuel salt may have
large quantities of actinides (as these may be the output of a breeder reactor), and thus
engineered criticality controls would be in place to prevent an excursion. The fuel salt will be in a
solid state, and packaging would occur in a sealed hot cell, which combined with the salt container
and overpack would provide the radionuclide retention during waste packaging. See Table 5.11 for
more information.

5.1.4.9. MSR Used-Fuel Liquid-Cooled Security Assessment
Packaged used fuel will likely be stored within a below -grade liquid pool for many years until
sufficient decay has occurred to facilitate long -term air cooling. This used-fuel pool will likely be
liquid salt, which would have a high-heat capacity. The pool salt is normally cooled by an active
cooling system. A passive cooling system provides DID and will remove heat from the used-fuel
pool. The high-heat capacity of the salt will mean long times to damage if cooling fails. The pool
inventory thus has a high importance for protection. The individual fuel canisters contain the used

68

fuel salt and are the primary means of preventing criticality and the release of radionuclides into
the pool. Additionally, there would be engineered criticality controls in the salt pool to prevent
misplacement during normal operations. The solid salt, container, pool salt, and the structure or
shield of the used-fuel pool provide many successive layers of DID against the release of
radionuclides. See Table 5.12 for more information.

5.1.5. MSR Security Risk Reduction and Mitigation Approaches
MSRs, and all non-LWR technologies, should incorporate features and characteristics consistent
with expectations set forth in the NRC’s Advanced Reactor Policy Statement. 6 The policy states:
 5F

 Regarding advanced reactors, the Commission expects, as a minimum, at least the
 same degree of protection of the environment and public health and safety and the
 common defense and security that is required for current generation LWRs.
 Furthermore, the Commission expects that advanced reactors will provide
 enhanced margins of safety and/or use simplified, inherent, passive, or other
 innovative means to accomplish their safety and security functions.
Security costs will be substantially influenced by MSR design choices. Modern NPPs face an
economic decision to locate critical systems partially below grade with a substantial berm or fully

6 “ Policy Statement on the Regulation of Advanced Reactors, ” NRC, 73 FR 60612, October 14, 2008.

69

below grade to improve their ability to withstand a large civilian aircraft impact, to reduce hostile
access to the parts of the facility containing significant amounts of radionuclides, and to limit the
required number of measurement points required to be monitored to demonstrate that fissile
materials have not been removed (Holcomb, Kisner, and Cetiner 2018). Open-top construction has
now become common within the nuclear industry. The comparatively easy access to the facility
during construction increases the importance of monitoring construction progress to ensure that
safety, security, and safeguards features have been installed as intended.
It is reasonable for the NRC to consider new physical security requirements for AR technologies
because the enhanced safety and security features associated with many proposed designs should
make radiological sabotage extremely unlikely. This is a result of incorporating security -by-design
into the development process for the various technologies. Any new regulations that support the
security-by-design process should be consistent with the pre sent physical security objective of
providing high assurance that activities involving special nuclear material are not inimical to
common defense and security and do not constitute an unreasonable risk to public health and
safety (NEI 2017).
Key MSR sabotage-mitigation measures include:
 • Locate as many key safety systems and support systems as possible below grade.
 • Locate radioactive systems (reactor, off -gas, polishing, etc.) beneath bio-shields or in a
 more secure location.
 • Limit radionuclide accumulation in waste process systems to reduce off site dose
 consequences and to possibly reduce source below need for protection (cannot exceed
 offsite dose value target).
 • Limit access to heavy equipment necessary to remove bio -shields (interlocks).
 • Make extensive use of guard pipes, catch pans, and guard vessels to direct fuel salt
 leakage to a collection (drain) tank.
 • Limit access to mechanisms that could enable large or powered bypass of containment
 layers (e.g., containment depressurization lines, HVAC).
 • Limit the use of water in the vicinity of any systems containing fuel salt to avoid the
 potential for steam explosion and high -pressure release of radionuclides.
 • Limit the use of combustible materials within containment (e.g., employ zeolite beds
 within the off-gas system instead of activated carbon).
 • Limit access and access points to reactor building s surrounding radioactive systems.
 • Limit access and access points to the nuclear island.
 • Strengthen aboveground structures to be robust against flood, wind, missile, and
 explosive hazards.
 • Streamline the profile of aboveground structures to be resistant to damage such as by
 using a manmade pond as the ultimate heat sink instead of air -cooled towers.

70

5.2. HIGH-TEMPERATURE GAS REACTORS
This section presents the results of characterizing security risks, consequences, and possible
mitigation approaches associated with HTGR designs, which are a subset of gas-cooled reactors
(GCRs). As shown in Figure 5.17, most GCRs are thermal-neutron reactors, though there are
modern designs of gas-cooled fast spectrum reactors, such as General Atomics’ Energy Multiplier
Module (EM2). Early thermal GCRs used air and carbon dioxide coolants and eventually evolved to
use helium coolant. The helium-cooled thermal reactors in Figure 5.17, better known as HTGRs,
have two characteristics—helium coolant and graphite moderator —that jointly offer enhanced
neutronic and thermal efficiencies and allow for the production of high-temperature nuclear heat.

Figure 5.17. Categorization of gas-cooled reactor technologies.

An overview of domestic and international HTGR designs is provided in Table 5.13, as adapted from
an IAEA ARIS booklet (IAEA 2020). The table only includes HTGR designs for commercial,
demonstration, or prototype purposes. The table excludes test reactors, such as the Dragon reactor
located in United Kingdom and the Chinese HTR -10; very high-temperature reactors, such as
ANTARES by Framatome; and microsized reactor designs, 7 such as the Micro Modular Reactor
 6F

(MMRTM) by the Ultra Safe Nuclear Corporation in the United States and the Indonesian
RDE/Micro-PeLUIt.

IAEA definition (IAEA. 2020).

Table 5.13 shows that multiple countries are actively designing or currently operating HTGRs. The
earliest HTGRs in the United States were Peach Bottom Unit 1 and Fort Saint (St.) Vrain. Both were
designed by General Atomics, constructed in the 1960s, and permanentl y shut down in 1974 and

74

1989, respectively. The modular HTGR (MHTGR) was proposed by General Atomics in 1986 and put
on hold in the 1990s, but it inspired many subsequent prismatic HTGR designs. General Atomics
also designed the steam cycle modular helium reactor (SC-MHR). It was selected as a candidate
design for the U.S. DOE next-generation nuclear plant, which was put on hold in the 2010s. The
current active U.S. HTGR designs are the Xe-100 by X-energy and SC-HTGR by Framatome.
The HTGR study described in the subsequent sections was a generic study based on the design
features of four U.S. HTGR designs, including the Xe-100, MHTGR, SC-MHR, and SC-HTGR. Although
Peach Bottom Unit 1 and Fort St. Vrain are U.S. designs, they were not considered as this study
was informed by contemporary HTGR designs with a sufficient inventory of available design
information. The study was as generic as possible referencing all the selected U.S. HTGR designs
rather than using any specific design. Thus, this section involves no proprietary data or
information, and all references come from open sources such as the IAEA ARIS (IAEA n.d.), N RC
Agencywide Documents Access and Management System (U.S. NRC 2023a), NRC online repository
for AR licensing activities (U.S. NRC 2022), and DOE Office of Scientific and Technical Informati on
website (U.S. Department of Energy n.d.).
Significant design-to-design variations among the reference designs exist regarding development
status (actively developing or put on hold), design age (legacy or modern), inventory of available
open-source information (sufficient or limited), fuel composition (uranium oxycarbide [UCO] or
uranium dioxide [UO2]), and fuel matrix design (p rismatic or pebble bed). Several principles were
followed for appropriately handling the variations and developing the generic HTGR design:
 • The generic design was developed by looking at all the reference designs, and it does not
 represent any specific design.
 • The generic design reflected modern design characteristics rather than legacy ones , unless
 there was a lack of open-source information for modern designs .
 • The generic design includes as many systems as possible, retaining a system if it was
 theoretically applicable to different designs.
Following these principles, the assumptions below were used for the generic HTGR design:
 • Is a pebble-bed HTGR that aligns with the modern HTGR development trends.
 • Uses UCO TRISO particle fuels that align with the modern HTGR development trends.
 • Uses multiple barriers to retain radionuclide release, including a fuel particle kernel, fuel
 particle coatings, a fuel-element graphite matrix, a helium pressure boundary, and a reactor
 building using a vented system.
 • Does not use a conventional pressure-retaining containment and relies on a function al
 containment system consisting of the above -mentioned barriers. This assumption was
 based on the intrinsic radionuclide retention capability of a TRISO particle fuel, which has
 multiple coating layers forming a miniature pressure vessel.

8 MW(t) and MW(e) are the sum of those from multiple reactor modules, if applicable.

9 The 238 MW of the thermal energy produced by SC-MHR was expected to be routed to the energy user as

steam. The 56 MW of the electricity was estimated to be produced by the balance of the thermal energy.

75

 • Does not use spent fuel pools and instead uses dry casks only (in line with modern HTGR
 development trends). It should be noted that dry cask storage is usually categorized as an
 independent spent fuel storage facility (ISFSI) ( U.S. NRC 2023b) and may be out of the
 assessment scope for the other reactor technologies in this report , such as MSRs and
 sodium-cooled fast reactors that use spent fuel pools as the main storage facilities.

5.2.1. HTGR Technology Description
Figure 5.18 presents a functional block diagram for a generic HTGR. Although the diagram is
intended to be complete, covering the entire nuclear fuel cycle, this HTGR study did not include
front-end activities (i.e., fuel preparation onsite and transportation) or back -end activities (i.e.,
long-term used fuel storage and fuel reprocessing). The study included reactors, major plant
systems, radioactive waste management systems, and short -term onsite spent fuel storage
facilities.

Figure 5.18. Generic high-temperature gas-cooled reactor functional block diagram.

5.2.1.1. HTGR Fuel Preparation
Fresh fuel pebble construction and testing are assumed to occur at an offsite facility and then be
transported to the site for use by the HTGR. Once transported to the site, the pebbles are assumed
to be stored before being prepared for insertion into the reactor core during refueling operations.

5.2.1.2. HTGR Heat Removal Systems
There are typically multiple heat removal systems to remove both normal and decay heat loads
from the reactor building, including the main heat transport system (HTS), the auxiliary HTS, and
the reactor cavity cooling system (RCCS).
During normal operation, heat from the reactor core is removed by the main HTS, which is a forced
cooling system that consists of a motor-driven main circulator, a steam generator, and associated
feedwater and condensate systems. Driven by the main circulator, the primary coolant (helium)
flows through the reactor core and transfers the heat to the secondary coolant (wate r) in the steam

76

generator. If the main HTS becomes unavailable, the auxiliary HTS provides a second means to
provide forced cooling using a motor-driven auxiliary circulator to maintain the helium flow and
transfer heat to service water.
The reactor core of an HTGR is protected in the reactor pressure vessel (RPV), which is typically
located in a cavity that is below grade. If neither of the HTS systems are available, heat from the
reactor core will not be effectively removed by the secondary coolant or service water; instead, the
heat will be transferred to the graphite reflector, the core barrel, the vessel, the cavity, and then to
the surrounding environment. The RCCS is a fully passive cooling system for removing heat from
the reactor cavity. The system is typically an external system outside the RPV but still within the
reactor building. The system is a continuously operating system during normal operations,
transients, and other stages. As part of the RCCS, large cooling panels are installed below grade in
the cavity between the vessel and the cavit y wall. The heat from the vessel will be first transferred
to the panels through heat radiation and convection. The heat will be further transferred from the
cooling panels to the ultimate he at sink (atmosphere) through natural convection with water or air
through inlet and outlet structures, which are typically above grade (Lisowski et al. 2021). If using
water, the water is typically from large storage tanks at a higher elevation in the reac tor building,
and it might need additional cooling such as by using air-water heat exchangers and pumps to
transfer the heat from storage tanks to the atmosphere (May et al. 2021). The cooling panels and
the corresponding inlet/outlet structures are typically designed with redundancy and operate in
parallel. Although no active components are employed in the RCCS, the system might include
passive components such as gratings and screens to prevent foreign objects from entering the
cooling passages (GA Technologies 1987a; 1987b).

5.2.1.3. HTGR Reactivity Control Systems
HTGRs have intrinsic features by design to control reactivity. The first feature is a low excess
reactivity that is ensured by the continuous fueling scheme. The other feature is the negative
temperature coefficient of reactivity to reduce reactivity when there is a temperature increase. It
should be noted that given the negative coefficient, overcooling transients can potentially lead to
an increase in reactivity. Such an impact was considered benign in the MHTGR safety risk
assessment (GA Technologies 1986c) but should be included in the security risk assessment, as
this assessment mainly considers sabotage actions, and their impacts (i.e., how much overcooling
can be intentionally achieved, how much reactivity can be added, and can the reactor can
withstand the added reactivity) can be significantly different from those resulting from random
failures.
Besides the intrinsic reactivity control features, there are typically at least two sets of neutron -
absorbing systems, which are installed in penetrations commonly in the top of the RPV (X-energy
2023). One set is the control rods that control reactor power during normal operating conditions,
including startups, shutdowns, and transients. The other set is the reserve shutdown rods or balls
that keep the reactor subcritical under accidents. Under no rmal conditions, the control rods
typically remain partially inserted, and the reserve shutdown rods/balls are held in the fully
withdrawn position. Rod positioning for both rod types is controlled by the control rod drive
mechanisms (CRDM), which are motor-driven and need a power supply. When losing power to the
CRDM motors, the control rods and the reserve shutdown rods/balls are designed to fail-safe insert
into the core under the force of gravity and place the reactor in a safe shutdown mode. The power

77

supply cutoff can be done automatically by the reactor protection system or manually by operators.
Modern HTGR designs may also propose defueling fuel pebbles as a DID option to shut down the
reactor without the use of control rods (Mulder 2021).

5.2.1.4. HTGR Radionuclide Retention
A key feature of HTGRs compared to water-cooled reactors is the use of TRISO-based pebble fuel.
Each pebble consists of thousands of TRISO-coated fuel particles embedded in a graphite matrix
formed into a sphere. As shown in Figure 5.19, a fuel kernel is encapsulated by layers of inner
pyrolytic carbon, silicon carbide, and outer pyrolytic carbon. Silicon carbide is the primary FP
barrier and serves as the load-bearing layer for TRISO particle. Water-cooled reactors usually have
a steel-reinforced concrete pressure-retaining reactor building that houses a nuclear reactor,
reactor coolant pumps, steam generator, and other equipment or piping. With its triple -coated
layers, each TRISO fuel particle comes as a miniature pressure-retaining structure and eliminates
the need for another single-walled, thick containment structure to enclose the RPV.

Figure 5.19. TRISO-coated particle fuel pebble. Source: Southern Company (2021).

78

Figure 5.20. Xe-100 radionuclide retention and release phenomena. Source: Southern Company
(2021).

79

Figure 5.21. Isometric view through the MHTGR reactor building. Source: GA Technologies (1987a;
1987b).

Figure 5.20 shows the radionuclide retention barriers of a pebble -bed HTGR. Several barriers
jointly act as a functional containment, including the fuel kernel, particle coatings (particularly
SiC), fuel-element matrix, helium pressure boundary, and reactor building. The RPV, the helium
circulator, the valves, the piping of the primary coolant loop, and the steam generator tubes are all
collectively called the helium pressure boundary.
The HTGRs have reactor buildings, but these are not as thick or as leak tight as the containment
vessels used in water-cooled reactors. Water-cooled reactors adopt high-pressure, low-leakage
reactor buildings (known as containments), and HTGRs use low -pressure, vented reactor buildings
(known as confinements). With an example provided in Figure 5.21, the HTGR reactor building
typically includes the cavities enclosing the RPV, steam generator vessel, and other volumes in the
building. There may be blowout panels and dampers fitted on the cavity vent paths. These are
typically passive equipment designed to open on overpressure, close by gravity, and close upon a
reverse of flow direction or an increase in flow (PMT Nuclear n.d.). The blowout panels and
dampers can be used to permit flow from the cavities and restrict the flows from other volumes to
the cavities, and thus maintain control of the cavity environments ( Idaho National Laboratory

80

2010). The HTGR reactor buildings can usually be vented in a controlled and filtered manner
through the building HVAC system. Unfiltered ventilation from the reactor building to the
environment may happen under pressure-relief conditions through a passive structural component
such as a building flap and dampers.

5.2.1.5. HTGR Spent Fuel Storage Systems
For economic and flexibility considerations, HTGRs usually do not have pool cooling for spent fuel
storage and instead store spent fuel pebbles in dry casks, which are sealed and filled with inert
gas. In the HTGRs, discharged fuel pebbles from the core will be placed directly into dry casks and
stored onsite. An automated system will containerize, move, and store spent fu el in spent fuel
storage buildings. There is typically enough capacity for the lifetime storage of all the used fuel
produced at the site.
An HTGR plant may design two separate spent fuel buildings, both onsite : one for interim storage
and the other for long-term storage. In the interim storage building, the dry casks may be stored
above grade. In the long-term storage building, the dry casks are usually stacked in below -grade
silos for better protection. Compared to the fuel pebbles in the reactor core subject to irradiation
and thermal challenges, the fuel pebbles in the spent fuel storage casks might be subject to
additional mechanical challenges if elevation changes are involved during cask transfer.

5.2.1.6. HTGR Radwaste Management Systems
An HTGR generates radioactive waste in solid, liquid, and gaseous form. Liquid and solid radwaste
systems are typically designed to be located in the same at-grade radwaste building, which is a
separate building outside of reactor building (i.e., HTGR confinement). Gas radwaste typically does
not have a designated building. Low-level radioactive gas waste is usually filtered, monitored, and
released directly from the reactor building through its ventilation system. High -level radioactive gas
waste is often held up to let it decay before it is released from the reactor building. The filters
installed on the reactor building will therefore contain radioactivity and will be treated as solid
radwaste, which will be transported to the radwaste system to be compacted and drummed there.

5.2.1.7. HTGR Unique Systems
Pebble-bed HTGRs adopt automated continuous fuel handling systems. Fuel pebbles are loaded
into the core while the reactor is operating through a central tube at the top of RPV. A fuel
discharge system at the bottom of the core removes spent pebbles through the bottom of the
vessel for assessment by the burnup measurement systems that evaluate each pebble for physical
damage and burnup and can return them to the top of the vesse l, where they are again loaded into
the core, or send them to spent fuel storage. Each pebble typically remains in the core for years
and is circulated through the core a few times to achieve full burnup. At full power, typically
hundreds of fresh pebbles are added daily, and a similar number are also removed as spent fuel.
HTGRs adopt helium purification systems to purify the helium stream from the primary coolant
system. The system removes chemical impurities and gas -borne activities through a series of gas -
processing equipment (filters, absorbers, oxidizers, coolers, dryers, and compressors) and
associated piping and valves. The system is typically located within a reactor building.

81

5.2.1.8. HTGR Unique Challenges
Ingress of air or water into the primary loop is a unique challenge to HTGRs. Air or water ingress
may cause graphite oxidation, and chemical corrosion of graphite and other structural materials ,
and may result in the generation of combustible gases. Ingress may also increase reactivity by
affecting neutron behaviors. The impacts are highly dependent on the ingress quantity. Given a
small amount of water or air ingress, corrosion and oxidation are typically slow and can be
tolerated and controlled. If a large ingress of air or water occurs, the reactor’s safe operation can
be challenged. For instance, if the graphite matrix of the fuel pebbles is oxidized away, the fuel
particles will be exposed and increase the likelihood of radioactivity release. Steam generator tube
rupture leading to air and water ingress is a major concern. To deal with this, an HTGR plant
typically has a steam and water dump system to dump the steam/water from the steam generator
into a dump tank, thus limiting the volume of steam/water and minimizing potential damage to the
primary loop.

5.2.1.9. HTGR Support Systems
The support systems considered in this study include power, plant cooling, and I&C. For this
assessment, the system dependency matrix is assumed and shown in Table 5.14.

5.2.2. HTGR Consequence Assessment
The risks considered in this section focus on potential sources of radioactivity and their releases
that are typically associated with HTGR designs. The safety consequence methodology described in
4.3. was used to evaluate these risks and can be summarized by the following steps:
 1. Identify radioactive sources

 2. Assess dose potential for each source

 3. Assess the dispersibility of radioactive materials for each source

 4. Assess barriers for each source that would limit the transport of radioactive material.

The specific source details for each HTGR design may vary depending on plant power rating, facility
layout, fuel subassembly handling procedures both before and after irradiation, specific system
locations and interfaces, etc. The sources considered in thi s section have typically been included in
existing HTGR designs and would be expected to be relevant to newer facilities. Due to the
qualitative nature of the current assessment, assumptions are made for the sources considered.
Assumptions relevant to the ranking process for each source are provided in the individual source
sections along with justifications for each ranking. To perform more quantitative risk consequence
assessments, specific design details for each source and facility would be required , along with
more mechanistic radioactive material release and transport evaluations.
Table 5.15 shows two types of potential radioactivity sources at the generic HTGR site.
Table 5.16 lists the MHTGR radioactive waste sources adopted from the MHTGR preliminary safety
information documents (Bechtel National, Inc. et al. 1989; 2016a; 2016b; 2016c). The table shows
that the number of radioactive waste sources can be very large, and a qual itative screening
analysis was performed to narrow down the list to focus on the more significant sources of
radioactivity to demonstrate the proposed assessment methodology.
Table 5.17 shows the HTGR radioactivity sources selected for further risks, consequences, and
mitigation approach assessments.
Table 5.15. Potential sources of radioactivity for a generic HTGR site.

NOTE: Multiple barriers, inherent or applied, results in a lower ranking. For example, the “Low” ranking is characterized
by multiple inherent or mix of inherent/applied barriers while the “High” ranking is characterized by no inherent barrier
or a single applied barrier. Section 4.3. provides a more detailed description of these rankings.

5.2.2.1. HTGR Reactor Core Consequence Assessment
The core of an HTGR contains a significant amount of radioactive materials including the fuel itself,
FPs, activated components, and activated coolant. When evaluating the maximum dose potential,
all radioactive materials are considered independent of their ability to be dispersed or the number
of barriers in place to limit their release and transport. The a ssumed maximum dose potential
ranking for an HTGR core is very high, as would be expected for any reactor technology core source.
The dispersibility ranking for an HTGR core is low because the main contributors to the maximum
84

dose potential, the fuel and FP aerosols, would require significant energy for dispersal. The barrier
ranking for the HTGR core is also low because radioactive material release and transport are
prevented by multiple inherent and applied barriers such as the fuel particle kernel, fuel particle
coatings, graphite matrix, helium coolant, helium pressure boundary, and reactor building. A radar
plot of the three rankings for the HTGR core is provided in Figure 5.22.

Figure 5.22. HTGR core source radar plot.

5.2.2.2. HTGR Spent Fuel Storage Consequence Assessment
As discussed in Section 5.2.1.5, a fuel pebble goes through the reactor core multiple times until it
reaches its burnup limit and is then sent out for storage in the onsite spent fuel dry casks. It is
assumed that this storage option at a maximum capacity would be designed to hold a plant’s
lifetime number of fuel pebbles depending on whether offsite storage options are pursued. Due to
the large number of fuel pebbles presumed to be in the casks , the maximum dose potential ranking
is very high. The dispersibility ranking for this source is assumed to be low as the main contributors
to the maximum dose potential, the fuel and FP aerosols, would require significant energy for
dispersal. The barrier ranking for the HTGR spent fuel storage is also low because radioactive
material release and transport are prevented by multiple inherent and applied barriers including
the pebbles, sealed storage casks filled with inert gas, shielding around the casks, and spent fuel
building. A radar plot of the three rankings for the HTGR spent fuel storage is provided in Figure
5.23.

85

Figure 5.23. HTGR spent fuel storage source radar plot.

5.2.2.3. HTGR Helium Purification System Consequence Assessment
As discussed in Section 5.2.1.7, the HTGR helium purification system filters and treats the helium
by removing chemical impurities and gas -borne radionuclides from the helium. For this
assessment, the major dose contributors are assumed to include iodine, bromine, strontium,
ruthenium, cesium, xenon, and krypton. For this assessment, it is conservatively assumed (GA
Technologies. 1987b.) that the helium purification system can contain on the order of 5,000 Ci of
cesium, which results in a maximum dose potential ranking of high. This assumption was made
because the greater detail on the relative ratios of elements that could be contained in this system
were not available. The radionuclides in the purification system are assumed to be liquids and
solids, resulting in more energy needed to disperse the radionuclides than if they were gaseous but
less than would be required if they were all in solid forms. This results in the dispersibility ranking
of medium. The barrier ranking for the HTGR spent fuel storage is also low because radioactive
material release and transport are prevented by multiple inherent and applied barriers , including
the purification system boundary and the reactor building. A radar plot of the three rankings for the
HTGR helium purification system is provided in Figure 5.24.

Figure 5.24. HTGR helium purification source radar plot.

86

5.2.2.4. HTGR Gaseous Radioactive Waste Consequence Assessment
Gaseous radioactive waste is produced in various locations and processes throughout the plant.
Examples include the helium purification system, instrumentation gases, the fuel storage facility
exhaust gas, etc. As previously discussed, this gaseous radioactive waste will be released from the
reactor building to the atmosphere. The main radionuclides present are assumed to be noble gases
such as xenon and krypton, which result in a low maximum dose potential ranking considering their
dose conversion factors. Since the radioactive material is in a gaseous form, it would require little
to no energy to disperse, resulting in a dispersibility ranking of high. The barrier ranking for the
HTGR gaseous waste is low because radioactive material release and transport are prevented by
multiple applied barriers, including the gas holdup tanks and the reactor building. A radar plot of
the three rankings for the HTGR gaseous radioactive waste is provided in Figure 5.25.

Figure 5.25. HTGR gaseous radioactive waste source radar plot.

5.2.2.5. HTGR Liquid Radioactive Waste Consequence Assessment
Liquid radioactive waste is produced in various locations and processes throughout the plant.
Examples include wastes from helium circulators, steam water dump tanks, cooling water drains,
personnel decontamination drains, etc. For this assessment, it is a ssumed that the liquid
radioactive waste must be sent to a radioactive waste building for containment. The liquid waste
will be transferred through pipes from the various locations to the waste building and collected in
tanks. One of the highest-activity-level radionuclides present will be cesium. It is conservatively
assumed that there will be on the order of 100 Ci of cesium from the entire inventory of liquid
radioactive waste, which results in a maximum dose potential ranking of low. The radionuclides in
the liquid waste system are in a liquid form resulting in moderate energy needed to disperse the
radionuclides and the dispersibility ranking of medium. For this assessment, it is assumed that the
liquid waste is protected by multiple barriers including the storage tanks, a shielded area that will
contain leaks, and the radioactive waste building. It is also assumed that piping going to the tanks
contains isolation valves to prevent the entire liquid waste inventory from leaking if a leak did
occur. Based on these assumptions, the barrier ranking is low. A radar plot of the three rankings
for the HTGR liquid radioactive waste is provided in Figure 5.26.

87

Figure 5.26. HTGR liquid radioactive waste source radar plot.

5.2.2.6. HTGR Solid Radioactive Waste Consequence Assessment
Solid radioactive waste is produced in various locations and processes through out the plant.
Examples include spent reflector block, spent control rods, filters, detectors, etc. Some solid waste ,
such as contaminated personal protective equipment , will be stored and transferred in containers
to the radioactive waste building for processing . Other solid waste, such as resins, will be
transferred to the radioactive waste building through piping to be collected in containers for
disposal. It is assumed that there would be significant activities associated with these materials,
and therefore the assumed maximum dose potential ranking is high. Most of the radionuclides
present in this system are in a solid state and would require significant energy for dispersal. This
system, however, does contain high-level radioactive liquid wastes that need to be solidified and
would require moderate energy for dispersal. Considering the system contains both solid and liquid
wastes, the assumed dispersibility ranking is medium. The barrier ranking for solid radioactive
waste is low because radioactive release and transport are prevented by multiple inherent and
applied barriers, including the containers, shielded areas, the radioactive waste building, etc. A
radar plot of the three rankings for the HTGR solid radioactive waste is provided in Figure 5.27.

Figure 5.27. HTGR solid radioactive waste source radar plot.

88

5.2.2.7. HTGR Fuel Handling System Consequence Assessment
The fuel handling system of an HTGR contains a small fraction of fuel pebbles compared to the
reactor core. When evaluating the maximum dose potential, all radioactive materials are
considered independent of their ability to be dispersed or the number of b arriers in place to limit
their release and transport. The assumed maximum dose potential ranking for an HTGR fuel
handling system is high. The dispersibility ranking for an HTGR fuel handling system is low because
the main contributors to the maximum dose potential, the fuel and FP aerosols, would require
significant energy for dispersal. The barrier ranking for the HTGR fuel handling system is also low
because radioactive material release and transport are prevented by multiple inherent and applied
barriers such as pebbles, the system boundary, and the reactor building. A radar plot of the three
rankings for the HTGR core is provided in Figure 5.28.

Figure 5.28. HTGR fuel handling system source radar plot.

5.2.3. HTGR Security Risk Assessment
The risks considered in this section focus on potential security concerns that are typically
associated with HTGR designs. The security risk methodology used to evaluate these risks was
described previously in section 4.3. but can be summarized in the following steps:
 1. Identify the systems associated with each safety function for each radioactive source

 2. Assess the importance of protection for each system

 3. Assess the vulnerability of each system.

The specific system details for each HTGR design may vary depending on plant layout, DID
strategies, system locations, etc. The systems associated with each radioactive source are
expected to be relevant to advanced designs. Due to the qualitative nature of the current
assessment, assumptions are made for the systems considered. To perform more quantitative
security risk assessments, specific design details for each system and facility would be required.
Below are summary security assessment rankings. The complete security risk tables are found in
Appendix A of this report. Figure 5.29 shows the relative location of the major safety SSCs analyzed
for HTGRs in this report.

89

 Rx Cavity Rx Building(s) Exterior of/to Rx Building(s)
 Rx Core Fueling System Primary Heat Transport
 Fuel Pebbles Steam Generators DRACS/RVACS/PRACS
 Helium Pressure Boundary Helium Purification AC Power
 Reactivity Control Components I&C Plant Cooling
 Pebble Handling System Gaseous Waste Reactor Building Structure
 Shutdown Balls New Fuel Spent Fuel Building
 Used Fuel Packages Liquid Waste Tanks
 Containment Solid Waste Storage
 Radwaste Building

Figure 5.29. HTGR major system locations.

5.2.3.1. HTGR Reactor Core Security Assessment
An HTGR core has many systems that support the different safety functions. For this assessment,
the generic HTGR contains the primary HTS, the auxiliary HTS, and the passive RCCS that provide
heat removal. As noted in Section 5.2.1.2, the passive RCCS can be cooled by either air or water.
For reactivity control, there are inherent reactivity feedback mechanisms, control rods, reserve
shutdown rods/balls, and defueling pebbles. The fuel pebbles, helium pressure boundary, and
reactor building support radionuclide retention. The generic HTGR contains a steam generator
steam and water dump system to minimize water/a ir ingress into the helium under accident
conditions. The support systems for the core are power, I&C, and plant cooling. Each system was
analyzed regarding the core source to determine SSC importance and vulnerability ranking. Table
5.18 shows the rankings for each of the systems.

90

Table 5.18. HTGR reactor core security assessment rankings.
 Structures, Systems, and Defense SSC SSC

 Components Line Importance Vulnerability
 Heat removal Primary heat transport system 1 Low High
 Auxiliary heat transport system 2 Low High
 Reactor cavity cooling (air cooled) 2 Medium High
 Reactor cavity cooling (water 2 High High
 cooled)
 Reactivity control Inherent reactivity feedback 1 Medium Low
 Control rods 1 Medium Medium
 Reserve shutdown rods/balls 2 High Medium
 Defueling pebbles 3 Low Medium
 Radionuclide Pebbles 1 Medium Low

 Helium pressure boundary 2 Medium High
 Reactor building 3 Low Very High
 Chemical hazards Steam generator steam and water 2 Low High
 dump
 Support systems Power 1 Low Very High
 I&C 1, 2 Medium High
 Plant cooling 1 Low Very High
NOTE: The defense line value represents the typical use of the SSC in an operational plant according to the defense line

direct importance of that SSC to the saf ety of the plant), and the SSC Vulnerability represents the relative vulnerability

Based on Table 5.18, several systems have a high or very high ranking. For heat removal systems,
the RCCS has a high SSC vulnerability ranking as it is vulnerable to direct SSC attacks with the inlet
and outlet structures for coolant above grade, penetrating the reactor building, and exposed to the
environment. The air-cooled cavity cooling system is purely passive and has no I&C involved,
making it invulnerable to indirect and cyberattacks. The water-cooled cavity cooling system,
however, has above-grade water tanks and pumps, which are potential attack targets. The pumps
are controlled by I&C, making it susceptible to indirect and cyberattacks. If adopting a water -cooled
cavity cooling system, a tradeoff during the design process must be considered; this system
enhances the cavity cooling capability but increases the system vulnerability. Regardless of coolant
selection, the vulnerability can be reduced by hardening and better shielding the above -grade
inlet/outlet structures, protecting the structures with enhanced security actions, minimizing the
number of above-grade SSCs, and minimizing the funct ion reliance on I&C. Regarding the
importance of SSC protection, the reactor cavity cooling has a high ranking if the water -cooled
design is adopted since it has a moderate reliance on the support systems , plus it is the last
credible means to removing decay heat. If the air -cooled design is adopted, the reactor cavity
cooling does not rely on the support systems at all, and the protection importance ranking will thus
be lowered from high to medium.
For reactivity control systems, the reserve shutdown rods/balls have a high SSC importance
ranking as they are the most straightforward and effective means for tripping the reactor. The
91

reserve shutdown rods/balls have a medium SSC vulnerability. They have low vulnerability to direct
SSC attacks as they are located in the reactor building. They are controlled by I&C and thus
susceptible to indirect and cyberattacks. They do not have a high cyberattack vulnerability, though,
since they are not solely controlled by CDAs, and manual trip can be used as a backup.
For radionuclide retention barriers, TRISO pebbles are the first barrier and have a medium SSC
importance ranking; due to the robustness of pebbles, they are highly resistant to radionuclide
release and have a low SSC vulnerability. The helium pressure boundary has a high SSC
vulnerability mainly because of its vulnerability to cyberattacks. The piping on the boundary can be
very robust to direct SSC attacks; however, the valves on the boundary are directly controlled by
CDAs. If the valves are intentionally opened, the helium containing radioactive materials will be
immediately released into the reactor building, leading to a loss -of-coolant challenge and mostly
unfiltered and uncontrolled releases into the atmosphere. The reactor building has an even higher
vulnerability than the helium pressure boundary as it is not designed to be as robust as the
pressure boundary, plus it is exposed to the environment. The vented paths of the reactor building
can also be manipulated through CDAs. The vulnerability can be reduced by hardening and better
shielding the vented paths of the reactor building, protecting the building with enhanced security
actions, and minimizing the function reliance on I&C. Although the reactor building has a very high
SSC vulnerability, it has a low SSC importance as the radioactive materials are supposed to be well
contained within the pebbles and helium pressure boundary. The assumption is supported by the
Fort St. Vrain operating experience and TRISO testing results performed under the DOE Advanced
Gas-cooled Reactor project.
The steam and water dump system for chemical hazard controls has a low SSC importance ranking
as it is only activated under rare steam generator tube ruptures. However, from a vulnerability
standpoint, the system has a high ranking. This is because the dum p valves are directly controlled
by CDAs. If the dump valves are intentionally opened, the secondary coolant will be lost
immediately. The vulnerability can be reduced by better protecting the dump valves, minimizing
their reliance on I&C, and introducing mitigation measures to cope with undesired steam/water
dump situations.

5.2.3.2. HTGR Spent Fuel Storage Security Assessment
HTGRs typically adopt dry cask spent fuel storage with no pool storage. An HTGR spent fuel storage
system has many systems that support the different safety functions. For this assessment, the
generic HTGR uses both active cooling and passive cooling to pr ovide heat removal. For reactivity
control, there are engineered criticality controls in the form of fuel storage racks and neutron-
absorbing spheres, as well as administrative controls for the number of fuel pebbles. The pebbles,
casks, shielding, and spent fuel building support radionuclide retention. The support systems for
the dry cask spent fuel storage pool are power, I&C, and plant cooling. Each systems was analyzed
regarding the used fuel storage pool source to determine SSC importance and vulnerability ranking.
Table 5.19 shows the ranking for each system.

92

Table 5.19. HTGR spent fuel storage security assessment rankings.
 Structures, Systems, and Defense SSC SSC

 Components Line Importance Vulnerability
 Heat removal Active cooling 1 Low High
 Passive cooling 2 High High
 Reactivity control Engineered controls 1 High Medium
 Administrative controls 1 Low Medium
 Radionuclide Pebbles 1 Medium Low

 Casks 2 Medium Low
 Shielding 2 Medium Low
 Spent fuel building 3 Low Very High

 I&C 1,2 Low High
 Plant cooling 1 Low Very High

Based on Table 5.19, passive cooling, engineered reactivity controls, and pebbles have the highest
SSC importance rankings. Passive cooling has a high SSC importance ranking since it is the last
credible means to remove heat from the silos filled with dry casks. It also has a high SSC
vulnerability ranking because this system moves the heat to the atmosphere as the ultimate heat
sink, so the air inlet and outlet structures are exposed to the environment, making the system
susceptible to direct SSC attacks. Spent fuel passive cooling and reactor cavity air -based cooling
are similar in design since they both use passive means to remove heat from silos and dump it into
atmosphere. The spent fuel storage building is also very similar to the reactor building, considering
their vented confining functions. Thus, the associated recommendations for reducing
vulnerabilities discussed in the reactor core section can be applied to the spent -fuel-related
vulnerabilities.

5.2.3.3. HTGR Helium Purification System Security Assessment
An HTGR helium purification system is connected to the primary loop and typically located in the
reactor building. The system performs multiple purifying and processing functions and requires the
helium to be heated up and cooled down multiple times using a series of heat exchangers. The
heat exchangers are not considered for this assessment, as they are designed to serve purification
needs rather than to remove heat generated from system operation. There is still a need to cool
down the system components, and such cooldown is provided by the plant component cooling
system. There are no reactivity controls with this system as the radioactivity in the helium is low
during normal operations. The system boundary and reactor building support radionuclide
retention. The support systems for the coolant purification system are power, I&C, and plant
cooling. Each system was analyzed regarding the helium purification source to determine SSC
importance and vulnerability ranking. Table 5.20 shows the ranking for each of these systems.

93

Table 5.20. HTGR helium purification system security assessment rankings.
 Structures, Systems, and Defense SSC SSC

 Components Line Importance Vulnerability
 Heat removal None N/A N/A N/A

 Radionuclide System boundary 1 Medium Low

 Reactor building 2 Low Very High

 I&C 1,2 Low High
 Plant cooling 1 Low Very High

Based on Table 5.20, none of the SSCs have a high or very high SSC importance ranking. The
reactor building has a very high SSC vulnerability ranking , which is the same as the reactor core
assessment. Therefore, the associated vulnerability insights from that section can also be applied
to the reactor building to retain gaseous radioactive waste.

5.2.3.4. HTGR Gaseous Radioactive Waste Security Assessment
HTGR gaseous radioactive waste is released from the reactor building to the atmosphere. Low -level
gaseous wastes are directly released after filtration, and the reactor building is the only retention
barrier. High-level gaseous wastes are temporarily held in tanks before being released. For this
assessment, the generic HTGR contains no heat removal and no reactivity control for gaseous
radioactive waste. The support systems for the gaseous radioactive waste are power and I&C. The
tank and the reactor building support radionuclide retention. Both systems were analyzed regarding
the gaseous radioactive waste source to determine their SSC importance and vulnerability ranking.
Table 5.21 shows the ranking for each system.

Table 5.21. HTGR gaseous radioactive waste security assessment rankings.
 Structures, Systems, and Defense SSC SSC

 Components Line Importance Vulnerability
 Heat removal None N/A N/A N/A

 Radionuclide Tanks 1 High Medium

 Reactor building 1 High Very High

 I&C 1,2 Low High

Based on Table 5.21, the tank and the reactor building have high SSC importance rankings. For
this assessment, both are assumed to be the primary system that supports radionuclide retention.
The reactor building has a high SSC importance ranking for the gaseous system and a low SSC
importance ranking for the reactor core. This is because the reactor building is the only barrier to

94

retain low-level gaseous waste, while it is the last barrier among multiple barriers (i.e., TRISO fuel
pebbles and helium pressure boundary) to retain the radioactive material from the reactor core.
Based on Table 5.21, the tank has a medium SSC vulnerability mainly due to its vulnerability to
direct SSC attacks. It is in the reactor building, which is a confinement rather than a pressure -
retaining containment. The tank relies on I&C for level indication; it is thus subject to cyberattacks,
but the consequence has a negligible safety impact. To reduce vulnerability, the tank can be
hardened, better shielded, or protected with an enhanced security level. Compared to the results in
the reactor core section, the vulnerability result of the reactor building is the same as that for the
reactor core assessment. Therefore, the associated vulnerability insights from that section can also
be applied to the reactor building to retain gaseous radioactive waste.

5.2.3.5. HTGR Liquid Radioactive Waste Security Assessment
An HTGR liquid radioactive waste system collects, treats, and stores liquid waste and is usually
located in a radwaste management building. For this assessment, the generic HTGR contains no
heat removal and no reactivity control for liquid radioactive waste. The tanks, radwaste building,
and system isolation all support radionuclide retention. The support systems for liquid radioactive
waste are power and I&C. Each system was analyzed regarding the liquid radioactive waste source
to determine SSC importance and vulnerability ranking. Table 5.22 shows the ranking for each
system.

Table 5.22. HTGR liquid radioactive waste security assessment rankings.
 Structures, Systems, and Defense SSC SSC

 Components Line Importance Vulnerability
 Heat removal None N/A N/A N/A
 Reactivity control None N/A N/A N/A
 Radionuclide Receiver tank 1 Medium High

 Discharge tank 1 Low High
 Radwaste building 2 Low Very High
 System isolation 3 Low Very High
 Support systems Power 1 Low Very High
 I&C 1,2 Low High

Based on Table 5.22, the receiver tank has a medium SSC importance ranking, and the discharge
tank has a low SSC importance ranking. For this assessment, both types of tanks are assumed to
be the primary system that supports radionuclide retention, but they contain liquid wa ste of
different radioactive levels. Receiver tanks accept the liquid wastes directly from their sources and
discharge tanks containing processed liquid wastes that have been filtered and demineralized.
Based on the table, the tanks have high SSC vulnerabi lity rankings, and the radwaste building and
system isolation have very high SSC vulnerability rankings. Both types of tank are located within
the radwaste building, making them relatively resistant to a direct SSC attack. However, each tank
has a level indicator, and each discharge tank has a sample point for evaluating the contents of
the tank and determining whether the waste can be discharged. The reliance of tanks on the I&C
95

makes them vulnerable to cyberattacks, which can lead to intentional, unexpected, and
uncontrolled liquid waste discharges.
The radwaste building is also vulnerable to cyberattacks because the building HVAC relies on the
I&C. The radwaste building in an HTGR is typically not protected in a strong containment; rather, it
is a separate building from the reactor building, making it vulnerable to direct SSC attacks. The
system isolation is in a similar situation due to its multiple targets, access points, and connections
to the reactor building. Possible ways to reduce their vulnerabilities by design include minimizing
the liquid waste system’s reliance on I&C and increasing the associated cybersecurity forces,
hardening and better shielding the tanks and the radwaste building structure and increasing the
associated security forces, and improving the redundancy and diversity of —and hardening and
better shielding—the radwaste building’s HVAC pathways. Additionally, reducing the radioactive
source term and decreasing the time that the radioactive waste is in liquid form prior to converting
the waste into a solid state can also reduce th e security rankings.

5.2.3.6. HTGR Solid Radioactive Waste Security Assessment
An HTGR solid radioactive waste system provides holdup, solidification, packaging, and storage of
solid wastes prior to their shipment off site for ultimate disposal. The system typically shares the
radwaste management building with the liquid radioactive waste system. The system accepts solid
wastes as well as the high-conductivity liquid wastes that are required to be solidified. Solid wastes
are compacted and packed in storage drums. Liquid wastes are temporarily held up in resin tanks
before being packed in drums. Drums are stored in a shielded area in the radwaste building before
shipment. For this assessment, the generic HTGR contains no heat removal and no reactivity
control for solid radioactive waste. The resin tank, storage drums , shielded area, radwaste building,
and system isolation all support radionuclide retention. The support systems for solid radioactive
waste are power and I&C. Each of these systems were analyzed regarding the solid radioactive
waste source to determine SSC importance and vulnerability ranking. Table 5.23 shows the ranking
for each system.

Table 5.23. HTGR solid radioactive waste security assessment rankings.

 Heat removal None N/A N/A N/A
 Reactivity control None N/A N/A N/A
 Radionuclide Resin tank 1 Medium Medium

 Drum 1 Medium Low
 Shielded area 1 Low Low
 Radwaste building 2 Low Very High
 System isolation 3 Low Very High
 Support systems Power 1 Low Very High
 I&C 1,2 Low High

96

Based on Table 5.23, the resin tank and drum have medium SSC importance rankings. For this
assessment, the resin tanks and drums are assumed to be the primary system that supports
radionuclide retention. Resin tanks have level indicators; thus, they are subject to cyberattacks,
but the consequence of such an attack has a negligible safety impact. The resin tanks have a
medium SSC vulnerability ranking. The vulnerability can be reduced by hardening and better
shielding the resin tanks or placing the tanks in a well -secured or shielded area. The drums and the
shielded area are well hardened and resistant to direct SSC attacks, so their vulnerabilities are
ranked low. Compared to the results in the liquid waste section, the results of the radw aste
building and system isolation are the same as those for the liquid radioactive waste assessment.
Therefore, the associated insights from that section can also be applied to solid radioactive waste .

5.2.3.7. HTGR Fuel Handling System Security Assessment
An HTGR fuel handling system is an autonomous system that provides continuous defueling and
refueling based mainly on the fuel’s gravity flowing and pneumatic loading. The system is
connected to the reactor core and located in the reactor building. The system typically uses helium
for pneumatic loading. Depending on the design, the system might extract helium fr om the primary
loop and might need a heat exchanger to cool down the helium first. Such a heat exchanger is not
considered for this assessment, as it is designed to serve fuel handling rather than remove heat
generated from system operation. The plant cooling system cools down the system components.
There are no reactivity controls with this system. The system boundary and reactor building support
radionuclide retention. The support systems for the coolan t purification system are power, I&C, and
plant cooling. Each system was analyzed to determine SSC importance and vulnerability ranking.
Table 5.24 shows the ranking for each system.

Table 5.24. HTGR fuel handling system security assessment rankings.
 Structures, Systems, and Defense SSC SSC

 Components Line Importance Vulnerability
 Heat removal None N/A N/A N/A

 Radionuclide System boundary 1 Medium Low

 Reactor building 3 Low Very High

 I&C 1,2 Low High
 Plant cooling 1 Low Very High

Based on Table 5.24, none of the SSCs have a high or very high SSC importance ranking. The
reactor building has a very high SSC vulnerability ranking. This result is the same as it was for the
reactor core assessment. Therefore, the associated vulnerability insights from that section can also
be applied to the reactor building to retain gaseous radioactive waste.

97

5.2.4. HTGR Risk Reduction and Mitigation Approaches
Based on the security risk assessment, several risk reduction and mitigation approaches have
been identified for the generic HTGR. The details of these are discussed in the sections below.

5.2.4.1. Enhancing Designs of the SSCs with the Highest Importance Rankings
DID has been well implemented in the generic HTGR safety system designs. But no matter how
many levels of redundancy there are in place, there will always be SSCs for each safety function
that play a leading role in the response to off-normal conditions. Using the reactor core as an
example, the SSCs include the RCCS for heat removal, the reserve shutdown rods/balls for
reactivity control, and the TRISO fuel pebbles for radionuclide retention. The se leading SSCs
usually have the highest SSC importance rankings . SSeBD or SeBD could be considered to both
reduce the importance rankings and the vulnerability rankings for the se SSCs. Reducing the
reliance on support systems and increasing the levels of intrasystem redundancy can help reduce
the SSC importance rankings. Adopting more robust designs and introducing enhanced protections
could help reduce SSC vulnerability rankings. The generic HTGR already has some SSeBD features
in place, such as the passive cooling design minimizing the reliance on a support system, and the
TRISO pebbles that may have great strength and resilience to direct SSC attacks. More SSeBD
recommendations that could be added to the HTGRs are discussed in the remainder of this section.

5.2.4.2. Enhancing Designs of the SSCs Located Above Grade or Exposed to the
 Environment
The safety systems of the generic HTGR have been largely placed below grade. However, some
SSCs are inevitably located above grade or exposed directly to the environment, especially for the
generic HTGR that uses atmosphere as the ultimate heat sink. Examp les of the SSCs include the
inlet/outlet structures of the reactive cavity cooling system, the reactor building, the spent fuel
building, and the radwaste building. General SeBD recommendations for the structures and
buildings include minimizing the number of above-grade or exposed SSCs, hardening and better
shielding the exposed structures and vented paths, protecting the buildings with enhanced security
actions, and minimizing function reliance on the I&C.
It is recommended that the HTGR reactor building is prioritized for SeBD considerations for several
reasons. It is a confinement rather than a containment. It is not as thick or as leak tight as the
classical containment vessels used in water-cooled reactors. It houses multiple radioactivity
sources including the reactor core, the helium purification system, the gaseous radioactive waste
system, and the fuel handling system.

5.2.4.3. Enhancing Designs of the Support Systems
The support systems considered for the generic HTGR include power, plant cooling, and I&C. The
generic HTGR feature of passive cooling reduces the reliance on the power by a large extent. The
reliance of the HTGR safety systems on the plant cooling and I&C is still fairly high.
As shown in Table 5.14, the plant cooling system for the generic HTGR serves the safety systems
protecting almost all the radioactive sources, including the primary and auxiliary heat transport
systems, control rods and reserve shutdown rods/balls, active trains of the spent fu el storage
cooling, fuel handling system, and helium purification systems. The cooling systems are mostly
located outside of the reactor building and are vulnerable to direct SSC attacks. Hardening and
98

better shielding the plant’s cooling reservoirs and minimizing its reliance on the I&C could reduce
the vulnerability ranking. Increasing the number and design diversity of the plant ’s cooling
reservoirs can meet the single failure design criterion and could mitigate the consequence if a
single reservoir is lost.
As shown in the table, the I&C serves all the safety systems except for the passive SSCs. Better
cybersecurity protection could be provided as well to reduce the I&C system’s vulnerability to
cyberattacks. The I&C system, especially the software and control -function related aspects, could
be better designed to eliminate common cause failure potentials that could allow a single
cyberattack to propagate to multiple controlling f unctions. Additional redundant trains and the
application of security controls on I&C could lower the security risk associated with this SSC. The
resilience of the I&C system under the cyberattacks could be improved to ensure a timely function
recovery from cyberattacks and to mitigate the attack consequences.

5.2.4.4. Balancing Safety and Security Considerations in the Design Process
From this assessment, it was observed that a design decision does not necessarily enhance both
safety and security; sometimes, it could even lead to contradictory impacts. One example,
introduced in Section 5.2.1.2, is the adoption of water-cooled reactor cavity cooling; it provides
better cooling capability (improving safety) but is more vulnerable to an adversary attack (impairing
security). Adoption of digital I&C to replace analog I&C is a common trend for ARs, and is another
example. Digital I&C has lower component failure rates (improving safety) but is more vulnerable to
cyberattacks (impairing security). The safety and security trade -offs need to be kept in mind and
appropriately handled during the design process.
