5.4. SODIUM-FAST REACTORS
SFRs use sodium as the primary system coolant to transfer heat from the reactor core to a primary
heat exchanger (HX). The HX transfers heat from the primary system to an intermediate coolant,
which has historically been sodium but can be other fluids such as molten salts. The heat from the
intermediate loop can then be used to produce electricity. Historically, two types of SFR plant
configurations have been considered: loop-type plants and pool-type plants. These configuration
types refer to the layout of the primary system. In a loop-type SFR configuration, the primary HX is
located outside the reactor vessel, and primary system sodium is routed to the HX via primary
system loop components, such as piping, pumps, etc. In a pool-type SFR configuration, the majority
or all the primary system sodium components are located inside the reactor vessel. Depending on
the reactor design, the primary sodium processing system can be located in -vessel or ex-vessel. In
addition to the plant configuration, SFRs can also be subdivided based on fuel type: metal fuel vs .
oxide fuel. Even though different SFR plant configurations and fuel types are possible, active U .S.-
based concepts are pool-type, metal-fuel SFRs, therefore that type will be the focus of the
information provided here. A list of known active U.S. SFR vendors along with details of their
proposed designs are provided in Table 5.25.

Table 5.25. U.S. SFR vendor/design details.
 Vendor Reactor Configuration Fuel MWe
 TerraPower Natrium Pool Metal 345
 GEH PRISM Pool Metal 165 or 311
 ARC Clean Technology Inc. ARC-100 Pool Metal 100
 Oklo Inc. Aurora* Pool Metal -
*Aurora refers to Oklo’s series of SFR designs. Specific names and reactor size information are not publicly available.

The advantages of SFRs compared to traditional LWRs include:
 • Heat transfer of sodium coolant is approximately 100 times more effective compared to
 water
 • Sodium coolant has a relatively wide margin to boiling
 • Sodium coolant is compatible with structural components and metallic fuels
 • The primary and intermediate coolant systems are operated near atmospheric pressures
 and typically have multiple physical barriers to contain coolant leaks and maintain coolant
 inventory, which alleviates loss-of-coolant concerns
 • Net negative reactivity feedback provides inherent reactivity control
 • have the ability to rely solely on independent and diverse passive modes of decay heat
 removal.
For the analysis provided in subsequent sections, a generic SFR functional diagram was developed
and is provided in Figure 5.30. For the generic facility, it is assumed that fuel preparation is
performed offsite and transported to the facility where it is stored before being used in the reactor.
The generic reactor design is assumed to include an online ex -vessel primary sodium processing
system and an online ex-vessel cover gas cleanup system (CGCS). The used fuel from the reactor is

101

assumed to be treated and then moved into short -term storage before ultimately being placed in
long-term storage. It should be noted that irradiated fuel in this section is referred to as “used
fuel” to indicate the possibility of the fuel being recycled and reused. SFR fuel recycling and reuse
are not specifically described in this section , nor are facilities required to do so. For the discussion
and analysis here, a once-through fuel cycle is assumed.

Figure 5.30. Generic SFR functional block diagram.

5.4.1. SFR Areas and Systems/Processes Descriptions
This section will provide an overview of the major areas identified in Figure 5.30 and descriptions
of relevant systems and processes in those areas. These descriptions are based on historical
designs of SFRs and design features common in modern SFRs. Depending on the specific design of
the facility, some of the information provided below may not be relevant. For example, Section
5.4.1.8 provides details for storing used subassemblies in a sodium vessel outside of the reactor
vessel to cool before being treated to remove residual sodium from the exterior of the
subassembly. All SFR designs may not use this storage strategy, depending on the heat load of the
subassemblies being removed from the reactor vessel. Some SFR designs have proposed storing
used subassemblies in the reactor vessel itself but away from the active core region for cooling.
Whether additional cooling time is required or not depends on how long the used subassemblies
can be stored in the reactor vessel before being removed for treatment.

5.4.1.1. SFR Fuel Subassemblies—Front End
Fresh fuel subassembly construction and testing are assumed to occur at an offsite facility before
the subassembly is transported to the site for use by the SFR. Once transported to the site,
subassemblies are assumed to be stored before being prepared for insertion into the reactor core
during refueling operations.

102

5.4.1.1.1 Offsite Fuel Subassembly Construction, Testing, and
 Transportation
Offsite fuel preparation would occur at a separate facility from the SFR site. Transportation
between the fuel subassembly construction and testing site and the SFR site would be governed by
regulatory requirements, such as the Code of Federal Regulations in the United States. The
sabotage risk of fuel preparation at an offsite NRC -licensed facility and the subsequent
transportation risk of licensed material over public roads is the subject of separate studies and is
beyond the scope of this review.
5.4.1.1.2 Storage and Preparation
Fresh fuel subassemblies are assumed to be stored on the site prior to being inserted into the
reactor core. The subassemblies are likely to be stored in an inert environment where they can be
heated and flow tested. The subassemblies would then be transported from the storage area to the
reactor vessel in inerted casks that would connect to the reactor vessel head such that the
subassemblies can be moved from the cask into the core region. The number of subassemblies
stored in the storage area at any given time and the number of subassemblies moved between the
storage area and the reactor at a time depend s on the size of the reactor (number of
subassemblies being replaced per refueling operation) and the design of the cask utilized. The
attractiveness and severity of sabotage directed at the storage area and/or during preparation
activities would depend on the size and specific content of the storage area and cask as well as
the accessibility to these areas.

5.4.1.2. SFR Core Sources
A pool-type SFR primary system consists of the core, heat exchangers that transfer heat from the
primary sodium to the intermediate coolant loop, and pumps. The pumps circulate primary sodium
from the cold sodium pool through the reactor core to the hot so dium pool before passing through
the heat exchanger and returning to the cold sodium pool. In pool -type designs, all these
components are contained within the reactor vessel. This section will focus on reactor core sources
and vulnerabilities, while subsequent sections will focus on heat removal systems and primary
system support systems such as the CGCS and the primary sodium purification system.
While the reactor core contains a significant amount of radioactivity compared to other locations at
a typical SFR facility, it also has several barriers to radiological release that limit the attractiveness
of the core as a target for sabotage. Barriers t o radiological release from the core region can be
seen in Figure 5.31. The first barrier to release is the fuel matrix and fuel pin cladding. If that
barrier were to fail, the sodium coolant itself acts as a barrier to radionuclide transport and release
via various retention mechanisms. For radionuclides that are not retain ed in the sodium coolant,
for example gaseous FPs, the next barrier is the reactor vessel and head. If radionuclides were able
to transport outside of the reactor vessel and head, the containment structure (if the specific
design includes a containment) would act as a barrier to release. Additional path ways for release
from the reactor vessel include subsystems such as the primary sodium purification system and the
CGCS. However, both of those systems typically include isolation capabilities from the reacto r
vessel. These systems will be described in more detail in subsequent sections.

103

Figure 5.31. SFR core radiological source with transport and retention barriers . Source: Grabaskas
et al. (2015).

5.4.1.3. SFR Heat Removal
There are typically multiple systems to remove heat from the reactor during normal operations,
transient conditions, and shutdown.
5.4.1.3.1 Reactor Normal Operation Heat Removal
As described above, during normal operation heat from the reactor core is transferred to the
primary sodium coolant and then to the intermediate heat transport loop. The coolant in the
intermediate loop is typically sodium or salt. Heat from the intermedia te loop is typically passed to
a tertiary loop for electric power generation. Some designs have also proposed passing excess heat
from the intermediate loop to an energy storage system where it could be utilized later. Since the
information available on normal-operation heat removal systems is very general, opportunities for
sabotage and potential mitigation measures are not included here.
5.4.1.3.2 Reactor Decay Heat Removal
SFRs typically have multiple means for removing decay heat from the primary system. The primary
means of heat removal would be via systems associated with normal operational heat removal ,
which include the intermediate coolant loop and other balance of plant systems. In addition to this,
both active and passive systems may be available for heat rejection directly from the primary
system via an RVACS or DRACS. An RVACS is used to remove decay heat by passively transferring it
via radiation and convection from the exterior surface of the reactor guard vessel to a natural -draft-
driven cooling system. Alternatively, a DRACS can remove decay heat directly from the SFR primary
coolant by inserting a natural-circulation-driven decay heat removal loop into the sodium pool.
While an RVACS is typically a completely passive system, both active and passive DRACS designs
have been developed, including designs that can operate in both modes.

104

Practical heat rejection to air in an RVACS or DRACS generally employs a structural chimney to
drive natural air circulation past an air draft heat exchanger. While a substantial portion of an SFR
structure may be located below grade, the cooling chimneys must extend above grade to provide
adequate heat removal. Severe natural phenomena , such as tornadoes, floods, and earthquakes,
and airborne missiles can damage aboveground SSCs. Therefore, SFR cooling chimneys must be
mechanically robust enough to withstand such natural phenomena. In addition, large civilian
aircraft impacts, especially engine impacts, could damage or destroy even a reasonably robust
chimney or cooling tower.
Sabotage actions have the potential to mimic any of these external threats to the decay heat
removal system. Security-by-design and DID can include the principles of independence and
redundancy to limit the loss of multiple decay heat removal systems. Adequate separation between
redundant heat exchangers or chimneys could prevent a single sabotage action from affecting all
decay heat rejection simultaneously. Flooding mitigation can also be implemented by hardening air -
based natural circulation heat rejection systems against flooding by locating the air contact heat
exchangers in watertight compartments , and by elevating both the air inlet and outlet well above
the height of any credible flood.

5.4.1.4. SFR Primary Sodium Purification System
U.S. experience with pool-type SFRs demonstrates that sodium activation (particularly 24 Na)
dominates primary sodium radioactivity during operation (Grabaskas et al. 2015). However, its
short half-life means that it quickly decays after shutdown. Fuel pin breaches aside, tritium and
22 Nabecome the next major sources, with radionuclides from corrosion products and sodium

impurities likely falling at lower levels. Any fuel pin breaches will likely result in the presence of
iodine and cesium in the primary system due to their high solubility in sodium. Primary sodium
purification systems are used to reduce oxygen and hydrogen impurities that would normally
corrode (and therefore contaminate) primary system components. While these systems are
designed for this reason, they can also assist in reducing other radionuclides that are present
within the primary circuit. Cold traps are the most common sodium cleanup method; they cool the
sodium below the saturation temperature of the impurities, allowing them to crystal lize on the walls
of the cold trap vessel or on a stainless steel mesh within the trap. To reduce the amount of
cesium available to transport to the cover gas region of an SFR, cesium traps are typically installed
as part of the primary sodium purification sys tem.
The location of the major components of the primary sodium purification system vary by SFR
design, but typically they are located ex-vessel but within a vault inside containment , as shown in
Figure 5.32. The vault may also be inerted to minimize sodium oxidation in leak scenarios. Designs
for in-vessel primary sodium purification systems have been explored but likely need additional
R&D to be feasible. The quantity of radionuclides contained in the cesiu m trap and cold trap is
dependent on the reactor operational history, the number of fuel pins that have failed over the
lifetime of the facility, and the size of each component (some designs have components designed
to last for the reactor’s lifetime, while others are designed to be replaced). Both the cesium trap
and cold trap will have cooling systems to cool the sodium passing through to be optimal
temperatures for purification.
The most severe primary sodium cleanup system incident, from a radiological release perspective,
is likely a breach in the cesium or cold traps followed by a sodium fire. Both traps are expected to
105

be located in a room or vault designed to contain sodium fires (airtight to limit oxygen with steel -
lined floors and walls). The fire could potentially vaporize radionuclides found in both traps, but the
extent of the fire and radionuclide release is highl y dependent on the size/design of the traps and
the room they are in. Since primary sodium cleanup systems have historically been located within
the upper containment, another barrier to environmental release is provided.
Sabotage concerns associated with the sodium purification system include intentionally failing
structural materials of the major components or piping of the system. Depending on the location of
the failure, primary sodium leaking out could burn , which would provide additional energy to drive
the release of the radionuclides contained in the primary sodium. If the cooling systems of the
cesium trap and/or cold trap were disabled, additional radionuclides would be reintroduced back
into the primary sodium from the traps and then could be transported outside the system if a
structure failure was induced. Barriers to the release of radionuclides from this system include the
components themselves (vessels, pipe walls, guard pipes, etc.), the vault, and containment.

Figure 5.32. Diagram of an example SFR primary sodium purification system . Source: Grabaskas et
al. (2015).

5.4.1.5. SFR Cover Gas Cleanup System
There are several sources of radionuclides found in the cover gas region of a pool -type SFR.
Sources of varying importance include activation of the cover gas itself, migration of tritium into
the cover space, release of decay products from activated sodiu m, and noble gases from fuel pins
that have failed during normal operation. The radionuclides of the cover gas region are dominated
by 23 Ne. However, its short half-life (38 s) leaves activated argon (if argon is used as the cover gas)
and the noble gases from failed fuel pins (Xe and Kr) as the most active radionuclides.
CGCSs are not only used to remove impurities from the cover gas region but can also be used to
identify failed fuel pins through gas -tagging. For cleanup systems that use a cryogenic distillation
106

column, radioactive FPs are retained in the column, which is placed inside a cold box and usually
located outside of containment, as shown in Figure 5.33. The cold box is designed to be capable of
retaining the radioactive gas inventory in the event of a leak.
The most severe CGCS incident is likely a rupture of the cryogenic distillation column coupled with
a loss of cooling. This would result in the revaporization of the noble gases stored inside. The
inventory of radionuclides would depend on the number of structural pin failure s and the decay
time. Since the cleanup system would likely be outside the containment, there are few engineered
barriers to release. However, the short half -lives of many of the noble gas radionuclides may
greatly reduce the release.
Sabotage concerns associated with the CGCS include intentionally failing structural materials of
the major components or piping of the system. Depending on the location of the failure,
radionuclide gases contained in the system could be released. If associated cooling systems were
disabled, additional gaseous radionuclides would be reintroduced back into the system from the
cryogenic distillation column and then could be transported outside the system if a structure
failure were induced. Barriers to the release of radionuclides from this system include the
components themselves (vessels, pipe walls, guard pipes, etc.) and the cold box.

Figure 5.33. Diagram of an example SFR cover gas cleanup system. Source: Grabaskas et al.
(2015).

5.4.1.6. SFR Used Fuel Handling
After fueled subassemblies are removed from the reactor core, they can be transported in either
gas-cooled moving casks or sodium-pot moving casks to treatment cells or storage pools. Incident
scenarios during transport for either type of cask present different challenges. For gas -cooled cask
moving incidents, the concern is that some of the spent fuel’s fission gases, including the noble
gases, would be released if the cask barrier was breached, cooling capability was lost, and fuel pin
107

failure were to occur. A sodium-pot moving cask incident could present a more serious challenge if
a breach large enough to result in a sodium fire occurred. The sodium fire could potentially result
in larger releases from the spent fuel, but this scenario is heavily dependent on design and should
be addressed by a vendor pursuing the sodium-pot moving cask option. Both accidents could occur
outside of containment, as spent fuel is moved to short - or long-term storage areas. Sabotage
concerns during fuel subassembly movements include disabling cask cooling systems and/or
intentionally failing the structural integrity of the casks.

5.4.1.7. SFR Used Fuel Treatment
After fueled subassemblies are removed from the reactor core, they can be stored in designated
locations in the reactor vessel; if the SFR design incorporates in-vessel storage, they can be
removed and transported to another sodium vessel or pool, or they can be processed for short -term
storage in water or dry casks. Before the subassemblies can be stored in water or air, they must be
cleaned to remove any sodium residue after draining and blowdown with argon. Sodium cleaning
would be performed in a dedicat ed space capable of handling the effects of any sodium reactions
with air and moisture and rejecting residual heat generated by the subassemblies. Heat rejection in
the cleaning process is typically done via a recirculating inert gas (argon) cooling system that
transfers heat away from the subassemblies and to other systems to be dissipated. Back cooling
systems may include options for flooding the assemblies with liquid metals to increase heat
transfer from the subassemblies to the other structures or syst ems before the heat is dissipated.
The cleaning process is typically initiated by introducing humidified air to the recirculating inert gas
coolant. The amount of humidified air and its moisture content is increased gradually while the rate
of reaction with residual sodium is monitored. Onc e the residual sodium has been sufficiently
removed from a subassembly, purified water is circulated through it to further ensure that it is
clean, and then it is dried via air circulation.
Possible vulnerabilities exist in this area in that the coolant systems for the cask and/or cleaning
station could be disabled, leading the subassembly to heat up and fail. Additionally, the moisture
content of the humidified air could be increased too soon, or the water flushing could be
introduced before enough sodium in a subassembly has been reacted , causing an energetic
sodium-water reaction that could fail the subassembly. Depending on the design of the facility and
the specific location of the cleani ng station, additional barriers may be available to deal with any
radiological releases from failed subassemblies , or there may be additional backup cooling
systems.

5.4.1.8. SFR Used Fuel Storage
The interim storage of SFR used fuel subassemblies onsite can typically be in either a sodium pool
within a sealed vessel or in an open-water pool. Some reactors have proposed storing used fuel
assemblies inside the reactor vessel but outside the active core region where they can cool before
being removed and treated. Once subassemblies are removed from the reactor vessel, they may be
stored in a separate vessel containing sodium specifically designed for interim cooling. This
storage option would consist of components/systems such as: a vessel and guard vessel, vessel
internals designed to hold the subassemblies in place, sodium coolant, an inert cover gas, cooling
systems that may include both active and passive cooling options, and sodium and cover gas
purification/monitoring equipment.

108

For longer interim storage periods, and following a treatment where residual sodium is removed
from the external surfaces of subassemblies, storage in larger open-water pools, similar to LWR
spent fuel pools, is possible. Subassemblies stored in open -water pools may be contained within
subassembly canisters designed to (1) contain any releases from failed fuel pins , preventing the
larger water pool from being contaminated, and to (2) limit and contain reactions of bond sodium
with air or water if the fuel pins contain bond sodium. Major components/systems of an open -water
pool include the pool structures, such as the walls and floor, subassembly racks, the water coolant
along with purification/monitoring systems, and the cooling systems, which may include both active
and passive cooling system options.

5.4.1.9. Sodium Reactions with Air or Water
Additional factors that can contribute to increased radionuclide release are associated sodium
coolant reactions with air or water. At normal plant operating temperatures, sodium exposed to
oxygen will react exothermically via combustion. More energetic reactions are possible if sodium
interacts with water. During either reaction, FPs or activated sodium can be released if they are
present. The reactions themselves can also provide energy to enhance the release and transport of
FPs. SFR designs typically limit co-locating sodium and water sources or ensure that multiple
barriers are present to prevent sodium -water interactions. Eliminating the possibility of sodium -air
reactions is difficult without inerting compartments , which house sodium systems and components.
For this reason, sodium fire protection systems (SFPSs) and strategies are incorporated into SFR
designs to detect sodium leaks, protect plan personnel, limit the amount of sodium that can be
released, limit the combustion of any sodium that is released, protect SSCs important to plant
safety, handle sodium combustion byproducts, and provide facility asset protection.

5.4.2. SFR Consequence Assessment
The risks considered in this section focus on potential sources of radioactivity that are typically
associated with SFR designs and their potential for release. The safety consequence methodology
used to evaluate these risks is described in Section 4.3. but can summarized by the following
steps:
 1. Identify radioactive sources
 2. Assess dose potential for each source
 3. Assess the dispersibility of radioactive materials for each source
 4. Assess barriers for each source that would limit the transport of radioactive material .
The specific source details for each SFR design may vary depending on plant power rating, facility
layout, fuel subassembly handling procedures both before and after irradiation, specific system
locations and interfaces, etc. The sources considered in this section have typically been included in
historic SFR designs and would be expected to be relevant to newer facilities. Due to the
qualitative nature of the current assessment, assumptions are made for the sources considered.
Assumptions relevant to the ranking process for each source are provided in the individual source
sections along with justifications for each ranking. To perform more quantitative risk consequence
assessments, specific design details for each source and facility would be required , along with
more mechanistic radioactive material release and transport evaluations. Table 5.26 provides a
summary of the SFR sources considered.

109

Table 5.26. Summary of SFR risk consequence rankings for various sources .
 Reactor Maximum Dose Dispersibility Barrier
 Source
 Type Potential Ranking Ranking Ranking 1
 Core Very High Low Low
 Cesium trap Medium Medium Low
 Sodium cold trap Medium Medium Low
 SFR Noble gas decay tanks Low High High
 Used subassembly wash station High Medium Low
 Used fuel storage pool (Na) Very High Low Low
 Used fuel storage pool (H 2 O) Very High Low Medium
1. Multiple barriers, inherent or applied, results in a lower ranking. For example, the “Low” ranking is characterized by
multiple inherent or mix of inherent/applied barriers , while the “High” ranking is characterized by no inherent barrier or
a single applied barrier. Section 4.3. provides a more detailed description of these rankings.

5.4.2.1. SFR Core Consequence Assessment
The core of an SFR contains a significant amount of radioactive material including the fuel itself,
FPs, activated components, and activated coolant. When evaluating the maximum dose potential,
all radioactive materials are considered independent of their ability to be dispersed or the number
of barriers in place to limit their release and transport. The a ssumed maximum dose potential
ranking for an SFR core is very high, as would be expected for any reactor technology core source.
The dispersibility ranking for an SFR core is low because the main contributors to the maximum
dose potential, the fuel and FP aerosols, would require significant energy for dispersal. The barrier
ranking for the SFR core is also low because radioactive material release and transport is
prevented by multiple inherent and applied barriers such as the fuel matrix, cladding, sodium
coolant, reactor vessel, guard vessel, and facility structures. A radar plot of the three rankings for
the SFR core is provided in Figure 5.34.

Figure 5.34. SFR core source radar plot.

110

5.4.2.2. SFR Cesium Trap Consequence Assessment
Anticipated fuel pin cladding failures during normal operations are the source of cesium in the
primary sodium coolant. The estimated number of failed fuel pins during the lifetime of the plant
has uncertainty due to cladding failure mechanisms, material and welding quality assurance,
whether new fuel types and cladding will b e tested in the reactor, if used fuel subassemblies are
temporarily stored in-vessel or ex-vessel, and other considerations. For this assessment, it is
assumed that the primary sodium purification system cesium trap would be designed to last the
plant’s lifetime, resulting in its cesium capacity being significantly higher than what would be
expected to be removed from the primary sodium during the plant ’s lifetime. The assumed large
cesium maximum inventory (~4,000 Ci) results in a maximum dose potential ranking of medium for
the cesium trap. Any cesium released from the trap would be in the form of an aerosol , which
would require energy to initiate the release and provide a driving force for dispersal. It should be
noted that the energy resulting from sodium reactions with air are a possible driving force to
enhance cesium transport in scenarios where cesium and sodium are released from the trap if it is
not located in an inerted environm ent. Without such an energy source, cesium aerosol transport
would be limited, and therefore the dispersibility ranking for the cesium trap is medium. The barrier
ranking for the cesium trap is low because it is assumed that the cesium trap would have mult iple
physical barriers to prevent release such as the cesium trap vessel and possibly a secondary or
guard vessel. The major components of the primary sodium purification system are also likely to be
contained inside of a sealed (possibly inerted) vault in the reactor building or in some d esigns in
the reactor vessel. A radar plot of the three rankings for the cesium trap system is shown in Figure
5.35.

Figure 5.35. SFR cesium trap source radar plot.

5.4.2.3. SFR Cold Trap Consequence Assessment
During normal operation, corrosion products are removed from the primary sodium and stored in
the primary sodium purification cold trap. These corrosion products can be activated , and along
with other impurities, such as tritium , which is also removed from the primary sodium and held up
in the cold trap, constitute most of the radionuclides in this component. The primary sodium itself,
flowing through the trap and likely to include activated sodium, is an additional source of
radioactivity. For this assessment, it is assumed that the primary sodium purification system cold
111

trap would be designed to last the plant ’s lifetime, resulting in its radionuclide capacity being
significantly higher than what would be expected to be removed from the primary sodium during
the plant’s lifetime. It is also assumed that the primary sodium purification system would include a
separate cesium trap, without which cesium would also need to be accounted for in the cold trap.
Being a plant lifetime component, and based on the types of radionuclides present in the cold trap,
an assumed maximum dose potential ranking of medium is assigned. Radionuclides released from
the cold trap would be in the form of aerosols , which would require energy to initiate the release
and provide a driving force for dispersal. It should be noted that the energy resulting from sodium
reactions with air are a possible driving force to enhance aerosol transport in scenarios where
sodium is released from the trap if it is not located in an inerted environment. Without such an
energy source, the transport distance of those ae rosols would be limited, and therefore the
dispersibility ranking for the cold trap is medium. The barrier ranking for the cold trap is low
because it is assumed that it would have multiple physical barriers to prevent release such as the
cold trap vessel and possibly a secondary or guard vessel. The major components of the primary
sodium purification system are also likely to be contained inside of a sealed (possibly inerted) vault
in the reactor building or in some des igns in the reactor vessel. A radar plot of the three rankings
for the primary sodium purification system cold trap is provided in Figure 5.36.

Figure 5.36. SFR cold trap source radar plot.

5.4.2.4. SFR Noble Gas Decay Tanks Consequence Assessment
The main purposes of the CGCS is to remove radionuclides from the cover gas region to avoid
radionuclides from leaking into containment, facilitate maintenance, and identify ruptured fuel pins
using a gas-tagging system. The removed radionuclides are assumed to be stored in decay tanks
such as the cryogenic distillation column described in Section 5.4.1.5. Like the primary sodium
cleanup system, there are varying designs, with some SFRs using a continuous operation strategy
and others cleaning the bulk cover gas during shutdown. The inventory of radionuclides in this
system, which include xenon and krypton from failed fuel pins, activated argon, and radioactive
neon (a decay product of activated sodium), would depend on the number of fuel pin f ailures
during operation and the decay time. A rupture to this system, which would result in an increase in
temperature and vaporization of the noble gases, would likely lead to a release of most of the
distillation column inventory. Based on the radionuclides present in the system and their potential

112

dose impact, a ranking of low is assigned for the maximum dose potential. The released
radionuclides would be noble gases, which have extremely high vapor pressures and are essentially
unreactive with other elements/substances . Such a release would require little to no additional
energy to be dispersed; therefore, the dispersibility ranking is high. While the distillation column
may be located within a cold box, some leakage could occur even if the cold box functions as
designed. Since the system can be located outside of containment i n some designs, there would
likely be no additional barriers of sufficient resistance to impede the release of the noble gases to
the environment. This results in a barrier ranking of “ high.” A radar plot of the three rankings for
the primary CGCS noble gas decay tank is provided in Figure 5.37.

Figure 5.37. SFR noble gas decay tank source radar plot .

5.4.2.5. SFR Used Subassembly Wash Station Consequence Assessment
The used fuel subassembly wash station, where residual sodium is removed from subassemblies
prior to additional treatment or storage, is assumed to contain a single subassembly at a time. The
subassembly is assumed to be contained within a cask used to tra nsport it from either the reactor
or another storage system. Due to potential ly large sodium-moisture reactions during the washing
process, the cask is likely to be located in a shielding hot cell designed for potential release. The
maximum dose potential for this source is assigned a rank of high because only a single
subassembly is assumed to be in the station at any given time. Most of the radionuclides driving
the maximum dose potential to be high would be either solids , such as the fuel, or aerosols if
released. These solids and aerosols would require energy to initiate their dispersal and continuous
energy to support their transport beyond a limited range. Therefore, the dispersibility ranking is
assumed to be medium for this source. The barrier rankin g for the used subassembly wash station
is assumed to be low because the radionuclides have both inherent barriers and multiple physical
barriers including the fuel matrix, cladding, wash station cask, and sealed shielded hot cell. A radar
plot of the three rankings for the used subassembly wash station is provided in Figure 5.38.

113

Figure 5.38. SFR used subassembly wash station source radar plot .

5.4.2.6. SFR Used Fuel Storage Sodium Pool Consequence Assessment
As discussed in Section 5.4.1.8, used fuel subassemblies may be stored in the reactor vessel or an
external sodium storage pool to allow time for cooling before treatment. The external used fuel
storage sodium pool is assumed to hold a significant number of subassemblies , with the minimum
being the number of subassemblies removed during a single refueling cycle. The maximum dose
potential ranking for this pool is very high due to the assumed number of used subassemblies in
the pool. The dispersibility ranking is low, as it was for the SFR core, as the main contributors to
the maximum dose potential, the fuel and FP aerosols, would require significant energy for
dispersal. The barrier ranking for this storage pool is also low because radioactive material release
and transport are prevented by multiple inherent and applied barriers such as the fuel matrix, the
cladding, the sodium coolant, the sodium vessel, most likely a guard vessel, and the reactor
building structures. A radar plot of the three rankings for the SFR used fuel storage sodium pool is
provided in Figure 5.39.

Figure 5.39. SFR used fuel storage sodium pool radar plot .

5.4.2.7. SFR Used Fuel Storage Water Pool Consequence Assessment
As discussed in Section 5.4.1.8, used fuel subassemblies may be stored in a used fuel storage
water pool after treatment to remove residual sodium as an onsite interim storage option. It is
114

assumed that this storage option, at a minimum, would be designed to hold a significant number of
subassemblies representing several complete core loadings , and at a maximum it would be
designed to hold a plant’s lifetime number of subassemblies depending on whether or not multiple
pools are utilized and/or offsite storage options are pursued. Due to the number of subassemblies
presumed to be in the pool, the maximum dose potential ranking is very high. The dispersibility
ranking for this source is assumed to be low as the main contributors to the maximum dose
potential, the fuel and FP aerosols, would require significant energy for dispersal. The barrier
ranking for this storage pool is conservatively assumed to b e medium. While there may be inherent
barriers and multiple physical barriers to radionuclide transport in this system, the possible
presence of bond sodium in the cladding , which could react energetically with water in the pool if
cladding breaches occur, could affect additional barriers. To mitigate these concerns, the
subassemblies could be placed in inert canisters that would then be placed in the water pool to
cool. Additionally, the used fuel storage water pool may be located outside the reactor build ing in
another industrial building onsite that may not be credited for radionuclide retention. A radar plot
of the three rankings for the SFR used fuel storage water pool is provided in Figure 5.23.

Figure 5.40. SFR used fuel storage water pool radar plot .

5.4.3. SFR Security Risk Assessment
The risks considered in this section focus on potential security concerns for the radiological
sources described in Sections 5.4.1. and 5.4.2. that are typically associated with the SFR designs .
The security risk methodology used to evaluate these risks is described in Section 4 but can be
summarized by the following steps:
 1. Identify the systems associated with each safety function for each radioactive source
 2. Assess the importance of protection for each system
 3. Assess the vulnerability for each system.
The specific system details may vary depending on the SFR plant layout, DID strategies, system
locations, etc. Due to the qualitative nature of the current assessment, assumptions are made for
the systems considered. To perform more quantitative security risk assessments, specific design
details for each system and facility would be required. Below are summary security assessment

115

rankings. The complete security risk tables are found in Appendix A of this report. Figure 5.41
shows the relative location of the major safety SSCs analyzed for SFRs in this report.

5.4.3.1. SFR Core Security Assessment
An SFR core region has many systems that support safety functions such as heat removal,
reactivity control, and radionuclide retention and which are shown in Table 5.27. For the generic
SFR assessment performed here, it is assumed that the facility has two means of heat removal.
The first is the primary heat transport system , which typically includes SSCs such as an
intermediate coolant loop, heat exchangers, a steam generator, and other balance of plant SSCs.
The second is the passive heat removal system (s) such as a DRACS and/or RVACS. For reactivity
control, it is assumed the generic SFR facility has three systems/features that include control rods,
safety rods, and inherent reactivity feedback. Radionuclide retention is provided by the fuel matrix,
the fuel pin cladding, the sodium coolant, the primary sodium coolant boundary structures, a guard
vessel, and the containment/confinement structures and systems. Due to the reactive nature of
sodium with oxygen, oxygen is listed as a chemical hazard as possible sodium -oxygen reactions
could provide additional energy to enhance radionuclide release and transport. Support systems
identified for this area include electrical power, I&C, and plant cooling. Each system was assessed
to determine SSC importance and vulnerability ranking. Table 5.27 provides summary results of
that assessment for the SFR core with detailed results provided in Table 8.18.

116

As expected, several systems have a medium or high SSC importance ranking due to their assumed
safety-related classification to ensure that FSFs, such as heat removal, reactivity control, and
radionuclide retention, are maintained. For example, for the generic SFR assessment performed
here, it was assumed that the passive decay heat removal system(s), DRACS and/or RVACS, are
safety-related, and other means of heat removal provide DID. Therefore, the SSC importance of the
DRACS and/or RVACS is medium. The safety rods were assessed to have a high SSC importance
because they were assumed to be classified as safety -related SSCs in the generic design, and they
were also assumed to have a medium compromise risk. If it were assumed that measures were
incorporated into the design of the safety rod controls to limit the ability of an adversary's actions
to compromise the system or to limit the consequences of such action s, the high ranking could be
reduced to medium. It is likely that such measures would be incorporated into the design , but they
were not assumed here to highlight the importance of such considerations. It should be noted that
for each system with a medium or high SSC importance, other than I&C, its SSC vulnerability is
medium or low. Also, some of the medium rankings may be conservative here and are intended to
highlight the necessity of limiting the vulnerability of SSCs to direct SSC attacks, indirect SSC
attacks, and cyberattacks if they are important to safety. Systems that are highly susceptible to
those types of attacks due to their location, access, or reliance on subsystems , such as power and
I&C, have lower SSC importance. The systems with high/very high SSC vulnerability for the SFR
core include the primary heat transport system (PHTS), containment/confinement, power, I&C, and
plant cooling due to their number of components, access points, and/or reliance on other
susceptible support systems. The SSC importance ranking for each of these systems is medium or
low. Some possible SSC vulnerability-reduction mechanisms include limiting the number of access
points to the SSCs associated with these systems, limiting the connections with other locations
and/or systems, and providing cyberattack mitigation strategies. Examples of these reduction
mechanisms include utilizing underground secured connection points for piping and conduits,
minimizing entrance locations to the reactor building , requiring multifactor authentication for
personnel at entrance locations, requiring multifactor authentication on all computer systems to
prevent unauthorized access, and including virus scanners to detect malicious software and
activities.

5.4.3.2. SFR Cesium Trap Security Assessment
The SFR primary sodium purification system cesium trap components/area in this generic
assessment is assumed to have heat removal provided by the building HVAC and/or a system
designed to remove heat from the atmosphere of the sealed compartment that contains the cesium
trap components. Radionuclide retention is provided by the cesium trap vessel, the sealed
compartment housing the system/components, system isolation components such as valves, and
the containment/confinement structures and systems including the building HVAC. Similar to other
systems containing sodium, chemical hazards exist for oxygen reactions that could provide energy
to enhance radionuclide release and transport. Support systems for the cesium trap include power
and I&C. Table 5.28 provides the summary SSC importance and SSC vulnerability rankings for
these systems with detailed results provided in Table 8.19.
Two SSCs have a medium SSC importance ranking in this generic SFR assessment : the cesium trap
vessel, which is credited as the main barrier for retaining radionuclides contained within it , and the
sealed compartment housing the cesium trap components. The SSC vulnerability of these two SSCs
is low and high, respectively. The high SSC vulnerability of the sealed component is due to a high
direct SSC attack vulnerability and medium indirect and cyberattack vulnerabilities. These rankings
could be reduced by ensuring that the sealed compartment structures are in a controlled location
with limited access, they are robust enough that direct SSC attack concerns are minimized, the
reliance on support systems is limited to reduce in direct SSC attack concerns, and/or measures
are put in place to reduce any possible vulnerabilities to cyberattacks. High and very high SSC

118

vulnerability rankings for additional SSCs are due to their location, access, or reliance on
subsystems such as power and I&C. These SSCs all have SSC importance rankings of low.

5.4.3.4. SFR Noble Gas Decay Tanks Security Assessment
The SFR CGCS noble gas decay tanks in this generic assessment are assumed to have heat
removal provided by the building HVAC. Radionuclide retention is provided by the tanks
themselves; the compartment housing the system/components , which are not assumed to be
sealed; and system isolation components such as valves. Support systems for the noble gas decay
tanks include power and I&C. Table 5.30 provides the summary SSC importance and SSC
vulnerability rankings for these systems with detailed results provided in Table 8.21.
Two SSCs have a medium SSC importance ranking in this generic SFR assessment : the noble gas
decay tanks themselves, which are credited as the main barrier for retaining radionuclides
contained within them, and the compartment housing the noble gas decay tanks and other
components. The SSC vulnerability of these two SSCs is medium and high , respectively. The
medium SSC vulnerability of the noble gas decay tanks is due to an assumed medium accessibility
ranking for direct SSC attacks based on an assumption that these decay tanks may be located
outside of containment/confinement structures. The high SSC vulnerability of th e compartment is
due to a high direct SSC attack vulnerability and medium indirect and cyberattack vulnerabilities.
These rankings could be reduced by locating these SSC s inside of containment/confinement or by
ensuring that the compartment structures are robust enough that direct SSC attack concerns are
minimized, reliance on support systems is limited to reduce in direct SSC attack concerns, and/or
measures are put in place to reduce any possible vulnerabilities to cyberattacks. It should be noted
that the consequences (maximum dose potential) associated with releases from this system have a
low ranking, which should be considered when determining an appropriate location for the system
within the facility. High and very high SSC vulnerability rankings for additional SSCs are due to their
location, access, or reliance on subsystems such as power and I&C. These SSCs all have SSC
importance rankings of low.

5.4.3.5. SFR Used Subassembly Wash Station Security Assessment
The SFR used fuel subassembly wash station area in this generic assessment is assumed to have
two heat removal systems: one active and one passive for cask cooling. Administrative controls
prevent criticality, limiting the number of assemblies in the wash station at a time. Radionuclide
retention is provided by the fuel matrix/cladding of the subassemblies, sealed casks that contain
the subassemblies, and the sealed compartment where the subassemblies are washed. Support
systems for the subassembly wash station include power and I&C. Table 5.31 provides the
summary SSC importance and SSC vulnerability rankings for these systems with detailed results
provided in Table 8.22.
For the generic SFR assessment, the passive cask cooling system is the only SSC of the used
subassembly wash station to have a high SSC importance ranking because it fulfills the
fundamental safety function of heat removal and is assumed to only have a single redundant
backup. This ranking could be reduced if additional passive or active means of heat removal were
incorporated into the design. While the subassembly fuel/cladding fulfills the fundamental safety
function of radionuclide retention, its comprom ise risk was assumed to be negligible, and other
SSCs provide sufficient DID such that it was assigned a medium SSC importance. For radionuclide
retention purposes, the cask is ranked as medium but could also be ranked high. The medium
ranking highlights that it is a second line of defense if the primary line, the subassembly cladding,
were to fail. The sealed compartment that contains the wash station components is assigned a low
SSC importance for radionuclide retention as it provides a third line of de fense. The SSC
vulnerability of these four SSCs is low, low, medium, and high , respectively. The medium SSC
vulnerability of the cask is due to assumed medium rankings for both direct and in direct SSC
attack vulnerabilities. The high SSC vulnerability of t he sealed compartment is due to a high direct
SSC attack vulnerability and medium indirect and cyberattack vulnerabilities. This ranking could be
reduced by ensuring that the sealed compartment structures are in a controlled location with
limited access, they are robust enough that direct SSC attack concerns are minimized, reliance on
support systems is limited to reduce indirect SSC attack concerns, and/or measures are put in
place to reduce any possible vulnerabilities to cyberattacks. High and very high SSC vulnerability
rankings for additional SSCs are due to their location, access, or reliance on subsystems such as

could be reduced by ensuring that the SSCs are robust enough that direct SSC attack concerns are

5.4.4. SFR Risk Reduction and Mitigation Approaches
The SFR designs being proposed in the United States build upon lessons learned for safety and
security strategies that have been developed over hundreds of operation years for SFRs both in the
United States and worldwide. This experience is reflected in the security assessments provided in
the previous section in which SSCs with a high safety importance typically have low to medium SSC
vulnerabilities and a lack of reliance on support systems that have higher levels of SSC
vulnerability. Additionally, these highly important systems have SSCs that provide DID to ensure
that even if a single system is compromised, the FSFs can still be fulfilled. This is certainly evident
in the SSCs highly relied upon for heat removal, reactivity control, and radionuclide retention for
the SFR core. For non-core sources of radioactivity, the possible security risks may be relatively
higher than for the core source because of the location of the sources, the number of barriers
preventing release, and the dispersibility of the radioactive material of each source. This section
will provide an overview of possible risk reduction and mitigation approaches for SFR vendors to
consider and balance with other design priorities.

124

5.4.4.1. Reducing SSC Vulnerability by Location and Accessibility
The SSC vulnerability ranking is heavily dependent on where in the facility an SSC is assumed to be
located and how accessible it is. For example, SSCs located inside of containment/confinement
structures vs. outside are assumed to have lower accessibilit y. SSCs located below grade may have
reduced vulnerability compared to a system above grade. Additionally, it may be possible to reduce
SSC vulnerability by limiting the number of access points and limiting the connections with other
locations and/or systems. Examples of these reduction mechanisms include utilizing underground
secured connection points for piping and conduits, minimizing entrance/exit locations, and
requiring multifactor authentication for personnel access.

5.4.4.2. Risk Reduction via Source Quantity, Barriers, or Dispersibility
For the sources considered in this generic assessment, several ranked as high or very high for
maximum dose potential. These higher rankings for maximum dose potential are typically coupled
with lower barrier and dispersibility rankings. Conversely, sources with low maximum dose potential
can have higher barrier and dispersibility rank ings. While the current balancing of the maximum
dose potential ranking with barrier and dispersibility rankings may be acceptable from a safety
standpoint, additional considerations may be required for security concerns. For example, the
noble gas decay tanks of the CGCS have a low maximum dose potential but are assumed to have a
high barrier ranking because they may be located outside of containment in non -hardened
enclosures, and they have a high dispersibility ranking due to the gaseous state of the radioactive
material. This combination could result in an attractive security target due to the accessibility of
this source compared to other sources , and because minimal energy would be required to disperse
the radioactive material even though the possible consequences are relatively low. For similar
types of source risk, measures could be considered to reduce the barrier ranking by adding
additional robust barriers or to reduce the dispersibility ranking by changing the physical state of
the radioactive source material, if possible. For sources with higher maximum dose potential, risk
reduction could be achieved by limiting the amount of radioactivity in a given system/area. For
example, limiting the number of subassemblies being transported in casks at a time from the
reactor to the subassembly treatment or storage areas.

5.4.4.3. Sodium Fire Protection Systems and Strategies Considerations
As noted in several tables in the security assessment, for radiological sources where sodium is
present, the possible reactions of sodium with air or water should be considered , as these
reactions can provide energy to enhance radionuclide release and transport. While SFPSs are
typically designed to minimize these reactions, their effectiveness during a security event may be
reduced. For example, it is typical for sodium piping to have a leak jacket around the outside of the
pipe to retain and route any leaked sodium to other SFPS components (i.e., a sodium drain tank or
fire suppression decking) if pipe wall/weld failures were to occur. However, any intentional actions
to cause a pipe to fail could also impact the effectiveness of the leak jacket and/or other SFPS
components. Diminishing the effectiveness of SFPS components could result in higher than
anticipated radiological release rates for a given source if the SFPS is credited with minimizing
sodium reactions. SFPSs have historically been designed to wi thstand typical safety
events/scenarios, such as design basis earthquakes, but could be evaluated in the future to
ensure they perform adequately during security-related events. This would provide assurance that

125

an adversary’s ability to leverage to their advantage sodium reactions with air and/or water is
minimized.

5.6. ADVANCED LIGHT-WATER REACTORS
This section provides an overview of advanced LWR (ALWR) technology, including technology
taxonomy; reference reactor designs to perform generic analyses ; scope, principles, and
assumptions for the analyses; and sources of radioactivity that were identified and selected for the
analyses.
ALWRs comprise a subset of LWRs. The basic types of LWRs are pressurized -water reactors (PWRs)
and boiling-water reactors (BWRs). PWRs operate at high pressure and produce steam indirectly by
transferring the heat from the primary reactor coolant to a secondary loop via a steam generator.
BWRs produce steam directly by boiling the reactor coolant. ALWRs can be either type as well.
However, most ALWR designs are much smaller than those of the current operating fleet of LWRs.
Additionally, ALWRs are being designed with additional passive safety features so as to be
inherently safe. Figure 5.42 shows the categorization of LWR technologies.

 Light-Water
 Reactors

 Advanced
 Pressurized- Boiling-Water
 Light-Water
 Water Reactors Reactors
 Reactors

 Advanced Advanced
 Pressurized- Boiling-Water
 Water Reactors Reactors

Figure 5.42. Categorization of light-water reactor technologies.

2020a; NEA 2023). The table only includes ALWR designs that are under design and are land

 UK-SMR Rolls Royce & Partners PWR UK 443
 NuScale NuScale Power Inc. PWR USA 60

 AP300 Westinghouse PWR USA 300
 mPower BWX Technologies Inc. PWR USA 195

 Construction
