 Europe

As Table 5.34 shows, multiple countries are designing ALWRs. While ALWRs have new features, the
basic technology of LWRs has been around since 1957 , resulting in over 60 years of operating
experience (U.S. NRC 2021). Currently, in the United States, there are 93 LWRs operating
commercially (WNA 2023). The newest operating LWR, Vogtle Unit 3, which is a PWR, entered
commercial operation on July 31, 2023 (Georgia Power 2023). It is the first reactor to be
constructed and made operational in the United States in over 30 years. The past 60+ years of
operating experience has provided a greater understanding of LWR technology, which has resulted
in design improvements that have been incorporated into current ALWR designs.
This ALWR study was a generic study using several U.S. ALWR designs as references rather than
specific design information. None of the currently or previously operating LWRs in the United States
were analyzed for this study. The goal of the study was to evaluate AR designs that have not been
constructed. The study involved no proprietary data or information , and all references are publicly
available.
Several ALWR designs in Table 5.34 are discussed here briefly:
 • NuScale Power Module TM
 The NuScale Power Module TM (NPM) is a 250-MWt integral PWR (iPWR) that employs gravity-
 driven natural circulation of the primary coolant for both normal operation and shutdown
 mode (IAEA 2020b). The NPM, including containment, will be fully factory built and shipped
 to the plant site by truck, rail, or barge. NuScale’s VOYGR -12 power plant design can
 accommodate up to 12 NPMs, resulting in a total gross output of 924 MWe. Other smaller
128

 power plant options from NuScale are the four -module VOYGR-4 (308 MWe) and six-module
      VOYGR-6 (462 Mwe) (NuScale 2023).
  • Holtec SMR-160
 The SMR-160 is an SMR designed to produce 160 MWe using low-enriched uranium fuel
 (Holtec International n.d.). The SMR-160 is designed such that all the cooling water needed
 for safe shutdown of the plant, even for the most severe accident scenarios, is located
 within the plant. The safety systems that access the SMR -160 cooling water reserve are
 passive, meaning they operate under the force of gravity to cool the heat generated from
 the reactor operations and do not require active systems, like pumps. The SMR-160’s
 steam condensers can use either local water sources or air for cooling.
 Key features of this reactor include a below -grade core, a passive containment cooling
 system, a “black start” capability, the absence of penetrations in the lower region of the
 reactor vessel (i.e., no pathway for the inadvertent drainage of reactor cooling water), and
 an onsite below-grade storage of used fuel in welded multipurpose canisters.
  • GE Hitachi BWRX-300
 The BWRX-300 is a 300-Mwe, water-cooled SMR with natural circulation and passive safety
 systems that leverage the design and licensing basis of the U.S. NRC –certified 1,520-MWe
 economic simplified BWR (GE Hitachi Nuclear Energy 2023). The reactor uses steam
 condensation and gravity to cool itself for a minimum of 7 days without power or operator
 action. The BWRX-300 containment is smaller and simpler by using surface -mounted
 integral RPV isolation valves to rapidly isolate the flow from a downstream pipe break to
 minimize pressure and temperature buildup in the conta inment, and an isolation condenser
 system (ICS) to remove energy from the RPV rather than directing that energy into a
 suppression pool. The reactor building is built from steel -concrete composite modules with
 diaphragm plates.
 Key features of the BWRX-300 are the use of integral RPV isolation valves that mitigate the
 impacts of loss-of-coolant accidents and a large capacity ICS that provides overpressure
 protection without the need for safety relieve valves. The ICS also acts as a passive,
 naturally circulating emergency core cooling system without any need for alternating
 current power to perform its functions.
  • Westinghouse AP300
 The AP300 is a 300-MWe PWR design that is a miniaturized version of the existing AP1000
 (Westinghouse n.d.). Due to its smaller size, the AP300 uses a single primary loop and a
 single steam generator as opposed to the AP1000 that uses a conventional two -loop, two-
 steam-generator configuration. Key features of the AP300 include the use of a new
 accident tolerant fuel technology called Advanced Doped Pellet Technology (ADOPT TM ), an
 internal condensation and natural recirculation safety system, a steel contain ment vessel,
 a natural convection air discharge system, a passive containment cooling system, a gravity
 drain water tank, and the placement of spent fuel storage inside the containment.

129

5.6.1. ALWR Technology Systems
This section provides a generic technology description of ALWRs, including the different processes
and systems. For the analysis, a generic ALWR functional diagram was developed and is provided in
Figure 5.43. The generic ALWR utilizes an open fuel cycle. Fresh fuel is prepared off site,
transported, and stored onsite before it is used in the reactor core. The spent and damaged fuel
assemblies (if any) are stored temporarily onsite to allow them to decay considerably, typically in a
spent fuel pool. After the spent fuel assemblies ha ve decayed sufficiently, they are transported to
an onsite interim dry storage or an offsite storage facility.
During reactor operation, the chemical and volume control system (CVCS) treats the coolant to
maintain the chemical composition of burnable poison and filter impurities , including radionuclides
in the stream. The letdown valve has two main systems to perform this function: the gas treatment
system to process airborne materials and ion exchange resins to capture contaminants in the
coolant. Wastes from these systems are consolidated and stored onsite temporarily before being
transported to an offsite storage facility.

Figure 5.43. Generic ALWR block diagram.

ALWRs generate much less power than conventional LWRs. For that reason, many of the design
features are modified and differ from those of LWRs. For example, less power translates to a
smaller core, which results in there being more room for other components, such as steam
generators, and for the pressurizer to be integrated inside the reactor vessel. Additionally, ALWRs
use a passive decay heat removal system driven by natural phenomena instead of an active
system. This passive system removes many active SS Cs commonly found in LWRs. The removal of
these systems allows ALWRs to be compact in size. The reactor’s compactness permits some of the
reactor designs to be located below grade, potentially making them more secure than above-grade
designs.

5.6.1.1. Offsite Fuel Preparation
UO 2 fuel assemblies are manufactured and prepared off site. Fresh fuel is enriched up to 5%. The
cladding material is metallic, typically zirconium. Some other cladding materials may be considered
consistent with the accident tolerant fuel designs, such as silicon carbide/TRISO, coated zircalloy,
FeCrAl, etc.

130

5.6.1.2. Transportation
Fresh fuel from offsite is transported to the reactor site in secured transportation casks. The
transported fuel is in a solid and sealed state as fuel assemblies, which are not readily dispersible.
The frequency of transportation is relatively low, since ALWRs are targeted to have refueling cycles
of 18, 24, 36, or even 48 months. For a typical PWR-type ALWR, it can be conservatively assumed
that half of the assemblies are replaced during the refueling stage. This many assemblies may be
transported in a single shipment. A more optimized refueling cycle utilizes a three-batch in-out
refueling scheme where only one-third of fuel assemblies is replaced while the remaining two-thirds
are reshuffled in the core. The transportation of spent nuclear fuel assemblies typically is more
stringent than that for fresh fuel due to the presence of FPs and therefore higher radioactivity.
They are shipped in large and robust type-B packages.
The shipment follows regulations on transport ing hazardous materials and the safe transport of
radioactive materials. These regulations may be country -specific; however, they typically conform to
international standards such as the IAEA’s standard on radioactive material transportation (IAEA
2018). The International Air Transport Association’s ( IATA’s) dangerous goods regulations (IATA
n.d.), and International Maritime Dangerous Goods (IMDG) code (IMO 2023). In many cases,
regulations are in place to ensure the safe transport of fresh fuel to the site and spent fuel from
the reactor site. This is typically done by placing limits on material contents and stringent criteria
on the package, stowage, surface contamination , radioactivity, and criticality, etc. In the United
States, the security of radioactive packages during transportation is required by regulation, such as
10 CFR 73.26, “Transportation Physical Protection Systems, Subsystems, Components, and
Procedures,” and 49 CFR, “Transportation.” There may be some areas of overlap with the transport
safety regulations in 10 CFR 71, “Packaging and Transportation of Radioactive Material,” such as
the quality assurance requirements, advance notification of shipment, and internal inspections.

5.6.1.3. Storage
Fresh fuel assemblies may be temporarily stored onsite before being loaded into the core. To
optimize the reactor’s capacity factor, fresh fuel needs to be onsite before it is time to replace the
fuel assemblies. However, it may be only several days in adv ance, since the refueling outage is in
the range of 18 to 36 days. Furthermore, the fresh fuel assemblies are stored inside the protected
reactor building.

5.6.1.4. ALWR Core
The cores of ALWR are similar to those of conventional LWRs. The core consists of the typical 17 ×
17 fuel assembly design, although some cores utilize other array types , such as the 10 × 10 or 21
× 21 fuel assemblies. Core reactivity is controlled by the control rods and diluted boron in the
water coolant. The fuel itself is made of UO2 enriched up to 5%. The core is located within the RPV
inside the reactor building. Some ALWR designs have the core located below grade. However, for
conservatism, the generic ALWR is assumed to have an above -grade reactor core.
Heat from the core is removed by the reactor coolant system (RCS) using light water as a coolant
and moderator. During normal PWR operation, the coolant in the RCS transfers heat from the core
to the steam generator(s). The BWR models generate steam in the core, which is then used to turn
turbines and generate electricity. In case of accident events, the reactor is shut down , which
reduces the fission reaction in the core and terminates power generation on the turbine side. The
131

excess heat is then removed by emergency cooling systems and then eventually by a residual or
decay heat removal system, which is a passive system (unlike in conventional LWRs) utilizing the
passive containment cooling system with a natural circulation; however, it requires valve
alignment.

5.6.1.5. Chemical and Volume Control System
During reactor operation, there may be a minor buildup of radionuclides in the RCS due to neutron
irradiation or FPs from small cladding failures in the fuel assemblies. These radionuclides need to
be filtered out. In addition, the coolant’s chemical concentration needs to be maintained. Such
functions are carried out by the CVCS system. This system performs several sa fety functions, which
include adjusting the reactor coolant boric acid concentration, maintaining the RCS coolant
volume, adding corrosion-inhibiting chemicals to the RCS, removing radioactive contaminants from
the RCS to keep it within the designed activity limits, providing borated water for emergency core
cooling, and processing reactor coolant for the reuse of boric acid and reactor makeup water in the
boron recovery system.
The CVCS system removes radioactive contaminants through the gas treatment system and the
liquid ion exchange resin system. The gas treatment system removes gaseous products such as
fission gases, hydrogen from pressurized tanks, oxidation byproducts, impu rities in the SSCs, etc.
The waste gas can be stored in compressed tanks and/or processed through a combination of
HEPA filters, carbon adsorbers, and scrubber tanks before being vented to the atmosphere.
Meanwhile, the liquid form of RCS contaminants is r emoved by the ion exchangers or
demineralizers containing cation and anion resins. These charged resins capture ionic impurities,
such as lithium and cesium. A filter is placed downstream of the ion exchangers to capture broken
resin beads.

5.6.1.6. Onsite Storage of Non-fuel Radioactive Waste
An onsite radioactive waste storage facility is most likely needed to temporarily house low - to
medium-level radioactive wastes, such as used HEPA filters, used resins, or contaminated personal
protective equipment (PPE). The waste forms may be preprocessed to reduce their volumes and
immobilize them before they are stored in radioactive waste containers. This storage facility should
be in a building equipped with radiation monitors.

5.6.1.7. Spent and Damaged Fuel Assemblies
Spent fuel from ALWRs may have a discharge burnup ranging from 25 GWd/MTU to 65 GWD/MTU,
although many designs target a higher burnup of more than 40 GWD/MTU. The number of fuel
assemblies in the core ranges from tens to several hundred assemblies. NuScale for example uses
37 assemblies, while BWRX uses 240 assemblies. The refueling cycle ranges from 10 months to
several years. Some reactor designs aim for online refueling or even zero refueling, which means
they operate for their intended lifetime using a once-through set of fuel assemblies. The refueling
cycle of many of the ALWRs, however, falls within the range of 18to 24 months. ALWRs with a
longer-range refueling cycle typically utilize a 50% core replacement strategy, while ones with a
shorter cycle use the three-batch in-out refueling scheme (one-third core replacement strategy).
The longer refueling cycle is expected to result in a higher spent fuel burnup and a higher reactor
capacity factor.

132

5.6.1.8. Onsite Storage of Used Fuel
Spent fuel assemblies, and damaged fuel assemblies (if any), are stored temporarily in the plant
site before being transported to a final repository site. Most of the ALWR design s adopt the
conventional LWR approach of using a spent fuel pool to store spent fuel assemblies temporarily
until the assemblies cool down sufficiently to allow their safe transport to an offsite storage facility.
The ALWR spent fuel pool design is similar to that of existing LWRs. It requires submerging the
spent fuel in a steel-lined concrete pool under 20 feet of water or more to attenuate radiation. Pool
water is circulated using pumps to cool the assemblies. The pool is equipped with instruments to
monitor the water level and ambient radiation.
It is probable that a small percentage of fuel assemblies will be classified as failed or damaged, so
they will need to be removed from the reactor before reaching the end of their lifetime
(U.S. NRC 2007). Damaged fuels are therefore categorized as spent nuclear fuel. The criteria of
fuel damage differ for usage and transportation. The generic flowchart to determine fuel damage is
shown in Figure 5.44. The starting point is determining whether the fuel rod or assembly has any
defect greater than a hairline crack or a pinhole leak. If yes, a further assessment is conducted to
determine whether the breach is large enough to release fuel fragments outside the cladding. If
yes, it is considered a gross breach and should be considered damaged. If no t, an additional
assessment is done to decide whether the fuel rod or assembly can meet its fuel -specific and
system-related functions. These system-related functions depend on whether the fuel is used in the
core, stored in a storage facility, or transported to another facility. For example, the fuel functi ons
in the core include maintaining its physical integrity for a coolable geometry and maintaining
neutron flux specifications. Any fuel rod or assembly that fails to meet these functions is
considered damaged. For storage, 10 CFR 72 , “Licensing Requirements for the Independent
Storage of Spent Nuclear Fuel, High-Level Radioactive Waste, and Reactor-Related Greater than
Class C Waste,” states that the fuel must be retrievable and protected against gross ruptures. For
transportation, 10 CFR 71 states that the fuel must not be substantially rearrang ed during normal
conditions of transportation.

133

Figure 5.44. Flowchart for classifying spent nuclear fuel. Source: U.S. NRC (2007).

From the LWR experience, close to 2% of fuel assemblies failed in the United States from 1968 to
2013 (Petersen 2020). Most of these failed fuels were put inside stainless-steel fuel cans in
conjunction with storage and/or transportation systems. These cans are containers that have a
mesh endpoint at one end to allow gaseous and liquid media to escape while still minimizing the
dispersal of gross particulate material.
Fuel assemblies in the storage pool are secured in racks. These racks may incorporate neutron
absorber panels between the assemblies to ensure that the fuel remains subcritical. This function
is important, especially when high-density racks are used to accommodate more assemblies in the
pool. Fuel assemblies can be stored in about one -half the volume required for storage in standard
racks using such racks. The neutron absorption capability of these neutron -absorbing racks is
generally characterized by their 10 Bareal density. 10 B is generally present in the chemical form of
boron carbide (B 4 C) within a metal matrix or a polymer matrix, although 10 B atoms are incorporated
into the alloy composition of borated stainless steel. 10 B is also dissolved in the pool’s water in the
form of boric acid. However, in the conservative scenario of risk analysis, this diluted boron is not
credited. Conservatively, the pool must remain subcritical with the full loading of the maximum
anticipated enrichment if the pool is flooded with pure water without soluble boron. Hence, the
spent fuel pool should be designed assuming the use of unborated water.

5.6.1.9. Offsite Storage
Some ALWR designs propose a dry storage approach, such as the SMR -160 that uses a
multipurpose canister to store all the spent fuel for the life of the plant in an underground vertical
storage cask design. Most ALWR designs, however, opt to transport the spent fuel assemblies to an
134

offsite storage facility after the assemblies have been sufficiently cooled in the temporary storage
pools (which may take several years).

5.6.2. ALWR Consequences Assessment
The risks considered in this section focus on potential sources of radioactivity that are typically
associated with ALWRs, specifically advanced PWR designs , and their release. The safety
consequence methodology used to evaluate these risks is summarized by the following steps:
 1. Identify radioactive sources
 2. Assess the dose potential for each source
 3. Assess the dispersibility of radioactive materials for each source
 4. Assess barriers for each source that would limit the transport of radioactive material.
The specific source details for each ALWR design may vary depending on the plant power rating,
facility layout, fuel assembly handling procedures, specific system locations, etc. The sources
considered in this section have typically been included in conventional LWR designs and would be
expected to be relevant to the advanced designs. Due to the qualitative nature of the current
assessment, assumptions are made for the sources considered. Assumptions relevant to the
ranking process for each source are provided in the corresponding sections, along with
justifications for each ranking. To perform more quantitative risk consequence assessments,
specific design details for each source and facility would be required , along with mechanistic
radioactive material release and transport evaluations. Table 5.35 provides a summary of the
ALWR sources considered.

Table 5.35. Summary of ALWR risk consequence rankings for various sources .
 Maximum Dose
 Reactor Dispersibility Barrier
 Source Potential
 Type Ranking Ranking
 Ranking
 Reactor core Very High Low Low
 Used fuel storage pool Very High Low Medium
 Coolant purification Medium Medium Medium
 APWR system
 Gaseous radioactive waste Low High High
 Liquid radioactive waste Low Medium Low
 Solid radioactive waste High Low Low
NOTE: Multiple barriers, inherent or applied, results in a lower ranking. For example, the “Low” ranking is characterized
by multiple inherent or mix of inherent/applied barriers while the “High” ranking is characterized by no inherent barrier
or a single applied barrier. Section 4.3. provides a more detailed description of these rankings.

5.6.2.1. ALWR Reactor Core Consequence Assessment
The core of an ALWR contains a significant amount of radioactive material including the fuel itself,
FPs, activated components, etc. When evaluating the maximum dose potential, all radioactive
materials are considered independent of their ability to be dispersed or the number of barriers in
place to limit their release and transport. The assumed maximum dos e potential ranking for an
ALWR core is very high, as would be expected for any reactor technology core source. The
135

dispersibility ranking for an ALWR core is low because the main contributor to the maximum dose
potential, the fuel, is in a solid state and would require significant energy for dispersal. The barrier
ranking for an ALWR core is also low because radioactive release and transport are prevented by
multiple inherent and applied barriers , including the fuel matrix, cladding, reactor vessel,
containment vessel, reactor building, etc. A radar plot of the three rankings for an ALWR core is
provided in Figure 5.45.

Figure 5.45. ALWR core source radar plot.

5.6.2.2. ALWR Used Fuel Storage Pool Consequence Assessment
After their time in the core, used fuel assemblies are then stored in a water -filled used-fuel pool to
allow time for cooling. The used-fuel pool is assumed to hold a significant number of fuel
assemblies. The maximum dose potential ranking for the used -fuel pool is very high due to the
number of fuel assemblies in the pool. The dispersibility ranking for the pool is low, as it is for the
ALWR core, since the main contributors to the maximum dose potential are in a solid state and
would require significant energy for dispersal. The barrier ranking for the used -fuel storage pool is
considered medium. While there are barriers to radionuclide transport, including the cladding,
water, reactor building, etc., there are not as many inherent and applied boundaries as the re are
for the ALWR core. Therefore, to be conservative, the used -fuel storage pool received a higher
ranking than the ALWR core. Depending on the actual design of an ALWR, this ranking may be
appropriate, or it may be too conservative if there are multiple inherent and applied barriers. A
radar plot of the three rankings for the ALWR used -fuel storage pool is provided in Figure 5.46.

136

Figure 5.46. ALWR used-fuel storage pool source radar plot.

5.6.2.3. ALWR Coolant Purification System Consequence Assessment
The ALWR coolant purification system filters and treats the primary coolant by removing
radionuclides from the coolant. One of the highest -activity-level FPs found in the primary coolant is
cesium. For this assessment, it is assumed that the coolant purification system can contain on the
order of 103 Ci of cesium, which results in a maximum dose potential ranking of medium. The
radionuclides in the purification system are in a liquid form resulting in less energy needed to
disperse the radionuclides. This results in the dispersibility ranking of medium. The barrier ranking
for the coolant purification system is also medium. The system is assumed to be in a similar
location as the used-fuel storage pool, which is outside the RPV. As previously stated, depending
on the design of the ALWR, this ranking may be appropriate or may be too conservative. A radar
plot of the three rankings for the ALWR coolant purification system is provided in Figure 5.47.

Figure 5.47. ALWR coolant purification system source radar plot.

5.6.2.4. ALWR Gaseous Radioactive Waste Consequence Assessment
Gaseous radioactive waste is produced in various locations and processes throughout the plant.
Examples include used-fuel storage pool evaporation, stack exhaust, gas leaks, etc. This gaseous
radioactive waste must then be sent to a radioactive waste building for capture. The highest
quantity of gaseous radioactive waste will be in the radioactive waste building where it is being
137

captured. However, gaseous radioactive waste must be transferred from all the various locations to
the radioactive waste building. The main radionuclides present are assumed to be noble gases
such as xenon and krypton. Therefore, the maximum dose potential ranking is low. Since the
radioactive material is in a gaseous form, it would require little to no energy to disperse , resulting
in a dispersibility ranking of high. While the radioactive waste process would be in a contained
radioactive waste building, there will be multiple pipes and ventilation systems that are inputs to
the building. For this assessment, it is assumed that the gaseous inputs cannot be isolated , and
therefore the barriers ranking is high. This ranking could be reduced if the design contained
isolation mechanisms for gaseous radioactive waste. A radar plot of the three rankings for the
ALWR gaseous radioactive waste is provided in Figure 5.48.

Figure 5.48. ALWR gaseous radioactive waste source radar plot.

5.6.2.5. ALWR Liquid Radioactive Waste Consequence Assessment
Liquid radioactive waste is produced in various locations and processes throughout the plant.
Examples include purification system waste, decontamination processes, drains, etc. Similar to
gaseous radioactive waste, liquid radioactive waste must also be se nt to a radioactive waste
building for containment. The liquid waste will be transferred through pipes from the various
locations to the waste building and collected in tanks. One of the highest -activity-level
radionuclides present will be cesium. It is as sumed there will be on the order of 102 Ci of cesium ,
which results in a maximum dose potential ranking of low. The radionuclides in the purification
system are in a liquid form resulting in less energy needed to disperse the radionuclides. This
results in the dispersibility ranking of medium. For this assessment, it is assumed that the liquid
waste tanks are contained in a shielded area that will contain leaks. It is also assumed that piping
going to the tanks contains isolation valves to prevent the entir e liquid waste inventory from
leaking if a leak did occur. Based on these assumptions, the barrier ranking is low. A radar plot of
the three rankings for the ALWR liquid radioactive waste is provided in Figure 5.49.

138

Figure 5.49. ALWR liquid radioactive waste source radar plot.

5.6.2.6. ALWR Solid Radioactive Waste Consequence Assessment
Solid radioactive waste is produced in various locations and processes through the plant. Examples
include waste from the purification system, sludges, resins, contaminated materials, etc. Some
solid waste, such as contaminated PPE, will be contained and transferred to the radioactive waste
building for processing in containers. Other solid waste, such as resins, will be transferred to the
radioactive waste building through piping to be collected in containers for disposal. Again, cesium
is expected to be the highest-activity radionuclide present in the solid waste with an activity on the
order of 104 Ci. Therefore, the maximum dose potential ranking is high. The radionuclides present
are in a solid state and would require significant energy for dispersal resulting in a dispersibility
ranking of low. The barrier ranking for solid radioactive waste is also low because radioactive
release and transport are prevented by multiple inherent and applied barriers , including the
containers, shielded areas, the radioactive waste building, etc. A radar plot of the three rankings
for the ALWR solid radioactive waste is provided in Figure 5.50.

Figure 5.50. ALWR solid radioactive waste source radar plot.

139

5.6.3. ALWR Security Risk Assessment
The risks considered in this section focus on potential security concerns that are typically
associated with the ALWR designs. The security risk methodology used to evaluate these risks was
described previously but can be summarized by the following steps:
 1. Identify the systems associated with each safety function for each radioactive source
 2. Assess the importance of protection for each system
 3. Assess the vulnerability for each system.
The specific system details for each ALWR design may vary depending on plant layout, DID
strategies, system locations, etc. The systems associated with each radioactive source are
expected to be relevant to advanced designs. Due to the qualitative nature of the current
assessment, assumptions related to system design and locations are generally made for the
systems considered. To perform more quantitative security risk assessments, specific design
details for each system and facility would be required. Below are summary security assessment
rankings. The complete security risk tables are found in Appendix A of this report. Figure 5.51
shows the relative location of the major safety SSCs analyzed for ALWRs in this report.

 Rx Cavity Rx Building(s) Exterior of/to Rx Building(s)
 Rx Core I&C Primary Heat Transport
 Primary Coolant Boundary ECCS Passive Cooling
 Fuel New Fuel AC Power
 Coolant Purification Hx Used Fuel Pool Plant Cooling
 Control / Shutdown Rods Radwaste Reactor Building Structure
 Containment

Figure 5.51. ALWR major system locations.

5.6.3.1. ALWR Reactor Core Security Assessment
An ALWR core has many systems that support the different safety functions. For this assessment,
the generic ALWR contains the primary HTS, passive decay heat removal, and emergency core

140

cooling for systems that provide heat removal. For reactivity control, there are control rods and
shutdown rods. The fuel/cladding, primary containment vessel, and reactor building support
radionuclide retention. The support systems for the core are power, I&C, and plant cooling. Each
system was analyzed regarding the core source to determine SSC importance and vulnerability
ranking. Table 5.36 shows the rankings for each of the systems.

Table 5.36. ALWR reactor core security assessment rankings .
 Structures, Systems, and Defense SSC SSC

 Components Line Importance Vulnerability
 Heat removal Primary heat transport system 1 Medium Very High
 Passive decay heat removal 2 High High
 Emergency core cooling 2 High High
 Reactivity control Control rods 1 Medium Low
 Shutdown rods 2 High Low
 Radionuclide Fuel/cladding 1 Medium Low

 Primary coolant boundary 2 Medium Medium
 Reactor building 3 Low High
 Support systems Power 1 Low Very High
 I&C 1, 2 Medium High
 Plant cooling 1 Low Very High
NOTE: The defense line value represents the typical use of the SSC in an operational plant according to the defense line

direct importance of that SSC to the safe ty of the plant), and the SSC Vulnerability represents the relative vulnerability

Based on Table 5.36, several systems have a high or very high ranking. The passive decay heat
removal, emergency core cooling, and shutdown rods have a high SSC importance ranking because,
for the generic design, they are considered safety-related systems and only have one additional
redundant system for DID. For the heat removal systems, adding multiple redundant trains at
different locations would result in a decreased ranking. For reactivity control, another method for
reactivity control could be included to reduce the shutdown rod ranking . The primary HTS, power,
and plant cooling have a very high SSC vulnerability rating due to the number of components and
access points. These systems contain components that are within containment, outside of
containment, and even external to the reactor building. Since these systems have external
connections that can affect the reactor itself, they are susceptible to attack and deserving of the
very high SSC vulnerability ranking. Depending on the design of the ALWR, this could be decreased
to a lower ranking. The passive decay heat removal, emergency core cooling, reactor building, and
I&C all have high SSC vulnerability rankings. These SSCs could have vulnerabilities associated with
the defeat or compromise of CDAs. The passive decay heat removal, emergency core cooling, and
I&C SSC vulnerability ranking could potentially be reduced by increasing the cybersecurity of these
systems and by providing mitigation strategies in case a cyberattack occur red. For this
assessment, it was assumed that these systems are solely controlled by CDAs with no mitigation
for operations. The reactor building SSC vulnerability ranking could be reduced depending on t he

141

design. Some possible reduction mechanisms could be to limit the number of access points to the
reactor building, limit connections with other locations and/or systems that could compromise the
building, and provide mitigation strategies against cyberattac ks. Some examples of these reduction
mechanisms could be having underground secured connection points for piping and conduits,
having minimal entrance locations to the reactor building , requiring entrance locations to use
multifactor authentication for personnel, requiring multifactor authentication on all computer
systems to prevent unauthorized access, and including virus scanners to detect malicious software
and activities.

5.6.3.2. ALWR Used Fuel Storage Pool Security Assessment
An ALWR used-fuel storage pool has many systems that support the different safety functions. For
this assessment, the generic ALWR contains the pool water inventory that provides heat removal.
For reactivity control, there are engineered criticality controls in the form of fuel storage racks and
administrative controls on the number of fuel assemblies. The fuel/cladding, coolant, and reactor
building support radionuclide retention. The support systems for the used -fuel storage pool are
power and I&C. Each system was analyzed regarding the used-fuel storage pool source to
determine SSC importance and vulnerability ranking. Table 5.37 shows the ranking for each of
these systems.

Table 5.37. ALWR used-fuel storage pool security assessment rankings .
 Structures, Systems, and Defense SSC SSC

 Components Line Importance Vulnerability
 Heat removal Pool water inventory 1 Very High Medium
 Reactivity control Storage racks 1 High Medium
 Administrative controls 1 Low Medium
 Radionuclide Fuel/cladding 1 Medium Low

 Coolant 1 Medium Low
 Reactor building 3 Low High
 Support systems Power 1 Low Very High
 I&C 1,2 Low High

Based on Table 5.37, the pool water inventory and storage racks have the highest SSC importance
rankings. The pool water inventory has a very high SSC importance ranking since, in this
assessment, it is assumed to be the only heat removal system. A backup emergency water sup ply,
such as FLEX equipment, to provide makeup water to the used -fuel storage pool would reduce the
importance ranking. The storage racks have a high SSC importance ranking. Adding another
reactivity control method could reduce this ranking. The high and very high SSC vulnerability
rankings of the reactor building, power, and I&C are similar to those of the core security
assessment.

142

5.6.3.3. ALWR Coolant Purification System Security Assessment
An ALWR coolant purification system has many systems that support the different safety functions.
For this assessment, the generic ALWR contains heat exchangers that provide heat removal. There
are no reactivity controls with this system as fissile materia l is not present in normal operations.
The containment/piping, system isolation, and reactor building support radionuclide retention. The
support systems for the coolant purification system are power and I&C. Each system was analyzed
regarding the coolant purification source to determine SSC importance and vulnerability ranking.
Table 5.38 shows the ranking for each of these systems.

Based on Table 5.38, the heat exchangers have a high SSC vulnerability. This ranking could
potentially be reduced by increasing cybersecurity and providing mitigation strategies in case a
cyberattack occurred, or by reducing reliance on CDAs. For this assessment, it was assumed that
these systems are solely controlled by CDAs with no mitigation for operations. Possible reduction
mechanisms could include requiring multifactor authentication to access the computer systems,
the two-person rule to make changes to the system, and computer s to be accessed in a secured
area with limited access. The other high and very high rankings are similar to those of previous
sections.

5.6.3.4. ALWR Gaseous Radioactive Waste Security Assessment
ALWR gaseous radioactive waste only has two systems that support the safety function of
radionuclide retention. For this assessment, the generic ALWR contains no heat removal, no
reactivity control, and no required support systems for gaseous radioactive waste. The filter
structure and containment support radionuclide retention. Both systems were analyzed regarding
the gaseous radioactive waste source to determine SSC importance and vulnerability ranking. Table
5.39 shows the ranking for each system.

this source term would be screened out as a concern and a security assessment would not be
needed.

Based on Table 5.40, the tank and containment have high SSC importance rankings. For this

stored in different containment locations. This would reduce the rankings because it would
increase the DID of radionuclide retention. The system isolation has a very high SSC vulnerability
ranking due to the number of components and access points. This system contains components
connected to various locations including the reactor building. Since this s ystem has various
external connections, it is susceptible to attack and deserving of the very high SSC vulnerability
ranking. Possible reduction mechanisms include securing the connections between the various
systems via physical protection and cybersecurity protection. Additionally reducing the radi oactive
source term and decreasing the time that the radioactive waste is in liquid form prior to converting
it into a solid state can also reduce the security rankings.

5.6.4. ALWR Risk Reduction and Mitigation Approaches
Based on the security risk assessment, several risk reduction and mitigation approaches have
been identified for the generic ALWR. The details of these are discussed in the sections below.

5.6.4.1. Decay Heat Removal
Two out of three SSCs responsible for removing core decay heat (i.e., passive decay heat removal
and emergency core cooling) have the high-high ranking for their importance and vulnerability
categories. A possible way to ease decay heat removal is by utilizing accident tolerant fuel designs
that increase the cladding’s failure limit , increase the heat transfer rate from fuel to cladding, and
flatten the radial power distribution. A higher cladding failure limit might provide more coping time
for the emergency core cooling system (EECS) to actuate, and/or reduce the ECCS flow rate
criterion allowing the ECCS to cool the core successfully even with a reduced flow rate due to
blockage or sabotage. A higher heat transfer rate across the fuel’s radial cross section reduces
heat accumulation at the center of the fuel that provides another safety margin that can be
capitalized to tolerate a decreased performance of decay heat removal SSCs without fuel melting.
Additionally, tying redundant trains for passive core cooling to large adversary-resistant heat sinks
(water pools, atmosphere) provides increased resistance to adversary action.

5.6.4.2. Backup Water for Pool
The highest SSC importance ranking was for the pool water inventory in the used -fuel storage pool.
A possible risk reduction method for this would be to include a backup water supply in case of an
off-normal situation. This could be something like including FLEX equipment to pump water into the
pool if the water inventory was being removed. Adding a backup water supply would lower the used -
fuel storage pool SSC importance ranking since there would be a DL2 or 3 system and it would
increase the heat removal DID.

5.6.4.3. Access Locations and Cybersecurity Mitigation
The highest SSC vulnerability rankings were for the primary HTS for the core, power and I&C for
multiple sources, and system isolation for liquid and solid radioactive waste. While these systems
are different, they have the same vulnerability risks regarding access points and remote
operations. One possible risk reduction method would be to decrease the number of access points
to these systems via reducing the number of physical locations and the number of systems
connecting to them. Another risk reduction method would be to provide cybersecurity mitigation
strategies. These systems can be controlled remotely, and for this analysis methodology it was
assumed that no physical or cybersecurity protection measures are in place. Therefore, providing
cybersecurity protection and mitigation could decrease the rankings as well.

5.6.4.4. Backup Reactivity Controls
The shutdown rods and the used-fuel pool storage racks both contained a high SSC importance
ranking. Both systems provide the safety function of reactivity control. Therefore, to reduce the
ranking, an additional reactivity control mechanism would be advantageous. This could include
adding a strong neutron absorber to the core or used -fuel storage pool in the case of an off -normal
scenario. The goal would be to add a DL3 system to the reactivity control for the core and a DL2
system to the reactivity control for the used-fuel storage pool.

146

5.6.4.5. Design Attributes
As discussed for many specific applications in Section 5.6.3. , risk reduction and mitigation
approaches could lessen the risk associated with multiple components depending on the design of
the reactor. These design attributes could include placing high , important SSCs and systems in
locations with limited access. This could include requiring badging, passwords, and biometric
verification. These same requirements could be used for both physical access to buildings as well
as access to computer systems. Another design attribute to consider is reducing the amount of
radioactive material or changing material form in specific locations. By reducing the amount of
material or form in a single location, the risk associated with that material should be reduced since
the consequences associated with it will likely be reduced. Last, reducing gaseous radioactive
material would also reduce risk since it is so easily dispersible. While eliminating it completely
would not be feasible, limiting the location of the waste and the associated piping as well as
implementing isolation mechanisms to redu ce dispersibility should reduce the risk of this source
term.

5.8. MICROREACTORS
5.8.1. Microreactor Technology Overview
This section is focused on microreactors and the various designs emerging within the nuclear
power industry. The many microreactor concepts and designs that exist today use and modify
existing nuclear reactor technologies such as HTGRs as well as introduce new technology and ideas
for these small-footprint, lower-power output, compact, and transportable designs. Many
companies designing and building microreactors favor keeping technology and components similar
to those used in current and AR designs due to experience, familiarity of the components, and the
fact that the licensing and approval process tends to be streamlined with this approach. While
similar to other designs, microreactors are more compact with smaller source terms. As
documented in the Nuclear Energy Alliance (NEA) Small Modular Reactor Dashboard Report (OECD
2023), many of the more mature conceptual reactors are either still early in the design process or
have yet to engage in the regulatory licensing process and , as such, have very little design
information available for review and analysis. Based on the lack of outward -facing information, a
technology-specific microreactor analysis was not performed in detail but was scaled down in
theory from the SMR parent technology analysis. An abbreviated heat-pipe reactor high-level
analysis was performed for parity and heat -pipe technology understanding. Due to the compact
nature of these technologies, further microreactor design analysis would prove useful when
sufficient design information is available. Some of the current microreactor concepts and designs
are listed in Table 5.42.

Table 5.42. Microreactor technologies.

149

5.8.1.1. Current Designs
There are three main types of microreactor technologies: H TGRs, heat-pipe reactors, and MSRs.
Table 5.42 provides a cross-cutting taxonomy of current technologies under development including
basic reactor types, power levels, and developers.
Figure 5.52 represents a typical microreactor technology. Most microreactor technologies under
development will have minimal onsite front-end or back-end fuel cycle activities due to their
modular designs and design life being shorter than that of SMRs. The information available on
current designs lacks detail for any online cleanup systems that could represent non -core source
terms and for that reason are not depicted. However, that is subject to change as more information
becomes available. Because most microreactor technologies are very scaled -down versions of
larger SMR technologies discussed in this study, the systems and source term analysis for SMRs is
referenced in most cases.

Figure 5.52. Microreactor block diagram.

HTGRs are under development by companies such as BWXT, Radiant, Ultra Safe Nuclear, and
HolosGen. These reactors use HTGR technology and can provide process heat, electric power, or
both. They typically employ TRISO fuel, which consists of fuel particles coated with multiple layers
of ceramic materials. HTGRs are designed for a nominal lifetime of 5 to 6 years before core skid
replacement and use helium gas as a coolant. These reactors have a passive heat removal system
and employ mechanical means for reactivity control. They are designed with inherent safety
features and meet standard shipping requirements for rail, trucks, and ships. HTGRs offer a range
of power outputs, such as 1.9 MWth (thermal megawatts) in the case of Radiant's Kaleidos reactor

150

(Radiant Industries n.d.) up to 50 MWth in the case of BWXT's Advanced Nuclear Reactor (BANR)
(Nygaard 2021). See Section 5.4 for more information on HTGR technology and potential sources
and systems that could benefit from them.
Another dominant design for microreactors is the MSR, which is utilized by Copenhagen Atomics
and Brigham Young University. Their designs use molten salts to transfer heat from the reactor
core. The molten salts can function as coolants or both fuel and coolants. MSRs can produce
electricity or support industrial processes. More information regarding general MSR technology can
be found in Section 5.1.
A very different reactor type based on a proven but older technology for heat transfer is the heat-
pipe reactor. Heat-pipe designs are planned for use by Westinghouse ’s eVinci microreactor and
Oklo’s Aurora microreactor. These reactors are designed to be fully factory built, fueled, and
assembled and utilize heat pipes for passive core heat extraction. Heat pipes are devices that
transfer heat through the evaporation and condensation of a working fluid, eliminating the need for
mechanical pumps and large-diameter primary loop piping. Westinghouse's eVinci microreactor
(see Figure 5.53) employs a solid monolithic core block with heat pipes for efficient heat transfer.
The reactor can operate at temperatures greater than 650°C and has high -grade heat capabilities
that can be used for industrial applications such as desalination and hydro gen generation. These
reactors are transportable and have a simplified decommissioning process (Arafat and Wyk 2019).

Figure 5.53. Example of a heat-pipe reactor: Westinghouse’s eVinci microreactor cutaway. Source:
Standish and Nestor (2023).

Overall, HTGRs, heat-pipe reactors, and MSRs represent three prevalent technologies in the field of
microreactors. They are designed with inherent safety features and can provide both process heat
151

and electric power. These types of microreactors aim to provide compact, transportable, and
versatile solutions for power generation and other applications. Based on the difference s from
other SMR technologies evaluated in this report, heat-pipe reactors, specifically the heat-pipe heat
transfer and removal system itself, are discussed in sections below.

5.8.1.2. Footprint
The physical footprint of these reactors varies among different designs , with each offering unique
advantages in terms of size and portability.
Some microreactor designs, such as the HolosGen generators, are specifically engineered for a
reduced physical footprint. These reactors are designed to be compact and easily transportable,
fitting inside ISO containers. The Holos -Quad configuration, for example, has a base capacity of 3
MWe, which can be scaled up to 13 MWe. On the other hand, the Holos Titan configuration offers
an even larger capacity of 81 MWe, fitting inside four ISO containers. These microreactors will
provide an efficient use of space and be suitable for rapid deployment scenarios.
Similarly, the Westinghouse eVinci microreactor is designed with a small site footprint in mind.
With its simplified design and minimal moving parts, this heat -pipe-based reactor offers a compact
solution for remote locations. It can be transported by road, rail, or sea, providing flexibility in
deployment. The reactor’s design eliminates the need for large-diameter primary loop piping and
additional equipment associated with active systems, further reducing its physical footprint ( Arafat
and Van Wyk 2019).
In contrast, other microreactor designs prioritize safety features and underground installation,
resulting in a larger physical footprint. Ultra Safe Nuclear ’s Micro Modular Reactor (MMR) Energy
System, for instance, is designed as an underground reactor to provide excellent protection against
terrorism. While the exact size and capacity have not yet been specified, the MMR is composed of
multiple modules and has a footprint of approximately 130 × 96 m². This design ensures enhanced
safety and security but at the cost of a larger physical space requirement ( Ultra Safe Nuclear n.d.).
Overall, microreactor designs exhibit a range of physical footprints, enabling flexibility in
deployment and meeting various application requirements. From compact and transportable
systems that fit inside ISO containers to underground installations with e nhanced safety features,
these reactors offer diverse solutions for different energy needs. Their smaller physical footprints
make them suitable for deployment in remote locations, providing clean and reliable power without
the need for extensive infrastructure. The consequences and considerations for what these smaller
footprints mean in terms of security will be discussed later.

5.8.1.3. Output
Microreactors offer a range of energy outputs, providing both electrical (MWe) and thermal (MWth)
power. These compact reactors are designed to meet various energy demands, making them
suitable for diverse applications. Typical electrical power levels for microreactors range from a few
megawatts to tens of megawatts. These reactors will be able to generate electricity to power small
communities, military installations, remote industrial sites, or critical infrastructure. The electrical
power output of microreactors will be tailored to the specific needs of the application, ensuring a
reliable and consistent energy supply.

152

In addition to electrical power, microreactors can also produce thermal energy, which can be
harnessed for a variety of purposes. The thermal power output of microreactors ranges from a few
megawatts to several tens of megawatts. This thermal energy can be used for district heating,
desalination plants, industrial processes, or other applications that require high -temperature heat.
By utilizing the waste heat from the reactor, microreactors can maximize their energy efficiency and
provide additional benefits beyond electricity generation.
The balance between electrical and thermal power output varies depending on the design and
intended use of the microreactor. Some reactors prioritize high electrical power output, while
others focus on maximizing thermal power for specific applications. Th e flexibility of microreactors
allows for customization for the energy requirements of the end users, ensuring optimal utilization
of the generated power.
Overall, microreactors offer a versatile energy solution by providing both electrical and thermal
power. Their capacity ranges from a few megawatts to tens of megawatts, making them suitable for
various applications. Combining electrical and thermal power output enables microreactors to meet
the energy demands of remote locations, critical infrastructure, and industrial processes,
contributing to a more sustainable and reliable energy future.

5.8.1.4. Fueling and Refueling
Microreactors are designed to operate for extended durations. They are fueled at the factory and
minimize or eliminate the need for frequent refueling while maximizing energy output. The specific
operating durations and refueling intervals vary depending o n the reactor design, fuel type, and
power output requirements. Regulatory guidance (see Figure 5.54) is in draft for the NRC to
effectively and efficiently perform necessary oversight and reviews of factory -fueled and
transportable microreactors (U.S. NRC 2023).

Figure 5.54. Generic microreactor factory-fabricated deployment model. Source: U.S. NRC (2023).

153

Generally, microreactors are engineered to operate for several years without the need for refueling.
This extended operating duration ensures a consistent and uninterrupted energy supply by
reducing the downtime and maintenance associated with refueling ac tivities. By optimizing fuel
utilization and efficiency, microreactors can provide long -term power generation while minimizing
operational disruptions.
The frequency of refueling will vary depending on factors such as the reactor’s power output and
fuel type. Some microreactors are designed to operate for 1 or 2 decades without refueling, while
others may require refueling every few years. The choice of fuel and its characteristics, such as
enrichment level and burnup, can significantly impact the refueling interval. To enhance the
operating duration, microreactors often incorporate advanced fuel designs and materials such as
HALEU and TRISO fuel types. These innovations aim to increase fuel efficiency and extend the time
between refueling. By leveraging advanced fuel technologies, microreactors can operate for
prolonged periods, reducing the overall operational costs and enhancing the reliability of the po wer
generation system.
The extended operating durations and infrequent refueling requirements of microreactors make
them attractive for various applications. They provide a reliable and continuous energy source,
particularly for remote and isolated locations where frequent refue ling would be impractical or
costly. By minimizing the refueling needs, microreactors can deliver long -term power generation,
supporting critical infrastructure, communities, and industrial processes efficiently and
sustainably.

5.8.1.5. Sources of Radioactivity
Due to the comparatively small fuel loadings (source term) of microreactors, the present a much
more compact technology package and in general have less secondary non -core radionuclide
systems and subsequent inventories. The compact nature and shortened plant life cycle before
return to the vendor for replacement and/or refurbishment are key aspects of the limited waste
streams and associated systems that would present non -core sources of interest. As designs
mature and additional nonproprietary information becomes available, non-core waste streams and
retention mechanisms may become sources of interest ; however, the compact nature of these
reactors still requires a small footprint and hence a possible target of interest.

5.8.1.6. Heat Removal
Microreactors employ diverse methods for heat removal, with passive heat removal serving as a
fundamental safety feature incorporated in every design option. In addition to this standard
practice, the focus on plant shutdown heat removal is essential in mi croreactor designs to ensure
meltdowns are effectively prevented and walk-away plant safety protocols are upheld. By
integrating robust and reliable mechanisms for both regular and emergency heat removal,
microreactor designs can guarantee optimal performa nce and mitigate potential risks associated
with heat buildup. Most of the designs currently in early development rely on passive system
cooling measures in contrast to the active cooling systems found in modern LWR plants. These
passive cooling systems warrant further analysis related to plausible radiological sabotage
pathways and resulting issues in a future study.

154

5.8.1.7. Reactivity Control
Microreactors incorporate multiple methods for reactivity control, ensuring the safe and stable
operation of the reactor. These control systems, often referred to as control elements, are
extremely important as they need to be functional and effective regardless of the reactor ’s or
plant’s state. Robust and reliable reactivity control mechanisms are vital to maintain the desired
power levels, prevent excessive reactivity, and respond to various operational scenarios such as
startup, shutdown, and transient conditions. The versatility and dependability of these control
elements contribute to the overall safety and performance of microreactors. Most current designs,
such as Aurora and eVinci, utilize control drums and can combine those drums with a single rod or
multiple shutdown rods where necessary to ensure the reactor is safely shut down during off-
normal conditions.

5.8.1.8. Physical Barriers
Physical barriers are necessary for many reasons related to nuclear safety and security.
Radionuclide containment, in terms of keeping harmful source terms inside the plant where they
are contained, as well as preventing adversaries from gaining unauthoriz ed access inside of the
plant, are reasons to have multiple levels of physical barriers. Most designs incorporate fuel
cladding or monolithic encapsulation of the fuel, followed by an overall surrounding structure
containing the fuel, and finally a canister containment-type system. Ensuring a robust, physical
layered structure is necessary when designing a microreactor due to the plant’s small footprint and
potentially the reduced available geographic area in urban or industrial deployments. Where
geographic limitations would preclude effective physical barriers, design considerations should be
evaluated for below-grade deployments to preclude vehicle-borne avenues that could challenge
plant integrity.

5.8.1.9. Remote Monitoring and Software
To be able to site microreactors in remote or temporary locations with little to no infrastructure is a
major motivation behind their design, and monitoring and receiving information about the plant’s
status will be required. The solution for this is to give the plant the ability to stream information
and plant analytics to one or many centralized sites that act as remote plant -monitoring centers.
While this functionality aims to solve the issue of how to stay up to date with the plant’s status, it
brings in security concerns that could be potential vulnerabilities to the plant. More information on
cybersecurity considerations and methods to minimize vulnerabilities can be found in the following
section.

5.8.2. Microreactor Consequence and Security Risk Assessments
5.8.2.1. Background
Microreactors are crucial in satisfying the need for compact, portable, and safe carbon -free power
generation in applications ranging from disaster relief, remote military operations, space power, to
backup power (Guillen et al. 2019). They are characterized by their compact form factor, rapid
deployment capabilities, and low-power outputs in the kilowatt to lower megawatt range (0.1 –50
MWe). Other benefits include load-following capabilities, seamless integration with renewables,
and low downtimes. These design features distinguish microreactors from conventional reactors
and near-term SMRs, particularly because of their unique applications.
155

Microreactor development for civilian applications is being spearheaded by the U.S. D OE
Microreactor Program (MRP) to directly reduce the technology risks and uncertainties related to
demonstration and deployment (Idaho National Laboratory 2021). MRP technical areas are
organized to broadly support microreactor development, demonstration, and licensing processes.
Program stakeholders (excluding end users) include commercial microreactor developers and the
Department of Defense. By 2025, the MRP aims to achieve technological breakthroughs on key
microreactor technologies and enable successful technology maturation pathways.
Microreactors are expected to be initially utilized in remote or off -grid locations (Sterbentz et al.
2017). A recent study by Aumeier et al. (2023) on the U.S. markets for microreactors specified
potential markets in the states of Alaska and Wyoming, the two least populated states in the
country where compact or mobile microreactors could provide great utility in civic, commercial, and
defense applications. The commercial opportunities identified include niche markets with high
energy costs, energy-intensive industries, value-added materials manufacturing, process heat
generation, and decarbonization-policy-driven markets. Applications in these markets include
mining operations, seafood-processing plants, deepwater ports, and data centers. Separate
economic analyses identified the cost drivers (Abou Jaoude et al. 2021) and showed microreactors
are cost competitive with systems of similar size such as diesel generators and renewable sources
in microgrids (Testoni, Bersano, and Segantin 2021).
Among other SMRs and microreactors, the heat-pipe microreactor (HPMR) concept in particular
offers unique advantages compared to conventional LWRs (Guillen 2023) and other AR designs by
utilizing heat pipes with liquid alkali metal working fluids to harvest the heat generated in the
reactor core. Heat pipes can be classified as high -efficiency passive two-phase heat transfer
devices, which operate through the cyclic evaporation and condensation of a working fluid (Faghri
2016). A schematic describing the operating principle of heat pipes is shown in Figure 5.55. The
working fluid evaporates in the evaporator, and the vapor later travels toward the condenser,
where it condenses and returns to the evaporator via the aid of capillary forces and/or gravity.

Figure 5.55. A schematic describing heat-pipe operation. Source: Yilgor, Lan, and Shi (2022).

156

There is a diverse range of applications for heat pipes including nuclear microreactors (Poston et
al. 2020; Levinsky et al. 2018), electronics (Lee, Mudawar, and Hasan 2013), solar thermal plants
(Liao and Faghri 2016; Bienert 1980), furnaces (Vidal and Cooper 1969), heat exchangers (Jung
and Boo 2014), and turbines (Yamawaki et al. 1998). The advantages of HPMRs mainly arise from
their compact size, passive operation, and the elimination of intricate coolant pumping systems,
which also enable increased reliability.

5.8.2.2. Heat-Pipe Reactor Specific Sources of Radioactivity
The sources of radioactivity associated with a n HPMR are very limited due to the passive heat
transfer and power generation technologies employed and the compact nature of the design. The
reactor core is the largest source of radioactivity and presents the most substantial consequence
to radiological sabotage (see Figure 5.56 and Table 5.43).

Figure 5.56. Heat-pipe microreactor radar plot of reactor core source term.

by multiple inherent or mix of inherent/applied barriers while the “High” ranking is characterized by no inherent barrier
or a single applied barrier. Section 4.3. provides a more detailed description of these rankings.

Other non-fuel but core sources could include reactor cavity backfill gas activation products, heat -
pipe liquid alkali metal activation products, and reactor structural core component activated
materials. Based on the compact and coupled nature of the HPMR, and the very small dose
potential contribution, these sources are not reviewed separately as part of this study. Future work
could be performed to evaluate the source terms of heat-pipe working fluids separately, but as
presented in the Oklo Aurora license submittal to the NRC for the Aurora HPMR, “The potassium is
sealed inside the heat pipes to facilitate heat -pipe operation, and the heat pipes are designed to
remain sealed for their entire operating lifetime … No further activation analysis is presented in this
157

chapter because the heat-pipe working fluid cannot exit the reactor module and be a hazard to
onsite personnel” (Oklo 2001).

5.8.2.3. General Heat-Pipe Security Considerations
Multiple HPMR designs have been proposed in the literature (Poston et al. 2020; Sterbentz et al.
2017; Swartz et al. 2021). These designs employ various approaches with respect to their purpose,
size, choice of fuel, choice of power conversion system, etc. However, their operation includes heat
pipes that transfer heat from the reactor core to the power conversion system, whi ch is the main
characteristic of HPMRs. Therefore, many concerns related to their safety and security can be
expected to be common. The risks of this design are significantly less than other designs due to the
simplicity of the heat removal mechanisms , which have essentially no chance of mechanical
failure, leading to a system with low vulnerability (Maioli 2019). Related to barriers and potential
for failure, alkali heat pipes operate optimally at vapor pressures ≤0.1 MPa (≤14.5 psi) ; so even at
hot operating conditions, the heat pipes are at low pressures and do not present a concern relative
to high-pressure explosions or structural ruptures (Sterbentz et al . 2017).
Security considerations related to HPMRs would largely depend on their use case and siting.
Security threats could be relatively similar to those of conventional reactors for civilian and
commercial applications, with an increased risk of cyberthreats due to the use of modern
technologies and remote operations. Overall, technologies that make microreactors mobile and
operationally flexible must meet or exceed existing safety and security standards of conventional
NPPs. Current regulations may help guide these standards; however, they currently do not offer
clear objectives applicable to highly mobile reactors (Moe 2019).
In this section, the security concerns for HPMRs related to heat -pipe operation are described, and
because a heat pipe is passive and contains no instrumentation control systems subject to critical
digital asset targeting, common cybersecurity -related topics are addressed in Section 0. The
concerns given for operating heat operation focus mainly on factors related to the physics of heat
pipes. Table 5.44 provides the security rankings for the various SSCs that provide a safety
function. The complete security risk tables are found in Appendix A of this report. Figure 5.57
shows the relative location of the major safety SSCs analyzed for microreactors in this report.

5.8.3. Microreactor Risk Reduction and Mitigation Approaches
5.8.3.1. General Considerations
As presented in Table 5.44, the overall risk profile (nuclear safety and security) for microreactors is
relatively low compared to other SMR and LWR technologies. The key areas of concern are
discussed in the following sections. It should be noted that based on the generic assumptions of
fuel types and power conversion units, additional design -specific concerns could develop if a more
discrete and specific analysis was performed.
5.8.3.1.1 Physical Barriers to Radionuclide Retention
As discussed in the general considerations, physical barriers are essential in a protective strategy
for microreactor and HPMR designs. Most designs incorporate fuel cladding or a monolithic
encapsulation of the fuel, followed by an overall surrounding structure containing the fuel and heat
pipes, and finally a canister containment -type system. In the case of the eVinci design, these layers
are surrounded by a fourth layer, referred to as a secure vault system, to protect the unit from
external events (Maioli et al. 2019). Similarly, the Aurora HPMR uses multiple layers of passive
barrier to FP release. The fuel matrix is the first barrier, followed by a steel envelope encapsulating
the fuel called the cell can. Next, a sealed steel capsule surrounds the cell can and is sealed and
enclosed by a final container called the shell.
The risk of unauthorized outside access could lead to possible significant vulnerabilities within the
plant that are meant to provide source term containment. Preventing unauthorized or insider
adversaries access to these plant internal lower -level essential barriers must be properly
protected.” It is important to consider how the greatly reduced footprint of microreactors
potentially increases the difficulty of providing adequate physical protection from outside attacks
as well as for radionuclide containment due to their intended temporary use and the lack of area
available to install such physical barriers. This small and compact nature must be addressed
through the site’s physical security plans (PSP) and methodologies for deterrence and or delay.
Design considerations allowing for subsurface deployments could greatly reduce risk if cooling
systems could support a plant’s safe shutdown objectives through passive cooling , such as through
geothermal means. Additionally, new detection methodologies such as deliberate motion analytics
(source https://ric.nrc.gov/docs/abstracts/russelj -hv-th17-r1.pdf) and denial of task PSP
methodologies should be considered to increase detection and deterrence or neutralization prior to
reaching the shell of the compact technology.
5.8.3.1.2 Passive Heat Removal Systems
The specific credited safety functions of the primary (heat pipe) heat removal and monolithic core
components for passive heat transfer through component mass to the surrounding environment
vary for heat-pipe designs. The Oklo Aurora design utilizes heat exchangers to transfer heat from
the reactor via heat pipes filled with potassium to the power conversion unit filled with sC O2 under
normal operations, and it credits the reactor core enclosure system with passive cooling of the
reactor to the surrounding environment of the reactor building for off -normal conditions where the

160

primary may not be available (Oklo 2001). The eVinci system similarly relies on the normal heat-
pipe transfer of heat through the sodium medium to the power conversion unit during normal
conditions. Both designs describe, at a very high level, direct heat transfer through core
components to external, channeled, naturally buoyancy -driven, passive decay heat removal to the
surrounding environment at a rate greater than the production rate of the reactor upon shutdown.
Without further design information, fully assessing the ultimate heat sink primary (heat pipes and
power converter) and passive conduction and natural convection paths is difficult but still very
important to nuclear safety and security. For the purposes of this analysis, passi ve cooling systems
are considered a medium risk since most designs do not credit any external building heat
dissipation needs and do not require any active or digital control systems. This would stress the
need for robust physical barriers and access controls. Reasonable and plausible adversary
methods for defeating passive safety cooling systems should be studied further, especially
considering the small footprint and compact design of microreactors in general.
5.8.3.1.3 Specific Heat-Pipe Considerations
The main constraint for a heat pipe’s performance can be identified as its operating limits
(Faghri 2016). Conventional heat-pipe analysis considers multiple limits , including the viscous,
sonic, entrainment, capillary, and boiling limits. The limit for a particular case depends on the heat -
pipe geometry, wick structure, working fluid, and operating conditions. Limits being reached during
operation may severely affect a heat pipe’s power throughput, especially under evaporator dry -out
conditions, which could be observed under capillary, entrainment, and boiling limits. Furthermore,
the failure of multiple heat pipes to remove core heat could cause a cascading effect that could
result in the failure of additional heat pipes. However, reactors may be effectively designed
considering this failure mode, such as using adjacent heat pipes to adopt the power increase
without failures (Lawdensky et al. 2020).
Since heat pipes are passive devices, they can normally only be manipulated via their evaporator
and condenser conditions. Therefore, an indirect SSC attack on a reactor’s heat pipes could involve
controlling the power input and output to the heat pipes. This could be in the form of a hijacking of
the reactivity control or power conversion systems to perform unsafe control actions (Antonello,
Buongiorno and Zio 2023). Reactor thermal power being increased from nominal design levels, or
the failure of the power conversion system, could cause higher failure probabilities for the heat
pipes as they might operate closer to their operating limits. Upon the failure of multi ple heat pipes,
the reactor might experience significantly higher temperatures and temperature gradients that
could damage components due to increased mechanical and thermal stresses. An increase in the
heat removal rate from the heat pipes could also caus e undesirable effects, such as unexpected
coolant outlet temperatures, the reduction of the heat -pipe operating temperature, and the
flooding of the wick in the condenser.
The reactor could be at an increased risk of attack during transients , such as startup and
shutdown. The reactor power ramp rate during the startup process must consider the viscous,
sonic, and frozen startup limits, which are significant at the lower operating temperatures observed
during startup. A ramp rate higher than designed could result in the rapid increase of core
temperature, which could result in structural damage to both the core and the heat pipes. Similarly,
a high cooling rate during shutdown could also result in structural damage due to larger thermal
gradients. Finally, depending on the type of power conversion system, a high cooling rate during

161

shutdown could cause the working fluid to freeze outside of the evaporator region , which could
considerably affect the future startup performance of the reactor.
It should be noted that any sort of significant physical breach of the heat -pipe casing could rapidly
render the heat pipe inoperable. Heat pipes usually operate at pressures that are less than
atmospheric, which means moisture could get in the heat pipe and potentially cause a fire as the
air comes in contact with the alkali meta l (working fluid). In addition, after the pressure is
equalized with the ambient pressure, the working fluid could be lost in vapor form to the
surroundings, which could result in additional safety hazards.
The physical protection of heat pipes by design would be a key design consideration to limit an
adversary’s direct or indirect access to the heat pipes or power conversion unit. Designs using the
encapsulation of the heat pipes to preclude direct adversary access to the heat pipes should be
incorporated through a container shield or shell and possibly a monolithic design that would
provide total protection of the heat pipes. An additional design consideration for the secondary
power conversion units control system is preventing the loss of heat removal or reversal. Finally,
key plant fuel design considerations for a reactor should include a negative temperature coefficient
of reactivity to ensure that heat-pipe failures or an adversary attempting to reverse heat -pipe
performance would intrinsically shut down the reactor. To address additional concerns with
vulnerabilities during startups and shutdowns of the reactor , or with higher than desired heat-up or
cooldown rates of the heat pipes, developers could use physical in addition to I&C limitations for
both reactivity systems and power conversion units to prelude the overheating or freezing of pipe
coolant outside the condenser. Additionally, reactivity shutdown systems by design should employ
redundancy and a separation of systems where possible and use fail -safe loss-of-power shutdowns
for rod insertion for shutdown.

5.10. CONVENTIONAL LIGHT-WATER REACTORS TECHNOLOGY
 OVERVIEW
This section provides an application of the methodology to a conventional large light-water reactors
(LLWRs) to illustrate the relative difference between the security postures for the anticipated
Generation-IV advanced nuclear reactor designs and the technology found in the current light -water
Generation II and III fleet. For the purposes of this analysis, only the radioactive sources of the
core and spent fuel pool were analyzed to align with the current regulatory requirements to prevent
radiological sabotage of the core or spent fuel sabotage.
The LLWR model used was the Lone Pine model, developed by Sandia National Laboratories
(Osborn et al. 2019). Lone Pine is a 1,150 -MWe two-loop PWR. Lone Pine is a generic model that
loosely resembles operational PWRs and was developed for modeling and simulating security
scenarios.

Figure 5.58. Lone Pine Nuclear Power Plant reactor coolant system model . Source: Osborn et al.
(2019).

5.10.1. LLWR Consequence Assessment
The risks considered in this section are similar to those of many reactor designs; we are
considering the core and used-fuel pool, the two largest sources of radioactivity at reactor sites.

165

Table 5.45. Summary of LLWR risk consequence rankings for various sources.
