 Ranking
 Reactor core Very High Low Low
 PWR
 Used fuel storage pool Very High Low Medium

5.10.1.1. LLWR Reactor Core Consequence Assessment
An LLWR core contains a large source of radioactive materials, primarily fission products, but also
short-lived and long-lived activation products. The maximum dose potential is very high due to the
activity of materials in the core. The dispersibility is low as most of the radioactive material is in
solid form and would require additional energy for dispersal. The barrier ranking is low due to the
successive layers to release (i.e., fuel/cladding, coolant boundary, and containment).

Figure 5.59. LLWR core source radar plot.

5.10.1.2. LLWR Used Fuel Storage Pool Consequence Assessment
The maximum dose potential ranking for the used -fuel pool is very high due to the anticipated 12
years of off-loaded fuel. The dispersibility ranking for the pool is low since most radionuclides are
in a solid state and would require significant energy for dispersal. The barrier ranking is medium as
there are fewer barriers to release from the used -fuel pool.

166

Figure 5.60. LLWR used fuel storage pool source radar plot.

5.10.2. LLWR Security Risk Assessment
The security risks in this section were determined to characterize the typical important SSCs to
protect in LLWRs and their security vulnerabilities. By doing so, we can compare the well-
understood security risks posed by current reactor designs and deployments to the advanced
technologies of AR designs. Below are summary security assessment rankings. The complete
security risk tables are found in Appendix A of this report. Figure 5.61 shows the relative location
of the major safety SSCs analyzed for LLWRs in this report.

5.10.2.1. LLWR Reactor Core Security Assessment
A typical LLWR has many systems that support the safe operation of the reactor. In the Lone Pine
model, the fundamental safety function of head removal is supported by the PHTS during normal
operation. During off-normal events, the auxiliary feedwater supports heat removal with the main
steam system, and the emergency core cooling system also provides a pathway for heat removal
from the core. In this analysis, we considered the availability of FLEX equipment to provide an
additional decay heat removal pathway. Reactivity control is accomplished through control rods,
shutdown rods, and boric acid injection. The fuel/cladding, reactor coolant boundary, and primary
containment vessel support radionuclide retention, as well as the containment spray system
provide a mitigating contribution. The support systems for the core are power, I&C, and plant
cooling. Each system was analyzed regarding the core source to determine SSC importance and
vulnerability ranking. Table 5.46 shows the rankings for each system.

Table 5.46. LLWR reactor core security assessment rankings.

NOTE: The defense line value represents the typical use of the SSC in an operational plant according to the defense line

direct importance of that SSC to the saf ety of the plant), and the SSC Vulnerability represents the relative vulnerability

As expected, LLWRs rely on active systems to maintain the safety of the reactor core. The PHTS
(steam system and power generation system) transports heat from the core to be used for
electricity production during operation. The main steam system and auxiliary feedwater system
serve the function to remove excess operational heat from the core during a shutdown initiation
but are limited for long-term operation. The emergency core cooling system can also remove excess
operational heat and provide long-term core cooling for decay heat removal. Both systems rely
heavily on support systems for operation (power, I&C, plant cooling). The dependence on active
168

support functions introduces numerous vulnerabilities, as the support systems are found
throughout the plant site. The passive shutdown rod injection upon a plant safety system signal or
loss of power is a reliable function, inserting in the case of losing power and/or the I&C, while the
control rods and boric acid injection require power and I&C to operate. The retention of
radionuclides relies primarily on passive robust structures designed to resist release in the event
of an accident (coolant boundary and containment). The containment spray functions as a
mitigating system, reducing the airborne concentrations of some fission products in the event of a
release from the core. LLWRs possess numerous system dependencies that often rely on active
systems, which have some degree of redundancy, but result in common focus points, such as
switchgear rooms, piping headers, motor control centers, plant control centers, or dependence on
single non-safety-related support systems (e.g., component cooling water). LLWRs are large and
provide a small security advantage in the distance and possibility of delay features between targets
for an adversary to traverse compared to more compact AR designs. LLWRs typically have several
personnel or equipment access points to the reactor containment , which impacted the rating for
direct SSC attacks; the value is medium for LLWRs versus the low values associated with most
A/SMR designs that offer less access to reactor core areas.

5.10.2.2. LLWR Used-Fuel Storage Pool Security Assessment
A typical LLWR used-fuel storage pool has systems that support the FSFs. Decay heat removal from
the used fuel assemblies is removed by immersion in the pool water inventory, and the pool cooling
system transfers this heat to the plant service water system and the ultimate heat sink. The
conceptual LLWR for this study was anticipated to have FLEX equipment connections for cooling
the used-fuel pool. Reactivity control in the used -fuel pool is primarily controlled by the fuel racks
with engineered controls that do not allow misplacement of a single fuel assembly , which would
create a critical configuration. Administrative controls would also be in place to prevent criticality.
Radionuclide retention is primarily accomplished by the fuel form and cladding and the retention of
solid radionuclides within the pool.

169

Table 5.47. LLWR used-fuel storage pool security assessment rankings.
  Safety Function Structures, Systems and Defense SSC SSC
 Components Line Importance Vulnerability
 Heat removal Pool water inventory 1 High Medium
 Pool cooling system 1 Medium Very High
 Pool water makeup 2 Medium Very High
 FLEX 3 Low High
 Reactivity control Storage racks 1 High Medium
 Administrative controls 1 Low Medium
 Radionuclide Fuel/cladding 1 High Low

 Pool water 1 High Low
 Reactor building 3 Low High
  Support systems Power 1, 2 Medium Very High
 I&C 1, 2 High Very High
 Plant active cooling 1, 2 Medium Very High

The most important SSCs for protecting the used -fuel pool are the pool water inventory, the fuel
storage racks, and the fuel cladding. The fuel pool inventory is assumed to be sufficient to prevent
fuel damage for 2 days, after which active cooling or makeup water would be required. Loss of pool
inventory would require intervention sooner. The fuel storage racks should be one point safe to
prevent a criticality accident but provide the primary protection for reactivity control. As the support
systems would sustain the used-fuel pool cooling system and makeup water, their importance
mirrors the importance of protecting those systems.

5.10.3. LLWR Risk Reduction and Mitigation Approaches
The security risk associated with LLWRs has been well studied. Limits to implementing SeBD exist
due to the built nature of the facility. SSeBD insights are possible, given the installation of
additional FLEX-type connections and equipment to provide addit ional DID (Christian et al. 2022).
Some Generation-III+ LLWR designs exhibit passive safety performance for several days with the
use of gravity-driven water supplies, the use of natural convective cooling, and by shedding decay
heat to the atmosphere. Limiting access to the reactor containment and spent fuel pool area
though SeBD changes would increase the facility robustness.

5.10.4. LLWRs Compared to A/SMRs
The evolution of commercial nuclear reactor safety has led to greater use of and dependence on
passive safety systems. This is clearly seen in the control of heat removal fundamental safety
function of the security risk matrices. High and very high importance for protection are placed on
the emergency core cooling system and the support systems in the LLWR core matrix vs. the core
for A/SMRs. Ultimately, the emergency core cooling system relies on the decay heat removal
system to shed decay heat to the ultimate heat sink, a path that requires pumps, monitoring, and
cooling to accomplish the fundamental safety function. Decay heat removal in A/SMRs is
accomplished through passive, often always connected, natural convective heat transfer to the
170

atmosphere that does not rely on support systems or plant control system action. In general, an
A/SMR’s systems rankings, lower reliance on support systems, and smaller source terms compared
to those of an LLWR provide a lower risk profile. When coupled with SeBD incorporated into their
design, this inherent, lower risk profile could further reduce security risks and therefore mean that
A/SMRs will not need as substantive a PPS, such as that required for LLWRs.

6. DISCUSSION ON VARIABLES OF RISK THAT ARE NOT TECHNOLOGY
 DEPENDENT

6.1. CYBERSECURITY CONSIDERATIONS
Microreactor and SMR designs are expected to utilize automated control systems that consist of
modern digital components. In fact, some designs proclaim autonomous or semi-autonomous
operation capabilities. Although not studied as part of this methodology, the broader non -CDA
aspects of external cyberattack on control systems are germane to a developer and end user. This
section provides a discussion and the research articles and tools that are available for addressing
the broader aspects of cyberattacks.
Cybersecurity regulations are currently in place for existing NPPs; however, they must be adapted
to consider the unique threats associated with these novel technologies. This study primarily
evaluated the cyber risk of technologies based on a critical digital asset’s availability to an
adversary or active insider; however, a discussion on the broader cybersecurity picture as it relates
to SMRs and microreactors is prudent. Sabharwall et al. (2021) presented a review of the
cybersecurity concerns, threat identification, and threat mitigation strategies that are relevant to
microreactor technologies. They also proposed recommendations for future research on concepts
such as autonomous cyber defense systems and digital twins that could utilize artificial intel ligence
and machine learning.
Digital systems are vulnerable to cyberattacks unlike legacy analog systems, and they are also
prone to malware and other malicious activity that could affect other systems in the network. In
addition, integrating uniform digital systems with standardized software platforms increases
adversaries’ familiarity with the systems present in different designs. Furthermore, complex
systems are usually more prone to contain errors that could be exploited. Therefore, rigorous
cybersecurity measures for both external and internal threats must be employed as a part of
designing microreactors.
Cyber threats to autonomous and semi-autonomous systems could be in the form of malware ( e.g.,
viruses, worms, trojans, and rootkits), human error, remote access, and advanced persistent
threats, which might affect reactor systems in a variety of damaging ways. I &C components are
particularly prone to attacks since they receive signals and interact with physical systems that
could be critical to the reactor’s operation. For instance, the hijacking of a control loop that
regulates important parameters such as temperature, pressure, or flow rate could seriously
compromise safe reactor operation.
To address these threats, Sabharwall et al. (2021) proposed multiple potential cybersecurity
solutions that include but are not limited to:
 • Commercial-grade or stronger encryption schemes
 • Wireless signal shielding and highly directional signals
 • Segmented wireless connections
 • One-way connections
 • Automated intrusion detection systems
 • Autonomous cyber defense systems
172

 • Evaluating vulnerabilities and developing threat mitigation steps via digital twin s
 • Semi-autonomous instead of fully autonomous control systems that allow human
 intervention during perceived attacks
 • Regularly updated antimalware software
 • A robust network of security checks and gates to prevent unauthorized access
 • Proper cybersecurity habits by employees
 • Promoting cooperative engagement for microreactor manufacturers, government agencies,
 and international organizations against cyber threats .
The safety and reliability of microreactors and SMRs are necessary both for their utility and the
future of the nuclear energy sector at large. Employing the solutions presented above starting with
the design phase will enable increased cybersecurity for microreactors. An assessment hub based
on the proposed solution is shown in Figure 6.1, where a digital twin, control feedback, and human
operator are integrated alongside a nearly autonomous control system (Sabharwall et al. 2021).

Figure 6.1. Nearly autonomous cybersecurity assessment hub . Source: Sabharwall et al. (2021).
Additional approaches to cyber-informed engineering are available to aid in design ing and
implementing a cybersecurity program and building cybersecurity into the process of engineering.
The Cyber-Informed Engineering Implementation Guide developed by the National Nuclear Security
Administration for application in the Integrated Project Team design phase also provides guidance
(Wright et al. 2023).

173

6.2. INSIDER THREAT MITIGATION
The advent of microreactors and SMRs is anticipated to capitalize on advanced safety systems ,
security systems, and control systems that may afford reduced staffing and possibly remote
operation. These advances, including the potential for a smaller number of operating staff and a
smaller footprint for facilities, amplify the need to ensure the reliability and integrity of the plant
operations and security staff. Although insider threat is not specifically analyzed as part of this
study, the following discussion is provided for context, and as a resource for end users to further
relate to the importance of providing plant personnel with direct access to control systems and
systems.
Insider threats are a significant concern for any organization but especially those operating within
the nuclear realm, where security risks can have severe consequences (Healey 2016). Threats may
arise when employees, contractors, or other authorized pers onnel abuse their access to sensitive
information, systems, or materials. To mitigate these risks, it is crucial to establish comprehensive
security policies and procedures that address access controls, personnel screening (or vetting
processes), monitoring, and reporting requirements. Additionally, regular training programs should
be implemented to raise the awareness of employees and contractors to the potential
consequences of insider threats and to encourage them to report any suspicious activity. Using
technology-based solutions, such as access controls (role -based access, password protection,
badge access, etc.), biometrics (hand geometry, fingerprint recognition, etc.), and behavioral
analytics (data transfer analysis, monitoring network traffic, etc. ), can also be effective in detecting
and preventing insider threats. Ultimately, a proactive approach to insider threat mitigation is
essential to protecting AR facilities from the potential damage that could be caused by these
security risks (IAEA 2020).
Establishing comprehensive security policies and procedures is critical to mitigating insider threat
risks in AR facilities. The policies and procedures should address access controls to ensure that
only authorized individuals can access sensitive information, systems, and materials. This includes
implementing access controls, such as passwords, biometrics, smart ca rds, or a combination of
these methods. Personnel screening (or vetting processes) is another important aspect of insider
threat mitigation. This involves conducting background checks on employees and contractors,
which can help identify any past criminal activity or behavior that may indicate a potential insider -
threat risk. It is also crucial to monitor employees and contractors for any suspicious activity, such
as accessing sensitive information outside of their job responsibilities or attempting to bypass
security controls. Monitoring is possible through physic al security measures, such as surveillance
cameras and security personnel, or through technical security measures, such as audit logs
(employee activity, file access, log in / log out attempts, etc. are recorded for further analysis) and
network monitoring tools (IAEA 2020).
Reporting requirements should also be established to encourage employees and contractors to
report any suspicious activity they observe. This can include reporting to their supervisors, security
personnel, or through anonymous reporting channels. It is ess ential to ensure that employees and
contractors understand the importance of reporting suspicious activity and the potential
consequences of failing to report. Regular training programs are also critical in raising the
awareness of employees and contractors to the potential consequences of insider threats. These
training programs should cover topics such as the types of insider threats, how to identify

174

suspicious activity, and how to report it. The training should also provide employees and
contractors with an understanding of the facility ’s security policies and procedures, access
controls, and reporting requirements (IAEA 2020).

6.2.1. Insider Risk and Nuclear Facilities
Risk can vary throughout a nuclear facility; however, the risk that insider threat poses to safety,
security, and emergency preparedness, or to the theft of nuclear material, may have grave
consequences. Insiders at a nuclear facility with legitimate access and knowledge of its systems
can exploit their privileges to compromise a facility’s integrity. For instance, an insider could
engage in unauthorized system access by using their credentials to gain entry. Once inside, they
might manipulate critical settings, disable safety features, or introduce malicious code,
endangering the functionality of the system. Additionally, insiders with physical access to the
facility could sabotage a critical system, such as the passive decay heat removal system, by
tampering with equipment, disabling safety mechanisms, or causing physical damage to critical
components (IAEA 2020). These actions can lead to system failure or create hazardous conditions
that jeopardize the facility’s ability to cool the reactor given an emerg ency or loss-of-coolant flow
condition (IAEA 2020). Lack-of-coolant flow could damage fuel.
To detect and mitigate such threats, behavior observation and close collaboration with the cyber
team, alongside other teams such as HR, are also vital. Timely reporting of such observations to
the cyber team enables it to monitor the insider’s cyber activities, analyze digital footprints, and
detect any unauthorized actions. By combining behavioral observation and cyber monitoring, the
facility can strengthen its defense against insider threats. This collaborative approach, integrating
behavior observation, alongside other observations, with technical cybersecurity measures, helps
facility personnel identify and address risks such as unauthorized system access and physical
sabotage, ensuring the safety of the passive decay heat removal system and overall facility
integrity (CISA 2020).

6.2.2. How to Identify an Insider
When looking for a suspicious insider, there are many signs that may indicate a potential insider
threat risk (IAEA 2016). These can include changes in an employee ’s behavior or attitude, such as
a sudden increase in stress, irritability, or aggression. Other signs may include unauthorized access
to sensitive information or systems, unexplained absences or late work hours, or an employee
expressing negative feelings toward the organization or their colleagues. Monitoring an employee ’s
activity can also reveal potential insider threats. This can include monitoring network activity, file
access, and emails to detect any unusual or suspicious behavior. It is essential to review audit logs
(i.e., employee activity, file access, and log in / log out attempts, etc. are recorded for further
analysis) regularly to identify any unauthorized access attempts or other signs of malicious activity.
Another warning sign may be an employee who suddenly acquires a large amount of wealth beyond
what their salary would suggest they should have, as this may indicate that they have engaged in
illicit activities, such as selling sensitive information to outsiders, that lead to wealth accumulation.
It is crucial to remain vigilant and report any suspicious activity immediately to the appropriate
authorities. By monitoring employee behavior, reviewing audit logs, and encouraging employees to
report suspicious activity, organizations can identify and mitigate insider threat risks before they
can cause harm (CISA 2020).

175

6.3. SUMMARY
The risk of insider threats in AR facilities is a concern that can be mitigated through comprehensive
security policies and procedures, including access controls, personnel screening, monitoring, and
reporting requirements. To complement these measures, it is important to implement regula r
training programs to raise awareness on how to identify an insider threat and the potential
consequences of insider threats, and to motivate employees and contractors to report any
suspicious activities. To detect potential insider threats, it is essential to monitor employee
behavior, including network activity, file access, and emails, and to review audit logs regularly to
identify any unauthorized access attempts or other signs of malicious activity. Changes in an
employee’s behavior or attitude, unauthorized access to sensitive information or systems, and
sudden wealth acquisition are all warning signs that can indicate a potential insider threat.
Organizations must remain vigilant and report any suspicious activity imm ediately to the
appropriate authorities to mitigate insider threat risks before they can cause harm (CISA 2020).

7. OBSERVATIONS, GAPS, AND CONCLUSION

7.1. OBSERVATIONS
A/SMRs present simpler designs that rely on fewer safety systems, passive or inherent
fundamental safety function fulfillment, and reduced reliance on active support systems. In this,
they differ from the currently deployed Generation II and III LWR designs. Further, A/SMRs employ
a greater use of below-grade structures, more inaccessible reactor cells, and digital monitoring and
control systems.
The designs of A/SMRs incorporate new materials and coolants, primarily sodium, irradiated salt,
and graphite, that present different challenges for security events compared to LLWRs. Some of
these materials and coolants present the possibility of increases in security concerns unless
managed by engineered safety (i.e., limiting the exposure of sodium to water or atmospheric
sources by design). Non-water-cooled reactors present additional challenges for implementing FLEX
equipment and methodologies unless water-based heat exchangers are utilized to shed decay heat
from the working fluid.

7.1.1. Ex-Core Source Terms
Many technologies studied have non-core source terms that rise to levels of potential consequence
near that of reactor core source terms. In many designs, sequestrating source terms in process
systems, reducing source term dispersibility through physical change, or strengthening
containment of ex-core source terms in process systems through barriers could provide
significantly increased resistance to a direct adversary attack and dispersion of those source
terms.

7.1.2. Technological Advancements in Control Systems
Most technologies studied, especially microreactors, are anticipated to rely heavily on advanced
and at times remote operations and monitoring systems. These advanced designs have many built -
in features that would naturally preclude significant intentional departure from normal operations
where an adversary could overpower or undercool the operating reactor if the control systems were
compromised. These technologies must employ fundamental shutdown characteristics such as
reactivity feedback from fuel design to compliment robust cybersecurity and insider threat
programs. Incorporating physical interlocks in addition to software interlocks enhances resiliency
and reduces plant control systems risk.

7.1.3. Critical Safety and Strong Source-Term System Locations and
 Protection
A common theme for all technologies studied reiterated the importance of locating critical safety
systems and ex-core source terms behind robust protective barriers. By design in many cases,
these barriers could use natural earthen berms or subsurface deployment to cut down on material
costs for adequate protection from adversary direct access or explosives. Developers should
evaluate based on the safety systems’ importance and source term strength where these systems
should be located and how they can be adequately secured to preclude vulnerability to adversary
attacks, to include vehicle bomb assaults. Adding layers of concrete, protective barriers, more

177

robust (schedule or double-walled) piping, or establishing a further safe-standoff distance is much
less cost up front during the design process than as a design modification during or after licensing.

7.1.4. Passive Decay Heat Removal
A/SMRs rely heavily on passive decay heat removal, but system designs that eliminate common
headers, susceptible dampers or valves, or robustness to direct, indirect, or cyber attacks have not
been defined. The integration of passive decay heat removal from non -core sources of radioactivity
has not been well understood. Designers may decide to exclude this fundamental safety function
from non-core sources based on their analysis of the security bounding time and the time of
irreversible damage that leads to unacceptable release.

7.1.5. Reactivity Control
The reactivity control mechanisms for A/SMRs include traditional control rods, control drums, and
the use of geometry changes via displacement or drain tanks. Some A/SMRs exhibit excess
reactivity similar to LLWRs, while some plan to have very little excess reactivity in the core. These
reactivity control mechanisms may pose challenges in digital control architecture and plant
response compared to analog LLWRs designs. Using HALEU fuel may pose greater reactivity control
concerns for fresh or used fuel in storage

7.1.6. Functional Containment
Most A/SMR designs include a functional containment, successive barriers to restrict the release
of radionuclides versus the traditional low leak rate LLWR containments. Using successive barriers
of less robust design may result in less importance on individual barriers because each provides
DID to the release of radionuclides. Penetrations though these barriers remain as important
aspects of a facility to protect.

7.1.7. Reliance on Support Systems
Most of the A/SMR designs have little reliance on support systems ( e.g., power, I&C, plant cooling)
to maintain the safety of the facility. Some systems that support the FSFs of non-core sources may
rely more on active systems. I&C remains an important aspect of the facility to protect.

7.1.8. Beyond-Design Basis Response Equipment
None of the A/SMR examples researched for this work showed plans to implement a beyond-design
basis-event response similar to FLEX equipment or methodolog ies. Though the safety analysis of
these A/SMRs demonstrates robustness to design basis events, the ability to apply mitigating
measures to prevent or reduce offsite exposure would provide an additional layer of DID in
response to a security event. Designers should consider adding this capability to their design for
any important cooling or power needs to prevent retrofitting the connections like Generation II
and III LLWRs.

7.2. Technology Gaps
In studying the ARs in this report, gaps in the evaluation process were identified that should be
closed through additional research efforts to support safe and secure industry development and

178

deployment of these advanced technologies. Some of the more significant gaps identified that
need to be closed for a clearer picture of the technology risk profiles are described below.

7.2.1. Passive Safety System Performance
The simplicity of passive decay heat removal systems boasts near walk-away safety of design.
However, that does not necessarily translate to substantial resilience to adversary attack of those
systems. As there is very little published to date on sabotage methods for these systems and , due
to proprietary designs, not much information exists on accessibility to these systems, this study
graded these systems as robust in most cases. Further future analysis of what actions are
realistically plausible to sabotage these types of passive systems internally (insider actions) and
externally by direct SSC attack is necessary. Future analysis will help with understanding the
security posture of these new designs and how these passive systems respond to attack scenarios
that could cause a loss of fundamental system functionality.

7.2.2. Dispersibility Models for Advanced Fuels
As part of this generic study of AR technologies, AR fuels such as TRISO were credited with being
nearly impervious as related to dispersibility. There is a great deal of data for a finite analysis with
LLWR fuels; however, information for advanced fuels is relatively limited if not non -existent. Further
studies should be performed to evaluate those advanced fuels and their performance related to a
direct adversary attack. Additional research needs to be conducted on sabotage consequences
associated with AR fuels, to include those using HALEU.

7.2.3. New or Novel Technologies Analysis
With the advent of AR designs, novel methods for heat transfer and compact designs are on the
forefront including microreactors that utilize heat -pipe designs among many other more standard
designs. Additional studies should be performed to ensure that in micro -designs, the compact
nature of the design does not provide an avenue for multiple target sabotage accomplishment from
a single attack scenario. Additionally, non-traditional heat transfer mechanisms , such as heat pipes
and Seebeck-effect direct-energy transfer systems, could benefit from further direct sabotage
effect studies, such as heat transfer reversals and cascading heat -pipe failures.

7.2.4. Source Term Determination
The quantification of mechanistic source terms for ARs is underway, as the U.S. NRC has identified
requirements for limits on offsite releases resulting from licensing basis events from any source of
radioactivity at licensed facilities. These mechanistic source terms are determined following
defined accident progression methodologies to bound the analyses within a realistic framework.
Similar guidance is needed to define the sabotage methods applicable to AR sources of
radioactivity within the bounds of th e prescribed DBT. As defined in the prescribed DBT, adversary
capabilities and equipment can influence the possible security event mechanistic source terms and
could exceed the calculated dose/consequence results from the safety analysis. Clear guidance on
applying the DBT in security events source term determination must be provided by regulators to
define the reasonable bounding source term(s) for consequence analysis.

179

7.3. CONCLUSIONS AND PATH FORWARD
The AR risks study provides a methodology to analyze, generically and qualitatively, differing
reactor technologies based on publicly available information. The study demonstrated that new AR
technologies provide improved levels of safety and inherent design features that result in a lower
security significance as compared to LLWRs. The study provides insights on the importance of
technology-specific (not design-specific) systems of interest and provokes thought on why and how
the system should or could be designed to minimize or lessen risk.
The methodology performed in this study could be refined and modified for use to quantitatively
evaluate any given technology, incorporating methods like PRA or STPA, to further inform a
developer when making decisions related to a tradeoff between additional systems redundancy or
system reinforcement or protection as part of the site protective strategy. The recommended path
forward for this study and its output would be to further refine this tool to be used in a more
comprehensive and detailed manner for end users to evaluate a specific design.
From this assessment, in most cases, a more robust nuclear safety posture could result in
increased levels of resistance to sabotage. Designers can utilize the insights gained in this study,
or as applied to their technology in conjunction with an integrated project team that brings security,
safety, engineering, and operations experts together to leverage security early into the design
process. This study focuses on the inherent technology design aspects of SeBD and not the plant
PPS. Additional information on SeBD and integration with the PPS can be found in the Physical
Protection Recommendations for Small Modular Reactor Facilities (Evans 2021) and the Security-
by-Design Handbook (Snell et al. 2013). This study provides foundational elements to both identify
the systems that could be further protected and enhanced and a way to analyze those changes in
advance of deployment. The nexus of nuclear safety and nuclear security must be considered
wholly in the design process to both provide cost -effective up-front enhanced security and improve
overall safety and security posture of AR technology deployment.

7.4. REFERENCES

 https://www.osti.gov/servlets/purl/1088049.

9. APPENDIX B: Security Risk Assessment Summary Use and
 Application Guide
This use and application guide is intended to provide an overview of the security risk assessment
methodology—its purpose, use, methods, and expected results.

9.1. Purpose and Use
The security risk assessment methodology is intended to provide guidance on identifying nuclear
facility sabotage targets at an early design phase, or from a high level, so SeBD and SSeBD
opportunities can be identified.
SeBD can be defined as an approach to integrating protection-related elements into the design of
nuclear facilities while considering security, safeguards, safety, and operations .
SSeBD is the incorporation of security goals into a new nuclear power plant or facility design ,
resulting in the optimization of safety systems and operational programs to increase the resistance
of the facility to radiological sabotage and reduce potential dose consequences to the public .
Reactor developers may use this security risk methodology to identify those SeBD and SSeBD
opportunities throughout the design process until high -fidelity targets can be determined (target
sets or vital equipment), at which point the PPS should be approaching a final design.
Regulators and potential end users may also use the methodology to assess a facility design for
the inclusion of SeBD and SSeBD concepts, to understand the relative security risk of a facility
without considerations for the application of an operational physical protection program.

9.2. Security Risk Assessment Methodology
The security risk methodology provides a process to determine qualitative ratings for radioactive
source potential offsite consequences, and the relative importance and vulnerability of safety
systems for those sources during security events. The security risk methodology is technology
neutral and flexible in determining bounding parameters (e.g., DBT, security program goals). In the
absence of a specific DBT, it is recommended that users refer to the National Nuclear Security
Administration International Nuclear Security generic DBT commonly used for international training
courses (Sandia National Laboratories n.d.). Users should define the security program goals
consistent with regulatory expectations for the planned deployment (e.g., no offsite release, no fuel
damage, no offsite release exceeding a defined value) or use a conservative value to provide a
bounding assessment.
This methodology is focused on SSeBD characterization and qualification. The methodology
identifies radiological sources in the design that are within the scope of assessment and qualifies
their possible consequences, dispersibility, and barrier to release. The security of each
radionuclide source is evaluated by the examination of the safety systems that support the FSFs:
control of heat removal, control of reactivity, retention of radionuclides, plus control of chemical
interactions, their relative importance of protection by the security systems, and their vulnerability
to attack. The result of the methodology is a heat map of sources and their safety systems

214

qualified in their importance of protection and relative vulnerability to adversary action. The
methodology is captured below in Figure 9.1.

 Design Information

9.2.1. Risk Consequence Assessment
The first step in the security risk methodology is identifying and screening radiological sources that
may need to be protected by the physical protection program. The identified sources are all of
those that could pose a danger to the public given adversary -initiated radiological sabotage, as the
methodology is not intended to assess diversion or theft. Identified sources are qualified based on

215

the maximum potential offsite dose impact, the possible dispersibility based on physical form, and
the radiological control barriers that could retain the possible release of the source.

The maximum dose potential should be determined first, to screen sources that would not exceed
the dose thresholds used for the assessment. Considerations for interfacing or co -located
radioactive sources should be taken into account (e.g., used fuel or wa ste processing or storage).
Those sources that screen into the methodology are assessed for dispersibility and barriers to
release in the consequence assessment and then proceed to the security risk methodology. The
consequence assessment results for each source could be represented as a radar plot diagram, as
seen in Figure 9.2. The radar plot quickly demonstrates the total risk of the radionuclide source
through the area shown within the resulting triangle.

Figure 9.2. Example consequence assessment radar plot.

The qualification values associated with the radar plot are shown in Figure 9.3.

Figure 9.3. Radar plot ranking key.

The consequence assessment values for all radionuclide sources in the facility would be
summarized in a heat map consequence assessment matrix . Table 9.1 shows the consequence
matrix for a generic sodium-fast reactor. The consequence assessment matrix allows a comparative
216

view of the radionuclide sources and their consequence potential, dispersibility, and barriers to
release to risk-inform the safety and security design of the facility.
Table 9.1. Summary of SFR risk consequence rankings for various sources .

9.2.1.1. Maximum Dose Potential
The security risk methodology can be adapted to different regulatory structures. For example, the
U.S. NRC has released draft rulemaking language requiring that the physical protection program for
advanced and small modular reactors “must be designed to prevent a significant release of
radionuclides from any source” consistent with the dose reference values in 10 CFR 50.34 and 10
CFR 52.79. The values in 10 CFR 50.34(a)(1)(ii)(D)(1) and (2) are 25 rem TEDE for 2 hours after
the event at the exclusion area boundary and 25 rem TEDE for 24 hours at the boundary of the low -
population zone.
For the consequence assessment, the dose impact qualification ratings that are captured are
consistent with Table 9.2. These ratings would be used to qualify the doses and provide a risk -
informed picture of potential radionuclide sources. In this example, medium equates to 10 to 200
rem. This rating would be the threshold for potential offsite dose consequences and would start at
40% of the 25 rem value to provide a conservative threshold and avoid cliff -edge effects. A low
rating would equate to a source that ma y not enter the regulated security space but would inform
possible low-dose offsite consequences, or sources that may enter the regulated space if combined
or co-located with other sources. Ratings of high and very high would risk-inform what radiological
sources present the greatest security consequence potentials.

217

Table 9.2. Maximum dose impact ranking structure.

 Ranking Maximum Potential Dose Impact Approximate TEDE Evaluation Level
 Possible wide-area early health Very large doses (large radionuclide
 effects inventories), such as those associated
 Very High
 with full core inventories or multiple
 used fuel assemblies (or equivalent)

 High Possible early health effects 200 rem < x < Very High Rank

 Medium Possible late health effects 10 < x < 200 rem

 Low Possible exceedance of EPA PAG 1 < x < 10 rem

 Screen Below screening limit (EPA PAG) x < 1 rem

9.2.1.2. Dispersibility
The dispersibility rating of radionuclide source rates the risk of dispersal of the radionuclide source
to the environment. Sources with a low rating would likely be solid form and be chemically bonded,
such that significant energy (e.g., explosives, chemical reactions, or heat) would be needed to
disperse the radionuclides. Medium-risk-rated sources would be easier to disperse, would require
less energy or long timeframes to release, and may include liquid sources. High risk of dispersibility
ranking values would equate to sources that would require very little or no energy for dispersal,
such as gaseous radionuclide sources. Table 9.3 captures these ratings.

Table 9.3. Dispersibility factor ranking structure.

 Ranking Dispersibility Characteristics
 High Very limited or no energy needed for dispersal

 Medium Energy needed to initiate dispersal or small amount of continuous energy

 Low Significant energy needed for dispersal

9.2.1.3. Barrier for Dispersion
The ratings for barriers to dispersion are intended to qualify the engineered physical barriers that
would prevent, retain, or mitigate a release of radionuclides. The qualifications are intended to
credit both the typical robust containments with leak rates as well as the multiple-layer functional
containments seen in AR designs. Credit for a barrier is contingent on that barrier meeting the
functional safety goal of retention, and barriers that mitigate would be given less credit in this
assessment. Radioactive sources that have a high qualification for barrier risk have no inherent
barriers to release or have a single applied barrier (i.e., a vessel wall). A medium barrier
qualification would be those sources that have a single inherent barrier (i.e., TRISO) plus an
applied barrier, or multiple applied barriers (e.g., vessel wall and shield barrier). Radioactive
sources with multiple inherent and applied barriers would be qualified to have a low barrier risk

218

value. These definitions are listed in Table 9.4. These barriers may also be security barriers .
Protection from adversaries is not considered in this methodology, only the performance of the SSC
in fulfilling safety objectives and contributing to functional safety.

Table 9.4. Barrier to radionuclide release ranking structure.

 Ranking Dispersibility Characteristics
 High No inherent barriers or a single applied barrier

 Medium Single inherent barrier or multiple applied barriers

 Low Multiple inherent barriers or a mix of inherent/applied barriers

9.2.2. Design Security Risk Assessment
For each radionuclide source that is screened into the assessment methodology, the design
security risk methodology identifies SSCs that meet the FSFs (i.e., reactivity control, heat removal,
radionuclide retention) and that control chemical interactions (e.g., the insertion of oxygen into a
sodium coolant boundary), as well as support systems, and qualifies their relative vulnerability to
adversary action. SSCs that respond to security events may not be required to adhere to strict
safety classifications, so all SSCs that provide functional safety to the radionuclide source shou ld
be assessed for their contribution to the fulfillment of the FSFs.
Initially, SSCs for each source are categorized according to FSFs and then further categorized into
three SSeBD DL levels. The SSeBD DLs are adapted from the five levels of DID for safety purposes
outlined by the IAEA safety reports series (SRS) 46 (IAEA 2005). IAEA’s DID gradation was intended
to meet safety objectives and was adapted for the security risk assessment methodology to credit
safety features for security objectives. The use of SSeBD DLs allows the functional safety of the
design to be mapped to the initial plant behavior and response before detailed design information
is available (e.g., control and safety system logic, fault trees, PRA).
The SSeBD DL1 includes SSCs that meet the FSFs during normal and transient operations. DL2
comprises SSCs that respond to accidents (both within the design basis and beyond the design
basis). DL3 consists of SSCs that provide mitigative actions in response to events. The adaptation
is illustrated in Table 9.5.

219

Table 9.5. Adaptation of SSC defense-in depth-level to security defense lines.
 Defense-in-Depth Levels and Defense Lines

 IAEA SRS-46 DID Levels Error! Bookm ark not d SSeBD Defense Line
 efined.

 and failures

 modes of operation)

 detection of failures

 Level 3: Control of accidents within the DL2: Control of fundamental safety functions in
 design basis response to an adversary-initiated event

 Level 4: Control of severe plant conditions, DL3: Mitigation of loss of fundamental safety
 including prevention of accident progression functions (mitigation to reduce or delay offsite
 and mitigation of the consequences of release)

 consequences of significant releases of consequences to the public should not be
 radioactive materials (offsite emergency credited, but should be considered in the DID
 response) stature of the facility

9.2.2.1. SSC Importance of Protection
Once the SSeBD DL is determined for each SSC that supports a radionuclide source, the
importance of protection qualification value can be determined. The importance of protection value
represents the relative risk the loss or compromise of that system to an adversary would have on
the safety of that radionuclide source. The SSC importance of protection is compiled from four
values: impact on safety function, reliance on support systems, DID risk, and compromise risk.
These values can be tabulated in a design risk matrix. Figure 9.5 shows the link between these
four values and the resulting total SSC importance of protection.

9.2.2.1.1 Impact on Safety Function
The primary factor in determining SSC importance of protection is the impact on safety function
value. The impact on safety function represents the degree to which the source relies on an SSC to
fulfill the fundamental safety function. Table 9.6 shows the ranking structure for the impact on
safety function. Generally, SSeBD DL2 systems (those that the plant relies on to fulfill the safety
function in response to an event) would be ranked high, and SSeBD DL1 systems (those normal-
operation active systems that are generally not credited with response to events), would receive a
low ranking. The contribution of SSeBD DL3 systems to fulfilling FSFs generally varies.

220

Table 9.6. Impact on safety function ranking structure.

 Ranking Safety Function System or SSC Characteristics
 Primary safety function system or SSC, ultimately relied upon to fulfill a safety
 High
 function

 SSC contributes to the fulfillment of a safety function for some period or
 Medium
 significance; contributes to safety function fulfillment as one of a few systems

 SSC may fulfill the safety function, but not under adverse conditions; contributes
 Low
 to safety function fulfillment as one of many systems

9.2.2.1.2 Reliance on Support Systems
The impact on safety function can by modulated by both the reliance on support systems or DID.
For an SSC, increasing reliance on support systems increases the security risk of that SSC and thus
would would result in an increased level of importance of protection. Table 9.7 shows the ranking
structure for reliance on support systems.

Table 9.7. Reliance on support systems ranking structure.

 Ranking Safety Function System or SSC Characteristics

 High Safety function system or SSC relies on three or more support systems

 Medium Safety function system or SSC relies on two support systems

 Low Safety function system or SSC relies on one support system

 None Safety function system or SSC is stand -alone, inherent, passive

9.2.2.1.3 Defense-In-Depth Risk
The SSC impact on safety function value can also be decreased with a lower DID risk ranking,
which indicates there are multiple redundancies for that SSC or diverse methods for the FSF. DID
risk relates to the functional redundancies and diversities, and the increased number of locations
that an adversary may need to access to prevent the safety function. Considerations for common
headers and instrumentation paths should be made to accurately capture the redundancies in the
SSC. The ranking structure for DID is captured in Table 9.8.

221

Table 9.8. Defense-in-depth risk ranking structure.

 Ranking Safety Function System or SSC Characteristics

 High Safety function system or SSC has no DID

 Safety function system or SSC has two or three redundant trains or another fully
 Medium
 redundant diverse system
 Low Safety function system or SSC has multiple redundant or diverse systems/trains
                  (i.e., four)

9.2.2.1.4 Compromise Risk
Compromise risk has a similar importance for determining the importance of protection ranking
value. For this methodology, we define this concept of “compromise” as an adversary using a
facility’s SSC or material against the intended safety function of that SSC or material for the
facility. Though traditionally captured within the concept of sabotage , this is captured separately in
this methodology. For this methodology, t he compromise risk qualifies the potential for the SSC to
be used by an adversary and thus factors equally to the determination of SSC importance of
protection. The characteristics of system or SSC compromise risk consequences are captured in
Table 9.9.

Table 9.9. Compromise risk ranking structure.

 Ranking Safety Function System or SSC Compromise Consequence Characteristics

 High Safety function system or SSC has severe negative outcomes

 Medium Safety function system or SSC has medium negative outcomes

 Low Safety function system or SSC has negligible negative outcomes

 None Safety function system or SSC has no negative outcomes

9.2.2.2. SSC Vulnerability to Attack Vectors
The safety function systems and SSCs that are assessed for importance of protection are then
evaluated for their vulnerability to three different adversary attack vectors. The methodology is
flexible and can be adapted to different DBTs. For the ARC assessment, we used the adversary
characteristics outlined in the Lone Pine Nuclear Power Plant Hypothetical Facility Data Handbook
(Sandia National Laboratories n.d.). The adversary characteristics result in three attack vectors : a
direct attack on a system or an SSC, an indirect attack on a system or an SSC, or a cyber-physical
attack for the delivery of malicious cyber hardware. These attack vulnerabilities are based primarily
on access to the location within the facility, which is based solely on the anticipated facility layout
required to support engineered safety features and operational needs. No security barriers are
credited in the assessment to maintain a security program agnostic method to evaluate the roles of
safety systems and SSCs in security events. Figure 9.4 below shows the primary determining factor

222

in the attack vector value by location within a facility. Other considerations may include time to
sabotage the equipment, or the difficulty of sabotage with adversary tools and equipment.

 Reactor Cell Reactor Building(s) Exterior of/to Reactor

 (Low) (Med) Building(s)

 (High)

Figure 9.4. Attack vector accessibility levels.

9.2.2.2.1 Direct Attack Vulnerability
Direct attacks are assessed as attacks that happen directly to a system or an SSC (adversary
hands on the equipment). Given the available sabotage and compromise methods, an adversary
having direct access would lead to loss of that equipment. The ranking structure for a direct attack
is largely based on relative access by an adversary. The direct attack vulnerability rankings are
captured in Table 9.10.

223

Table 9.10. Direct attack vulnerability ranking structure.

 Ranking Safety Function System or SSC Compromise Consequence Characteristics

 Safety function system or SSC can be defeated quickly by hand -carried
 High equipment or hand manipulation and is very accessible (exterior of nuclear
 island building or buildings)

 Safety function system or SSC can be defeated by hand -carried equipment with
 sufficient time required for the task and has medium accessibility (interior to
 Medium
 nuclear island with personnel access ; includes containment structures with
 doors but not areas that need heavy equipment for access)

 Safety function system or SSC is resistant to hand -carried equipment, takes
 Low extensive time to defeat, and is not very accessible (contained within a
 confinement cell that requires time and heavy equipment to access)

 Safety function system or SSC is beyond adversary capability , or the time to
 None
 defeat is beyond the timeframe established for a security event

9.2.2.2.2 Indirect Attack Vulnerability
Indirect attacks are assessed as attacks that happen to a system or SSC from a remote location.
This may include preventing actuation or function, or compromise. The main control room, remote
shutdown stations, and motor control centers may be locations where an indirect attack may occur.
Adversaries may also use other systems such as a remote overhead crane for indirect attacks. The
ranking structure for an indirect attack is largely based on relative access by an adversary. The
indirect attack vulnerability rankings are captured in Table 9.11.

224

Table 9.11. Indirect attack vulnerability ranking structure.

 Ranking Safety Function System or SSC Compromise Consequence Characteristics

 Safety function system or SSC can be defeated or compromised by other plant
 High systems or from several plant locations that have high accessibility (exterior of
 nuclear island building or buildings)

 Safety function system or SSC can be manipulated or compromised from a few
 plant locations with medium accessibility (interior to nuclear island with
 Medium
 personnel access; includes containment structures with doors but not areas that
 need heavy equipment for access)

 Safety function system or SSC can be manipulated or compromised from the
 Low
 main control room or a single location with medium accessibility

 None Safety function system or SSC cannot be manipulated or compromised remotely

9.2.2.2.3 Cyberattack Vulnerability
Cyberattacks are assessed as attacks that happen to a system or an SSC from a critical digital
asset (i.e., plant computer, control system, digital information device) that the adversary physically
accesses. This may include the manipulation or prevention of functions, spoofing data, or
compromise. The main control room, remote shutdown stations, plant computer centers, or other
digital systems (e.g., network locations) may be locations where a cyberattack can occur. The
cyberattack examined here is limited to the cyber-physical type (deployment of cyberattack
hardware); network or over-the-air remote attack types are not examined. The ranking structure for
a cyberattack is largely based on the degree to which the system or SSC is CDA controlled, and also
the relative access for an adversary. The indirect attack vulnerability rankings are captured in
Table 9.11.
Table 9.12. Cyberattack vulnerability ranking structure.

 Ranking Safety Function System or SSC Compromise Consequence Characteristics

 High Safety function system or SSC is directly controlled by a CDA that is accessible

 Medium Safety function system or SSC relies on CDA information that is accessible

 Safety function system or SSC has minimal connections to or requirements for a
 Low
 CDA, or the CDA is very inaccessible

 Safety function system or SSC has no CDA connections or requirements , or
 None
 access is beyond accessibility

9.2.2.3. Total SSC Importance of Protection and Vulnerability Determination
The total SSC importance of protection and total SSC vulnerability qualification values are
determined from the ratings above. Total SSC importance of protection is determined from impact
on safety function, reliance on support systems, DID risk, and compromise risk, as seen in Figure
9.5.

Figure 9.5. Design risk matrix illustrating the total SSC importance of protection.

The total SSC vulnerability is determined from the direct, indirect, and cyberattack vulnerabilities,
as shown in Figure 9.6

Figure 9.6. Design risk matrix illustrating the total SSC vulnerability.

The total values are arrived at through a set of log ic paths, as demonstrated in Table 9.13. The
SSC importance of protection is primarily determined by the impact on safety function, modulated
by the reliance on support systems and DID, and the compromise risk. The SSC importance of

226

protection is equal to the higher value of compromise risk or the modulated impact on safety
function.

The total SSC vulnerability is equally weighted between direct, indirect, and cyberattack and
represents the highest value. Two high values will result in a very high determination for the total
SSC vulnerability.

Table 9.13. Design risk logic for SSC importance and vulnerability.

The design risk logic from Table 9.13 is further explained below.
SSC importance of protection:
 • SSCs A, C, D, E, H, I show that the resulting SSC importance of protection correlates to the
 qualification in the Impact on Safety Function or Compromise Risk columns (H and I
 specifically show that whichever has the higher qualification gives the result for the SSC
 importance of protection).
 • The qualification values associated with the Reliance on Support Systems and Defense -in-
 Depth Risk columns can affect the resulting value from the impact on safety function to the
 SSC importance of protection. Examples in Table 9.13 are:
 o Reductions for importance of protection:
 ▪ SSC B shows that a medium value for the impact on safety function can be
 modulated to a low value if there are low values for both reliance on support
 systems and defense-in-depth risk. The intent is to place less emphasis on
 SSCs with low reliance on supporting systems and multiple redundant
 trains.
 ▪ SSC G shows that a high value for the impact on safety function can be
 modulated to a medium value if there are none and low values for reliance
 on support systems and defense-in-depth risk. The intent is to place less

227

 emphasis on SSCs that would otherwise be qualified as high, if they have no
 reliance on support systems and multiple redundant trains.
 o Increases for importance of protection:
 ▪ SSC F shows that if both the values for reliance on support systems and
 defense-in-depth risk are high, then the medium value associated with
 impact on safety function is elevated to high for the SSC importance of
 protection.
 ▪ SSC J, K, and L show that if the impact on safety function is qualified as
 high, and if reliance on support systems, defense-in-depth risk, or
 compromise risk values are also high, then the SSC importance of
 protection is very high.
Total SSC vulnerability:
 • The total SSC vulnerability evenly weights direct SSC attacks, indirect SSC attacks, and
 cyber/physical attacks Th(as show in SSCs A-D, and H-L).
 • Any two high vulnerabilities result in a very high SSC vulnerability rating (as shown in SSCs
        E, F, and G).

9.3. RESULTS AND NEXT STEPS
The result of the security risk assessment is a top -down, high-level assessment of a nuclear facility
for security targets, the qualification of the importance of protecting those targets, and their
relative vulnerability to adversary attack vectors. The results are intended to inform the design of
the facility and support SSeBD and SeBD methods. The security risk assessment method ology is
intended to be iterative and updated as designs mature, additional details are made available, and
SSeBD insights are integrated into the design.

The radionuclide sources that are screened in to the assessment will be contained tabulated
similar to Table 9.14. This table would support risk-informing the physical protection program, as
well as SSeBD changes to reduce the rankings of sources. SSeBD changes may include reducing
collected activities to reduce maximum dose potentials, converting more dispersible forms to less
dispersible forms, or increasing the applied radionuclide barriers.

228

Table 9.14. Example risk consequence rankings for various sources.
 Maximum Dose
 Reactor Dispersibility Barrier
 Source Potential
  Type Ranking Ranking
 Ranking
 Example Reactor core Very High Low Low
 Spent fuel storage system Very High Low Low
 Gaseous radioactive waste Low High Low
 system
 Liquid radioactive waste system Low Medium Low
 Solid radioactive waste system High Medium Low

Each radionuclide source tabulated in a table like Table 9.14 would have an associated design
security risk table, as shown in Table 9.15 (showing only the heat removal fundamental safety
function and example support systems). Design security risk tables would summarize the total SSC
importance of protection and total SSC vulnerability in the last two columns but also illustrate the
contributing ranking values. Design security risk tables would also support informing the PPS, as
well as SSeBD and SeBD insights. SSeBD insights could be found in the impacts on safety function,
reliance on support systems, DID risk, and compromise risk columns, and may include increasing
SSC diversity in meeting the FSFs, reducing reliance on support systems through passive actuation
or function, increasing DID, or changing functionality or including interlocks to reduce the
compromise risk. The attack vector columns would capture at a high level the vulnerabilities of
each SSC system and would inform SeBD changes and what targets should be prioritized in the
design of the PPS.

Table 9.15. Example design security risk table.

Nuclear facility designers would transition from the security risk methodology to target set / vital
area identification methods and design of the PPS (SSCs and operational programs) as the design
matured and as the preparations for license submittals, construction, and operational readiness
necessitated.

In addition to the contents of this report, further support on ARs can be found through U.S. Nuclear
NEXUS: https://nuclear-nexus.anl.gov/nexus/. NEXUS is an online resource center that can be used
to engage with the National Nuclear Security Administration on the global deployment of U.S.
civilian nuclear technology. NEXUS subject areas include international nuclear security, export
control compliance, international nuclear safeguards, and proliferation resistance in nuclear
systems.
